{"url": "https://www.bbc.com/news/articles/c043n0kl71wo", "depth": 1, "published_time": null, "markdown": "[](https://www.bbc.com/)\n[](https://www.bbc.com/sport)\n[](https://www.bbc.com/health)\n[](https://www.bbc.com/weather)\n[](https://www.bbc.com/newsletters)\n# New Silent Hill game inspired by tiny fishing village in Fife\n![Screen Burn/Konami/Annapurna Video game graphic of a man wearing glasses looking at a sign which says \"Welcome to St Amelia\" with ferry directions on it. It is foggy and grey and a building stands behind the sign](https://ichef.bbci.co.uk/news/480/cpsprodpb/243e/live/2f158ff0-0b1f-11f1-b2d9-a9bd89759413.jpg.webp)Screen Burn/Konami/Annapurna\nSilent Hill: Townfall is set in the fictional village of St Amelia, which developers based on St Monans in Fife\nThe next release in one of the biggest video game franchises in the world was inspired by a small fishing village in Fife.\nSilent Hill: Townfall is set in a fictional place called St Amelia, which is based on the real village of St Monans in the East Neuk of Fife.\nThe new release was created by Screen Burn, a Glasgow developer with about 30 employees, who said it was their \"biggest and most ambitious game yet\".\nThe survival horror series has sold millions of units since its first game was published by Japanese multinational Konami in 1999. \nThe most recent game in the franchise, a spin-off called Silent Hill f, sold a million copies the day it was released in September 2025.\nWhen Townfall is released later this year, one million people could walk the virtual streets of St Monans - which has a real-life population of just over 1,100 people.\n![Getty Images St Monans is a bright fishing village with colourful houses by a harbour with boats bobbing in the water](https://ichef.bbci.co.uk/news/480/cpsprodpb/6e7e/live/9c3c66b0-0b1c-11f1-b2d9-a9bd89759413.jpg.webp)Getty Images\nSt Monans is a small village along the Fife Coastal Path in the East Neuk\n![Screen Burn/Konami/Annapurna Video game graphic of a residential street cloaked in fog with a 20mph sign on a lampost](https://ichef.bbci.co.uk/news/480/cpsprodpb/ee84/live/6101ddc0-0b1f-11f1-b2d9-a9bd89759413.jpg.webp)Screen Burn/Konami/Annapurna\nThe trailer depicts a deserted fishing village\nA trailer for the game reveals a fishing village shrouded in haar - a cold sea fog common along the east coast of Scotland.\nIt shows a harbour littered with lobster boxes and boats bobbing in the water, as well as colourful bunting along the dark streets of St Amelia.\nPlacards by a monument read \"let the ferries in\" and \"this town is ours\", however the place appears to be deserted.\n![Screen Burn/Konami/Annapurna Video game graphic of a smoky harbour with a life ring and stairs leading up to a higher platform](https://ichef.bbci.co.uk/news/480/cpsprodpb/33a1/live/ac34a8e0-0b1f-11f1-a204-b3f033b84443.jpg.webp)Screen Burn/Konami/Annapurna\nThe developers said they wanted the setting to feel isolated \nScreen Burn co-founder Jon McKellan said the team were keen to bring a new setting to the popular series.\nIn a recent transmission on the Silent Hill YouTube channel, he said they originally planned to keep the location vaguely in northern Europe.\nBut he added: \"There was a drive to create something that felt authentic to us and it kind of felt natural to bring it home.\"\nStories you may have missed\nMcKellan said he was wanted to use the location to help tell the story, and his team studied the village's features to bring it to life in the world of Silent Hill.\n\"It's the smell and the wind and the sound of the place, all of that together is what you're trying to recreate,\" he said.\n\"When I started to write the story about what this town was, what happened to this town, the isolation was a big factor. \n\"There's like an existential dread that comes with that. Silent Hill has always had a sense of not being able to leave the town, and face why you're there.\"\nHe said recreating the haar accurately and how it obscures things from the player was a key part of this.\n![Screen Burn/Konami/Annapurna Video game graphic of a narrow street in fog with bunting and a lamppost dimly lighting the alleyway](https://ichef.bbci.co.uk/news/480/cpsprodpb/fefe/live/76ba0430-0b1f-11f1-b2d9-a9bd89759413.jpg.webp)Screen Burn/Konami/Annapurna\nThe winding alleyways in Silent Hill: Townfall mimic similar features in St Monans\nArt director Paul Abbott said the misty environment in the game reminded him of early morning walks to school when he was growing up in a small fishing village.\n\"It's a beautifully epic, grey, drizzly environment and we're bringing that to the game,\" he said.\nHe said the team had painstakingly recreated recognisable elements of the town, such as the winding alleyways and the texture of the piers.\nMcKellan said it was a \"genuine honour\" to work with and contribute to the franchise.\n\"It's not an opportunity we ever thought we'd get, but it's one we knew we all wanted right away,\" he added.\nThe game will be released later in 2026.\n[Gaming](https://www.bbc.com/news/topics/c008ql15vdxt)\n[St Monance](https://www.bbc.com/news/topics/c0n168gw8dpt)\n[Scotland](https://www.bbc.com/news/scotland)\n* * *\nRelated\n## [John Wick game starring Keanu Reeves unveiled at PlayStation {"url": "https://www.bbc.com/news/articles/cn8z7exgg9vo", "depth": 1, "published_time": null, "markdown": "[](https://www.bbc.com/)\n[](https://www.bbc.com/sport)\n[](https://www.bbc.com/health)\n[](https://www.bbc.com/weather)\n[](https://www.bbc.com/newsletters)\n# Red eyes and black beaches: How one of the worst environmental disasters left its mark\n![Huw Evans Agency A striking photo from the air of the Sea Empress tanker, surrounded by tugboats and vast swathes of oil polluting the sea around it.](https://ichef.bbci.co.uk/news/480/cpsprodpb/f32a/live/79361d10-fe00-11f0-bb32-2b2b28af4780.jpg.webp)Huw Evans Agency\nMore than 70,000 tonnes of crude oil spilled out of the Sea Empress tanker after it ran aground near Milford Haven in Pembrokeshire in February 1996\nWhen the oil tanker Sea Empress ran aground 30 years ago, some of south Wales' best-known beaches turned black.\nThousands of seabirds were killed off a globally significant stretch of coastline for wildlife in Pembrokeshire, while salvage crews fought for almost a week to lift the stricken tanker off the rocks and tow her to safety.\nDescribed as \"a wake up call\" that led to changes in public attitude towards the environment, to this day there is anger and debate over how the disaster could have happened.\nExperts warn climate change increases the risk of major pollution events in future on busier and more hazardous seas.\n![Getty Images Oil coating a beach and rocks after the Sea Empress disaster in February 1996](https://ichef.bbci.co.uk/news/480/cpsprodpb/148e/live/8f8092b0-09d8-11f1-bfd9-a1813d3ca61e.jpg.webp)Getty Images\nThe oil slick affected an estimated 200km of the Welsh coastline\n\"It's all coming back to me 30 years later,\" said retired BBC reporter Hefin Wyn, looking out to sea at St Anne's Head, a remote spot at the entrance to Milford Haven waterway, one of the busiest ports in the UK.\nHe recalled \"the humming of the tanker\" against the lashing wind and rain and then the \"heart-breaking\" sight of \"thick, treacle-like oil\" on the sea.\n\"One could see the birds landing [on the water]\u2026 but they couldn't fly away,\" he said.\n\"It was a wake-up call - by now we are far more aware of the need to look after the environment than we were at the time.\" \n![Retired BBC Wales reporter Hefin Wyn at St Anne's Head, Pembrokeshire.  He has white hair and a white beard and is wearing a green, waterproof coat.  Behind him can be seen the entrance to the Milford Haven waterway.](https://ichef.bbci.co.uk/news/480/cpsprodpb/2e75/live/b6dc8430-05c4-11f1-b8f3-e5c6454cb301.jpg.webp)\nHefin Wyn, from Maenclochog in Pembrokeshire, reported on the disaster for the BBC's Welsh language news programmes\nIt was \"the single worst incident I've ever seen\", said conservationist and TV presenter Iolo Williams, who worked at the time for the RSPB in Wales.\nThe oil \"got into [the pores of] your skin, it got into your hair... and everybody went around with these bright red eyes\", he told [a new BBC documentary marking the 30th anniversary](https://www.bbc.com/mediacentre/proginfo/2026/07/black-waters-sea-empress-disaster).\nAbout 7,000 oil-coated birds were picked up along the coast but \"the amount killed would have been at least double that because a lot of them would have sunk offshore,\" he said.\n![Getty Images Rescue of an oiled duck following the sinking of the Sea Empress](https://ichef.bbci.co.uk/news/480/cpsprodpb/e618/live/664fb710-09ef-11f1-b95c-79d90fbdbf52.jpg.webp)Getty Images\nAbout 7,000 birds were collected covered in oil but less than half of them survived \nIn rockpools, starfish, limpets and other wildlife were smothered - while Pembrokeshire's fishing industry was brought to a standstill for more than 18 months.\nThe disaster happened in what is the UK's only coastal national park, with 35 Sites of special scientific interest (SSSIs), a marine nature reserve and islands home to internationally significant seabird populations. \n![Mirrorpix Swans coated in oil stand on newspaper in the back of a van ](https://ichef.bbci.co.uk/news/480/cpsprodpb/ff4d/live/c5881cf0-084a-11f1-ba49-bb79b430dd38.jpg.webp)Mirrorpix\nThe RSPCA ran a makeshift hospital from an old industrial unit in Milford Haven where birds were taken to be nursed and cleaned\n## What happened when the Sea Empress ran aground?\nThe 274m (900ft) long, Liberian-registered tanker Sea Empress was on its way to an oil refinery when it hit rocks at the entrance to Milford Haven waterway at 20:07 on 15 February 1996.\nThe initial response focused on trying to bring another vessel alongside to offload the Sea Empress' cargo of crude oil.\nBut gale force winds led to the crew and tugboats having to evacuate and the ship was left to ride out the storm for several hours.\nEyewitnesses described the tanker \"swinging around like a compass\" as waves crashed over the abandoned ship and black plumes of oil spurted out into the sea.\n![Black Waters: The Sea Empress Disaster A TV showing a news piece from 1996 illustrating where the Sea Empress had run aground.  ](https://ichef.bbci.co.uk/news/480/cpsprodpb/8e09/live/c369a320-0670-11f1-a360-89dc08e51674.jpg.webp)Black Waters: The Sea Empress Disaster\nAs shown in news footage from the time, the Sea Empress ran aground near Milford Haven and close to Skomer and Skokholm islands, havens for rare seabirds\nThere were repeated attempts in the days that followed to refloat the vessel.\nIt was finally towed to a jetty on 21 February, ending six days of dramatic scenes which had made headlines around the world.\nMore than 70,000 tonnes - or 32 Olympic-sized swimming pools worth - of oil had spilled into the sea, affecting an estimated 200km (124 miles) of coastline.\n![Getty Images The Liberian-registered, Russian-crewed oil tanker, run aground 15 February near an offshore wildlife refuge off the Welsh coast](https://ichef.bbci.co.uk/news/480/cpsprodpb/d864/live/e74241c0-05b3-11f1-8336-6d2201ebafb2.jpg.webp)Getty Images\nIt took six days for rescue teams to move the 147,000-ton Sea Empress off the rocks\n\"It was such a shock to the local community,\" said Gordon James, then director of Friends of the Earth in Wales.\n\"I remember going down to Tenby and seeing the beautiful North Beach covered in black and there were people there in tears.\n\"We set up a legal team and we interviewed a lot of people... and the evidence was damning.\n\"The tugboat men and the marine pilots, they'd issued warnings that safety was being compromised [at the port], there were cutbacks and they were saying more or less it's a disaster waiting to happen.\"\nAn inexperienced pilot had been tasked with taking the tanker into Milford Haven and \"then we learnt that the radar [at the port] hadn't been working properly for months\".\nMilford Haven Port Authority was eventually fined \u00a34m, which at the time was the biggest penalty ever issued for a pollution case in Britain.\nIt was later reduced on appeal to \u00a3750,000 to enable the port to make changes to improve safety.\n![Getty Images Cleanup operation along the Pembrokeshire coastline after 72,000 tonnes of crude oil was released into the sea ](https://ichef.bbci.co.uk/news/480/cpsprodpb/6dea/live/dfcf8f50-05b4-11f1-8336-6d2201ebafb2.jpg.webp)Getty Images\nAbout 1,000 people spent weeks working to remove oil from the beaches \nJames still feels angry at what he sees as mistakes made during the response to the incident.\nHe insists the tanker should not have been held in place before the weather turned.\nLocal pilots and tugboat crews had said they could take the vessel further out to sea or to a jetty to offload the oil but \"were ignored\", he claimed.\n\"The storm came in and they lost control.\"\n![Gordon James, ex director of Friends of the Earth Cymru sat at his desk looking through documents and pictures from the time of the Sea Empress disaster](https://ichef.bbci.co.uk/news/480/cpsprodpb/ce26/live/bb86c5d0-05d9-11f1-a351-bb43301b14c5.jpg.webp)\n\"Looking back over 30 years, I still feel anger that it happened,\" says Gordon James, then director of Friends of the Earth in Wales\n## 'Risk of explosion'\nJoe Small sees things differently.\nHe was senior operations manager for the the UK's Marine Pollution Control Unit and one of the first people to step on board the Sea Empress after it ran aground.\nIt became apparent upon inspecting the ship that oil had moved internally into tanks not designed to hold it, presenting \"a huge safety issue\" and \"risk of explosion\".\nThat limited their options, he said, adding that they wanted to make sure the ship was safe before bringing it into a populated area such as Milford Haven.\nThe salvage operation was \"a huge technical challenge\" and the fact it was managed within a week was \"pretty good going\", he claimed.\n![Getty Images The Sea Empress pictured listing to side, with tug boats on either side](https://ichef.bbci.co.uk/news/480/cpsprodpb/0dd8/live/67b698d0-05b7-11f1-8336-6d2201ebafb2.jpg.webp)Getty Images\nThe disaster still ranks in the top 20 worst oil spills worldwide\nTom Sawyer, the Port of Milford Haven's chief executive, called the Sea Empress disaster \"a defining moment\", saying it had \"exposed shortcomings in our industry and caused profound harm to a coastline whose natural beauty and biodiversity we all treasure\".\nThe port had \nThe disaster led to major changes in how the UK prepares for and responds to maritime incidents, while the shipping industry worldwide \"accelerated the adoption of double-hull tankers, mandatory emergency towing points and improved compensation frameworks for environmental damage,\" Sawyer said.\n![Mirrorpix A man and two children watch as the Sea Empress is towed to a jetty ](https://ichef.bbci.co.uk/news/480/cpsprodpb/e892/live/fc426940-09d9-11f1-bfd9-a1813d3ca61e.jpg.webp)Mirrorpix\nThe Sea Empress disaster changed how the UK prepares for and responds to maritime incidents\n## Could a major oil spill happen again?\n\"We're constantly looking for ways to improve, better prepare and make things safer,\" said Joe Small.\nFiercer and more frequent storms brought by climate change was a major challenge - as are busier and more hazardous seas, he warned.\nThe loss of oil refining capacity in the UK meant a greater need to import refined products like petrol, which comes in on smaller tankers.\n\"We're now having four or five vessels to carry the same volume [as the Sea Empress],\" he said.\n\"More ships, worse weather - no matter how safe our operations are, things go wrong\".\nGlobally tankers were facing \"a resurgence in piracy and other attacks\", while a newer phenomenon of so-called \"\n## 'The real price of oil'\nThe Sea Empress disaster could have been much worse.\nThe time of year it happened meant many key migratory birds had not arrived back to Pembrokeshire's cliffs and islands - and the wind direction blew some of the oil away from the coast.\nA massive effort by the community to clean up the beaches was seen as a huge success, allowing the county to recover far more quickly than had been feared.\nFirst Minister Eluned Morgan, then a member of the European Parliament for mid and west Wales, revealed she still had a pair of wellington boots \"with Sea Empress oil on them\" after helping out.\nIt had shown \"how important it is that we treat our seas with respect\", \n![Getty Images Volunteers clean Castle Beach in Tenby ](https://ichef.bbci.co.uk/news/480/cpsprodpb/a02a/live/9892d970-09d9-11f1-bfd9-a1813d3ca61e.jpg.webp)Getty Images\nThousands of volunteers came to help clean up Pembrokeshire's beaches after the spill\n\"One of the long-term things that changed was people's attitude,\" said Nick Ainger, the area's MP at the time.\n\"Those images they saw - that's the real price of oil. And I think it was the beginning of this substantial change in public attitude towards the environment: how precious it was, and how easily it can be damaged if we're not sensible and caring about it.\"\n\"I think about it at every anniversary,\" added Iolo Williams. \"Thirty years on it's important to tell the story so we learn from our mistakes.\"\n_Additional reporting by Clare Hutchinson_\nMore top stories\n[Wales](https://www.bbc.com/news/wales)\n[Milford Haven](https://www.bbc.com/news/topics/c1kr68g2w1zt)\n[Pollution](https://www.bbc.com/news/topics/c40rjmqdq4xt)\n[Environment](https://www.bbc.com/news/topics/cnx753jenyjt)\n* * *\nRelated\n## ['Bizarre' 24-metre shipwreck spotted off Welsh coast](https://www.bbc.com/news/articles/cx24n5174r2o)\n## [Doctor who called junior colleague 'bad girl' and squeezed her waist is struck off](https://www.bbc.com/news/articles/c5y5xzypxp4o)\n## [Enigmatic aristocrat was ahead of his time, says It's a Sin star](https://www.bbc.com/news/articles/cj981k4ee7no)\n* * *\nMore from the BBC\n[ 2 hrs ago ![Susannah Gill, who is wearing glasses and a green turtle-neck jumper, stands in her sewage-flooded garden](https://ichef.bbci.co.uk/news/480/cpsprodpb/3801/live/8c3d8110-0b56-11f1-9972-d3f265c101c6.jpg) Why is sewage spilling into homes and gardens? {"url": "https://www.bbc.com/news/articles/c6209vqv3nlo", "depth": 1, "published_time": null, "markdown": "[](https://www.bbc.com/)\n[](https://www.bbc.com/sport)\n[](https://www.bbc.com/health)\n[](https://www.bbc.com/weather)\n[](https://www.bbc.com/newsletters)\n# How the tide turned against the leader of South Africa's second-biggest party\n![AFP via Getty Images DA leader John Steenhuisen is seen looking ahead during an event](https://ichef.bbci.co.uk/news/480/cpsprodpb/1409/live/8edac430-08c4-11f1-aef6-c5b8e6ae55fa.jpg.webp)AFP via Getty Images\nServing in the South African government for the first time, the Democratic Alliance (DA) - perceived as primarily representing racial minorities - portrays itself as a squeaky clean party but has suffered a major blow after its leader John Steenhuisen became engulfed in several scandals.\nThis forced him to announce he will step down as party leader, opening the way for a potentially divisive contest between a black and white candidate to succeed him. \nRace remains a major faultline in South Africa. The ending, 32 years ago, of the legalised form of racism, apartheid, that came with the rise to power of Nelson Mandela's African National Congress (ANC) has not totally erased the divisions.\nThe ANC has remained in government ever since, with the DA joining it for the first time after the 2024 election failed to produce an outright winner. \nThe DA secured six ministerial posts in President Cyril Ramaphosa's cabinet, with Steenhuisen holding the agriculture portfolio.\nAs the former leader of the opposition, and now minister, Steenhuisen was caught between making the government work while trying to appease his supporters - and prove to them that he is delivering in government.\nBut the 49-year-old has faced intense criticism from farmers over his failure to contain the foot-and-mouth disease, which has devastated South Africa's livestock industry.\nThe executive chair of the Southern African Agri Initiative (SAAI), \n![Gallo via Getty Images South Africa's Agriculture Minister John Steenhuisen is seen here in a yellow coat in front of a dairy farm](https://ichef.bbci.co.uk/news/480/cpsprodpb/1e36/live/c2a02280-08ea-11f1-94dc-adc7436c6c75.jpg.webp)Gallo via Getty Images\nJohn Steenhuisen's handling of the foot-and-mouth disease outbreak has pitted him against farmers\nFor the DA, such criticism is a major blow as it has long positioned itself as being far more democratic and competent than Ramaphosa's ANC. But it has now come under fire from one of its key constituencies - South Africa's mostly white farmers - many of whom vote for the centre-right DA or the right-wing Freedom Front Plus. \nBut more damaging for Steenhuisen was the financial scandal that he became embroiled in - possibly the first time that this has happened to a DA leader. \nIn response, Steenhuisen said: \"My personal finances have nothing to do with the Daily Maverick unless you can demonstrate that illegal funds or the proceeds of corruption or abuse of office or undue benefits have flowed into or out of my accounts.\"\nBut the disclosure tarnished his reputation - and that of the party - as people questioned how a leader who could not manage his own finances could manage the nation's finances. \nThe scandal coincided with Steenhuisen getting Ramaphosa to sack Environment Minister Dion George, then the DA's federal finance chair.\nWhile no official reason was given, various reports pinned his removal to his stance against wildlife trafficking. \nWildlife trafficking, particularly poaching, is a significant problem in South Africa and George was praised by conservation groups for his role in championing reforms in the conservation and environmental sector, particularly by calling for an end to captive lion breeding.\nThe EMS Foundation, which advocates for wildlife rights, said George's removal was a \"political execution\" and accused Steenhuisen of walking \"straight into the arms of the wildlife-breeding sector\" when he assumed the agriculture portfolio. \nThe Society for the Prevention of Cruelty to Animals also raised concerns, particularly over the apparent close ties George's successor Willie Aucamp had with wildlife breeders and hunters.\nGeorge's detractors within the DA pushed back against this narrative, citing under-performance and bullying allegations as reasons for his removal, while dismissing concerns about Aucamp's appointment, saying it expects \"continued progress\" in conservation and anti-poaching efforts under his leadership.\nThe DA also announced that it planned to institute a disciplinary inquiry into allegations that George \"unjustifiably\" raised the salaries of staff in his ministerial office - something that further tarnished the party's reputation. \nGeorge denied the allegations and said he was the victim of a \"smear campaign\" aimed at covering up the actual reasons for his firing. \nHe then resigned from the party, but not before revealing that he had confiscated Steenhuisen's DA-issued credit card last year because \"the account could not be reconciled\", with spending on UberEats, car rentals and hotel accommodation. \nSteenhuisen denied the allegations, and a DA inquiry cleared him of any wrongdoing. \nIt found that expenditure on the card had been \"fully reconciled\", and that the \"limited number of personal expenses had been adequately explained and reimbursed\".\nBut possibly reading the political tea leaves, Steenhuisen later announced that he would step down as DA leader at its congress in April. \nHe appeared upbeat as he addressed the media, looking nothing like a man being forced out, as he reflected on his political career and achievements.\n![Gallo via Getty Images Mmusi Maimane and John Steenhuisen, then both members of the Democratic Alliance, shake hands in South Africa's parliament](https://ichef.bbci.co.uk/news/480/cpsprodpb/3556/live/03950d40-08ec-11f1-94dc-adc7436c6c75.jpg.webp)Gallo via Getty Images\nJohn Steenhuisen replaced Mmusi Maimane (L), the DA's first black leader, in 2019\nSteenhuisen's first foray into politics came in 1999 when he was appointed a councillor in his home city of Durban, before rising to become party leader 20 years later.\nHis ascent had not been without scandal. In 2010 he was forced to resign as the DA leader for the KwaZulu-Natal region after it emerged that the married Steenhuisen was having an affair with a colleague's wife.\nSteenhuisen managed to quickly bounce back from that and found his way to parliament just a year later.\nIn 2014, he became the DA's chief whip, working closely with the party's first black leader, Mmusi Maimane. The two formed a close bond that soured when Maimane resigned from the role in 2019. Steenhuisen replaced him.\nSince then, the pair has often traded insults.\nPolitical analyst Sandile Swana told the BBC that Steenhuisen should have never been the DA leader.\nSwana mentioned his 2010 affair, public fallout with George and his alleged difficulties in managing his finances as examples of his poor leadership.\nPolitical analyst Khanyi Magubane offered a different perspective, saying South Africans in general may view him as someone who was able to rise above party politics, but in the DA \"he will go down as a betrayer to many who wanted him to continue the position of a hardliner\" in the coalition government. \nElaborating on this, she said that Steenhuisen's good relationship with the ANC in government was the \"core issue\" that led him to announce that he would step down. \nMagubane said that when Steenhuisen and his party joined the coalition government, \"there was almost an expectation that he would continue with the mandate of fighting for DA policies\".\n\"But the exact opposite started to happen,\" Magubane said.\n![Gallo via Getty Images Geordin Hill-Lewis is seen at a campaign event in Cape Town](https://ichef.bbci.co.uk/news/480/cpsprodpb/ff8e/live/0dd99d00-08ee-11f1-94dc-adc7436c6c75.jpg.webp)Gallo via Getty Images\nCape Town mayor Geordin Hill-Lewis is seen as a favourite to replace Steenhuisen but may face competition\nThis echoed George's sentiments in his resignation from the party; the aggrieved veteran accused the DA, through Steenhuisen, of being \"captured\" by the ANC.\nThe election result forced the DA and ANC, considered long-standing arch-rivals, to put aside their differences and work together. \nThe pro-business DA had been a fierce critic of the ANC on many issues, including foreign policy - where the ANC is seen to be too close to countries such as Russia - and domestic policy, where it champions affirmative action.\nDespite numerous hurdles along the way, including the DA's refusal to support the government's proposed budget twice last year, both parties have so far managed to make the best of the uneasy union.\nThis was best exemplified when\nAfter Trump confronted the delegation with a video in support of discredited claims of a white genocide in South Africa, it was Steenhuisen who assured the US president that most white farmers wanted to stay in the country.\nCape Town mayor Geordin Hill-Lewis has been viewed as a favourite to replace Steenhuisen at the party's elective conference, though local media is reporting that he could be challenged by the DA leader in the economic heartland of Gauteng, Solly Msimanga.\nAnalyst Magubane doubts Msimanga stands a chance of winning.\n\"As the Gauteng leader he has a lot of support, but Hill-Lewis still has the backing of senior party members like Helen Zille.\"\nShe also reflected that, following Maimane's stint in the post, she does not think the \"DA has the appetite for a black leader at the moment\".\nThe DA has long been perceived as a party that promotes the interests of whites, Indians, and coloureds, as people of mixed race are known in South Africa.\nMaimane stepped into the leadership post in 2015 as the DA was trying to broaden its appeal.\nFour years later he quit after the party's vote declined by about 1.5 percentage points in the general election.\nExplaining his exit at the time, Maimane said the DA was \"not the vehicle best suited to take forward the vision of building\" a united South Africa.\nBreaking up the party's now white-dominated leadership will be hard, says Magubane, reflecting that the party needs \"to appease the conservative right made up of Afrikaners\".\n\"A black leader will not be supported by the conservatives in the party.\"\nMore BBC stories on South Africa:\n## [South Africa's political marriage of convenience avoids divorce - just](https://www.bbc.com/news/articles/crennp25n7go)\n## [South {"url": "https://www.bbc.com/news/articles/cdr2xl1e41eo", "depth": 1, "published_time": null, "markdown": "[](https://www.bbc.com/)\n[](https://www.bbc.com/sport)\n[](https://www.bbc.com/health)\n[](https://www.bbc.com/weather)\n[](https://www.bbc.com/newsletters)\n# Ancient bone may prove legendary war elephant crossing of Alps\n![Getty Images An illustration of an elephant with soldiers crossing a river](https://ichef.bbci.co.uk/news/480/cpsprodpb/e64c/live/1cbfed40-0b52-11f1-b472-df9c1c12cffb.jpg.webp)Getty Images\nHannibal's invasion of Europe has acquired almost mythical status\nAn elephant foot bone found by archaeologists digging in southern Spain may be evidence that a troop of war elephants stomped through ancient Europe.\nIt would be the first concrete proof of the legendary Carthaginian General Hannibal's troop of battle elephants, according to academics.\nDrawings of Hannibal's war against the Romans had long suggested that the beasts were used in fighting, but no hard evidence backed up the theories.\nNow the creatures' skeletal remains appear to have been found in an Iron Age dig near Cordoba.\n\"Beyond ivory, the discovery of elephant remains in European archaeological contexts is exceptionally rare,\" says the team of scientists in a paper published in Journal of Archaeological Science: Reports.\nOften considered one of the most successful commanders of classical times, Hannibal led his army from the powerful imperial city Carthage, in modern day Tunisia, into Europe as he battled to control the Mediterranean.\nIt is thought he took soldiers and animals from Carthage through Spain and France to invade Italy, crossing the Alps with 37 elephants in 218 BCE during the second of the so-called Punic Wars.\nThe remains found in Spain are presumed to be from an animal that died before reaching the Alps.\nThe archaeologists, led by Professor Rafael M. Mart\u00ednez S\u00e1nchez, found the elephant's bone beneath a collapsed wall on a site called Colina de los Quemados. \n![Mart\u00ednez S\u00e1nchez et al A diagram showing different elephant bones for comparison](https://ichef.bbci.co.uk/news/480/cpsprodpb/b26d/live/b8cac8f0-0b51-11f1-b5e2-dd58fc65f0f6.jpg.webp)Mart\u00ednez S\u00e1nchez et al\nThe top row is the Iron Age elephant bone found in Spain\nThey used carbon dating techniques to estimate the age of the 10cm cube-shaped bone. \nThe result led them to believe it is from the Second Punic War.\nThey also compared the bone of modern elephants and steppe mammoths to determine which animal it came from.\nThe team found artillery, coins and ceramics during the excavations in 2020, providing more clues that the place was the site of a battle.\n\"As non-native species and the largest living terrestrial animals, these imported beasts would have required transportation by ship,\" the academics said.\nThey say that it is very unlikely that dead animals were transported, and the bones are unattractive suggesting they were not decorative or used in craft.\nBut the scientists say it will be very challenging to work out which species of elephant the creature was.\n\"While [the bone] would not represent one of the mythical specimens Hannibal took across the Alps, it could potentially embody the first known relic \u2212 so sought after by European scholars of the Modern Age \u2212 of the animals used in the Punic Roman wars for the control of the Mediterranean,\" the scientists conclude in their paper.\n[Archaeology](https://www.bbc.com/news/topics/c1038wnxyy0t)\n[Science & Environment](https://www.bbc.com/news/science_and_environment)\n* * *\nRelated\n## [Cannon dug up during city centre {"url": "https://www.bbc.com/news/articles/cm289we7jzdo", "depth": 1, "published_time": null, "markdown": "[](https://www.bbc.com/)\n[](https://www.bbc.com/sport)\n[](https://www.bbc.com/health)\n[](https://www.bbc.com/weather)\n[](https://www.bbc.com/newsletters)\n# What is the dart frog toxin allegedly used to kill Alexei Navalny?\n![Getty Images An Anthony's poison arrow frog perched on a leaf. Its skin is red with white stripes](https://ichef.bbci.co.uk/news/480/cpsprodpb/7fc2/live/a4c47040-0a98-11f1-9640-7f85e4d0c189.jpg.webp)Getty Images\nEpibatidine, which can be secreted by Anthony's poison arrow frog onto its skin, was blamed for Alexei Navalny's death by five European nations\nRussian opposition leader Alexei Navalny was killed using a deadly toxin found in poison dart frogs in South America, the UK and some of its European allies have said.\nTraces of epibatidine were found in samples from Navalny's body and highly likely resulted in his death in a Siberian penal colony two years ago, the UK Foreign Office said.\nThe allies said only the Russian state had the \"means, motive and opportunity\" to deploy this lethal toxin.\nThe Kremlin dismissed the finding as \"an information campaign\", according to Tass news agency.\n## What is the toxin?\nEpibatidine is a natural neurotoxin isolated from the skin of the Ecuadorian poison dart frog, according to toxicology expert Jill Johnson.\nIt was \"200 times more potent\" than morphine, she told BBC Russian.\nEpibatidine can be found naturally in dart frogs in the wild in South America as well as being manufactured in a laboratory.\nDart frogs in captivity do not produce this toxin and it is not found naturally in Russia, the European allies said in their statement.\nSpecies known as Anthony's poison arrow frog and the Phantasmal poison frog are among those that secrete the toxin onto their skin.\nAlthough epibatidine has been investigated as a painkiller and for relief from painful inflammatory lung conditions, it is not used clinically because of its toxicity.\n## How does dart frog poison work?\nThis powerful chemical compound acts on nicotinic receptors in the nervous system, according to Johnson.\nBecause it overstimulates these nerve receptors, if dosed correctly, it can cause muscle twitching, paralysis, seizures, slow heart rate, respiratory failure and ultimately death, she explained.\nAlastair Hay, professor of environmental toxicology at the University of Leeds, told PA that its effects can result in breathing being blocked, and that \"any person poisoned dies from suffocation\".\nThe toxin being found in someone's blood \"suggests deliberate administration\", he added.\nEpibatidine toxicity can even be \"increased by co-administration of certain other drugs and these combinations have been researched\", Hay said.\n## How rare is the toxin?\nEpibatidine is extremely rare and found in only one geographic region and only in trace amounts, Johnson said.\nIt is understood the dart frog referred to by the UK Foreign Office and others was Anthony's poison arrow frog, a species endemic to Ecuador and Peru.\nThe frogs produce the chemical through eating the right foods to produce alkaloids - a type of organic compound - that make epibatidine and accumulate it in their skin. If the frog's diet changes, its epibatidine reserves will be depleted.\n\"Finding a wild frog in the right place, eating exactly the food needed to produce the right alkaloids, is almost impossible... almost,\" Johnson said.\n\"This is an incredibly rare method of human poisoning. The only other cases of epibatidine poisoning I know of were laboratory-based and non-fatal.\"\n## What has Russia said?\nEuropean laboratories confirmed Navalny died from the obscure poison, the allies said on Saturday.\nMoscow has previously claimed Navalny died of natural causes, though Navalny's widow Yulia Navalnaya has consistently argued her husband was \"murdered\" by poisoning.\nThe Russian embassy in London denied Moscow was involved in Navalny's death and described the announcement as \"feeble-mindedness of Western fabulists\" and \"necro-propaganda\".\nKremlin spokesperson Maria Zakharova was quoted by Russian state-run news agency Tass as saying: \"All the talks and statements are an information campaign aimed at distracting attention from the West's pressing problems.\"\nHamish de Bretton-Gordon, a former UK and Nato senior officer, who is also a chemical weapons expert, said people should take anything the Kremlin says, including about Navalny's death, \"with a pinch of salt\".\nHe told BBC Radio 4's Today programme that Nato countries \"have demonstrated that they have the evidence that Navalny was murdered by the state\".\nAt the time of his death, Navalny had been in jail for three years and had latterly been transferred to an Arctic penal colony.\nAccording to Russian accounts, the 47-year-old took a short walk, said he felt unwell, then collapsed and never regained consciousness.\n[Russia](https://www.bbc.com/news/topics/ce1qrvlegnyt)\n[Alexei Navalny](https://www.bbc.com/news/topics/cpzyrklr4l8t)\n[Vladimir She has brunette hair and wears an off-shoulder black dress](https://ichef.bbci.co.uk/news/480/cpsprodpb/0f98/live/2bf90bb0-08c4-11f1-aef6-c5b8e6ae55fa.jpg.webp)Getty Images\nRings of Power star Morfydd Clark will play John Lennon's first wife in The Beatles biopic\nThe Rings of Power actress Morfydd Clark is one of the latest stars to join the cast of Sir Sam Mendes' four-part Beatles biopic.\nClark, who grew up in Penarth, Vale of Glamorgan, will portray Cynthia Lennon, the first wife of John, who is played by Harris Dickinson in the films based on the famous bandmembers.\nStarring Dickinson, Paul Mescal, Barry Keoghan and Joseph Quinn, the four films will follow the lives and careers of the Liverpool band from each member's point of view.\nThe newly announced cast members also include Harry Lawtey, who recently played Welsh actor Richard Burton, and Bohemian Rhapsody star Lucy Boynton. \n![Amazon MGM Studios Clark in character](https://ichef.bbci.co.uk/news/480/cpsprodpb/da1b/live/a9ad5430-08ce-11f1-bed4-8368e91e11b3.jpg.webp)Amazon MGM Studios\nWelsh actress Clark is best known for her role in Lord of the Rings spin-off series, The Rings of Power\nJohn and Cynthia, who \nTheir son, singer-songwriter Julian Lennon, was born in 1963, but the pair split after Cynthia discovered her husband's relationship with Yoko Ono.\nClark is best known for her role in Amazon Prime's The Rings of Power, playing warrior Galadriel in the Lord of the Rings spin-off series - even \n![Warren Orchard/Icon Film Distribution Harry as Richard Burton. he looks back over his shoulder past the mirror. Perhaps this is a chance to understand them a little more deeply. \n\"But together, all four films will tell the story of the greatest band in history.\"\nSaoirse Ronan, Anna Sawai, Aimee Lou Wood and Mia McKenna-Bruce have all been announced to play the wives and partners of the four.\nThe four films are due for release on the same day in April 2028. \nMore top {"url": "https://www.bbc.com/news/articles/c86y3ndqlxwo", "depth": 1, "published_time": null, "markdown": "[](https://www.bbc.com/)\n[](https://www.bbc.com/sport)\n[](https://www.bbc.com/health)\n[](https://www.bbc.com/weather)\n[](https://www.bbc.com/newsletters)\n# China is hitting Japan where it hurts. Will PM Takaichi give in?\n![AFP via Getty Images Japan's Prime Minister Sanae Takaichi smiles at the cameras, wearing a blue blazer, a string of pearls and a black top.](https://ichef.bbci.co.uk/news/480/cpsprodpb/d21b/live/7b084720-0b08-11f1-b8d0-21adbc414b0b.jpg.webp)AFP via Getty Images\nAnalysts say Takaichi's huge win at the recent election has given her political capital to stand firm against China\nLast month at Tokyo's Ueno Zoo, after receiving thousands of tearful goodbyes from Japanese fans, Xiao Xiao and Lei Lei were put on a plane bound for China - the latest symbols of the deteriorating relationship between China and Japan.\nThe [two giant Chinese pandas had to return home](https://www.bbc.com/news/articles/c8d0l778dero) after Beijing announced it was taking them back, leaving Japan without any Chinese pandas for the first time in decades.\nSince Japan's Prime Minister Sanae Takaichi made comments that have plunged ties with China to their lowest level in years, Beijing has been piling on the pressure in a wide range of ways - sending warships, throttling rare earth exports, curbing Chinese tourism, cancelling concerts and even reclaiming its pandas. \nAs Takaichi begins a new term as PM [after winning a historically strong public mandate](https://www.bbc.com/news/articles/cx2y7d2z29xo) from a recent snap election, analysts warn that both sides will find it difficult to de-escalate - and the China-Japan relationship will not recover anytime soon.\n[The row began in November,](https://www.bbc.com/news/articles/crklvx2n7rzo) when Takaichi appeared to suggest that Japan would activate its self-defence force in the event of an attack on Taiwan.\nChina claims self-governed Taiwan as its own and has not ruled out the use of force to \"reunify\" with it one day. The island counts the US as its key ally, and Washington has pledged to help Taiwan defend itself.\nThe worry has long been that any attack on Taiwan could result in a direct military conflict between the US and China, and then widen to include other US allies in the region such as Japan and the Philippines.\nThe issue of Taiwan is an absolute red line for China, which reacts furiously to any comment perceived as \"outside interference\" and insists that it is a question of sovereignty that only China can decide for itself.\nAlmost immediately after Takaichi's remarks, Beijing responded with a flurry of condemnation and demanded a retraction. \nObservers have noted that Takaichi's comments were in line with the government's position and what other Japanese leaders have said in the past.\nBut the difference is that it was the first time a sitting Japanese prime minister had voiced such views.\nFor her part, Takaichi refused to apologise or retract her remarks, a stance that analysts say may likely be vindicated by the strong mandate she has won.\nBut she said she would be more careful about commenting on specific scenarios, and her government has sent senior diplomats to meet their Chinese counterparts.\nThis, however, has done little to allay Chinese anger. \n## 'Greyzone' pressure\nFaced with Takaichi's steady refusal to back down, China has piled on a steady stream of pressure. \nWhile spats have flared up between the two countries in recent decades, fueled by historical animosity, this time it feels different, say analysts.\nChina has expanded its pressure on a much \"wider range of fronts\", noted Robert Ward, the Japan chair for the think tank International Institute of Strategic Studies.\nIt is a diffuse, low-level pressure that is similar to the [\"greyzone warfare\" it conducts on Taiwan,](https://www.bbc.com/news/world-asia-66851118) he said, where it's aimed at \"wearing down [the opponent] to normalise things that actually are not normal\".\nDiplomatically, it has lodged complaints with the United Nations and postponed a trilateral summit with Japan and South Korea. \nChina has also tried to pull other parties into the fray and has called on the UK and France to side with it, while urging its allies Russia and North Korea to denounce Japan. \nOver the weekend, Chinese foreign minister Wang Yi invoked Japan's World War Two history of aggression while addressing Western leaders at the Munich Security Conference, and called Takaichi's remarks a \"very dangerous development\".\nOn the military front, Japan has claimed that China has sent drones, sailed warships past their islands and its fighter jets have \"locked radars\" on their planes. Japanese and Chinese coast guard ships have confronted one another near the disputed Senkaku/Diaoyu islands, while last week Japanese authorities [seized a Chinese fishing vessel](https://www.bbc.com/news/articles/c2e4791re38o).\nBut it is clear that China also wants to hit Japan where it hurts - its economy.\nBeijing has imposed restrictions on exports to Japan for dual-use technologies including rare earth elements and critical minerals, in what has been seen as a form of economic coercion.\nIt has also warned Chinese citizens to avoid Japan for their studies and holidays, and cancelled flight on 49 routes to Japan, leading to a drop in tourists and [a slide in some stocks.](https://www.bbc.com/news/articles/c5y9n25r0qyo) Chinese nationals make up a quarter of all foreign tourists to Japan, according to official figures.\nEven entertainment and culture is not off the hook.\nJapanese music events have been cancelled in China, including one where [a singer was rushed off stage mid-performance](https://www.bbc.com/news/articles/c997n2zlegzo), while film distributors have [postponed the release of several Japanese movies.](https://www.bbc.com/news/articles/c93d08jg383o)\nOne of Japan's most famous cultural exports, Pokemon, was also criticised over an event that was due to be held at the Yasukuni Shrine. The temple honours Japan's war dead including some that China considers as war criminals. The event was eventually cancelled.\nAnd on the social media front, Chinese online nationalists have launched attacks on Takaichi, including sharing AI-generated videos showing pop culture figure Ultraman and anime character Detective Conan fighting the prime minister.\n![Getty Images Giant panda Xiao Xiao walks in his enclosure while two women look on](https://ichef.bbci.co.uk/news/480/cpsprodpb/2021/live/ac5ba5c0-0b07-11f1-90ed-df71cb736179.jpg.webp)Getty Images\nXiao Xiao, seen here on his final day in Ueno Zoo, was sent back to China along with his sister Lei Lei\nBut overall, China has taken actions that have been less provocative compared to previous conflicts with Japan, noted Bonny Lin and Kristi Govella with the think tank Center for Strategic and International Studies (CSIS).\n\"Thus far, its economic and military responses have been relatively limited compared to the past, but there is ample room for further escalation,\" they noted in a recent analysis.\nChina may also be holding back on going too hard on Japan as it is currently \"actively positioning itself as the guardian of the post World War Two order\" and wants to be seen as a responsible power compared to the US, added Ward.\n## A 'tango that will continue'\nObservers agree that if or when tensions calm down, they will likely settle at a higher level than before.\nBoth sides are less likely to de-escalate this time round, noted Lin and Govella in their analysis. China is a much stronger power now, and \"Taiwan is the core of Chinese core interests, which means that Beijing is more likely to take a hardline position than in past episodes\".\n\"Beijing is also deeply suspicious of Takaichi and are likely to view her attempts to de-escalate without explicitly retracting her comments as hypocritical or, even worse, strategically deceptive,\" they added.\nMeanwhile, Japan has a greater appetite to stand firm especially with Takaichi's huge electoral win, which \"she will take this as vindication for her stance on China\", noted Ward.\nGovella told the BBC that Takaichi would likely use her win as \"political capital\" to advance defence and economic policies that will strengthen Japan's position.\nTakaichi has pledged to raise Japan's defence-related spending to 2% of the GDP two years ahead of schedule, complete a revision of key security strategies by the end of this year, and launch an economic stimulus package soon.\nIn turn, China \"sees that Takaichi is a pretty strong leader and the pressure campaign could only make her stronger domestically, [so] they may not intensify their pressure much,\" said Kiyoteru Tsutsui, a Japan expert and director of the Shorenstein Asia-Pacific Research Center at Stanford University.\n\"So this tango will likely continue on for a while.\"\nThe wildcard could be that US President Donald Trump has so far pledged strong support for Takaichi, issuing an unusual endorsement for her in the run-up to the snap election.\nBut many expect US-China relations to warm further this year, Tsutsui noted, with several scheduled meetings between Trump and Chinese President Xi Jinping including the US president's state visit to Beijing in April.\nAnd compared to previous incidents, the US response to the latest spat \"has been muted so far, which may embolden China\", said Lin and Govella.\n\"The Japanese are terrified there's going to be some grand bargain between Xi and Trump,\" said Ward.\nOver the weekend, the US and Japan affirmed their ties on the sidelines of the Munich Security Conference in a meeting between US Secretary of State Marco Rubio and his Japanese counterpart Toshimitsu Motegi.\nTakaichi is also due to meet Trump again in March when she visits Washington DC, ahead of his trip to China.\nAs China keeps piling on the pressure, Tokyo will likely \"double down\" on shouldering more of the defence burden it shares with the US, said Ward, and \"really work more closely with them to make sure that the US doesn't sort of drift off and lose interest in the region\".\n## [A Japanese boy was killed in China. Was cyber-nationalism to blame?](https://www.bbc.com/news/articles/cly6xpz9p1no)\n[China](https://www.bbc.com/news/world/asia/china)\n[Japan](https://www.bbc.com/news/topics/cjnwl8q4g7nt)\n* * *\nRelated\n## [ByteDance to curb AI video app after Disney legal threat](https://www.bbc.com/news/articles/c93wq6xqgy1o)\n## [China confirms visa-free travel for UK and Canadian nationals](https://www.bbc.com/news/articles/c875d3d3x34o)\n## [Is this a 'very Chinese time in your life'? {"url": "https://www.bbc.com/news/articles/c4gjx1j1nkjo", "depth": 1, "published_time": null, "markdown": "[](https://www.bbc.com/)\n[](https://www.bbc.com/sport)\n[](https://www.bbc.com/health)\n[](https://www.bbc.com/weather)\n[](https://www.bbc.com/newsletters)\n# Trump eyes Venezuela visit \u2013 but obstacles to his oil plan remain\n![AFP via Getty Images A man walks past a mural depicting an oil pumpjack, or 'nodding donkey' on a Venezuelan flag in the country's capital Caracas](https://ichef.bbci.co.uk/news/480/cpsprodpb/a1d6/live/398af810-065d-11f1-ad0b-39fef3bf1fc8.jpg.webp)AFP via Getty Images\nVenezuela has the world's largest oil reserves, but the industry has been starved of investment\nAfter US President Donald Trump oversaw the seizure of his Venezuelan counterpart Nicol\u00e1s Maduro last month, he vowed to tap the country's oil reserves \u2013 the world's largest.\nTrump now says that he \nHis comments, made last Friday, came after US Energy Secretary Chris Wright completed a \nWright's visit came shortly after Venezuela's National Assembly passed a law to allow both private and foreign investment in its oil industry, following two decades of tight state control.\nIn Trump's eyes it is a big business opportunity for the US oil sector. \"We're going to be extracting numbers in terms of oil like few people have seen,\" he said at a news conference in mid-January, after a meeting with energy bosses at the White House.\nBut for the US oil firms that Trump wants to invest heavily in Venezuela, the question is a simple one - do the numbers add up?\nWilliam Jackson, chief emerging markets economist at Capital Economics, says the US president's aim is to \"revive Venezuela's oil sector and use that energy to increase supply and reduce costs to the consumer, possibly providing a source of revenue for a more friendly Venezuelan government to rebuild the economy after years of mismanagement\".\nFor US energy companies, however, there are huge practical difficulties to be overcome. Venezuela's state-owned oil company, PDVSA, is a shadow of its former self.\nThe governments of Maduro and his predecessor, Hugo Ch\u00e1vez milked the firm for all it was worth, and used the money to finance social spending on housing, healthcare and transport.\nBut they failed to invest in maintaining oil production levels, which have plummeted in recent years \u2013 partly, but not solely, because of US sanctions, which could now be revised.\n\"In Venezuela, you're dealing with equipment that's been degraded by many years of neglect,\" says Jackson. \"Ten to 15 years ago, Venezuela was producing 1.5 million barrels a day more than it does today.\"\nMonica de Bolle, senior fellow at the Peterson Institute for International Economics, agrees that PDVSA is in a parlous state.\n\"A lot of things have to be scrapped completely and rebuilt from the ground up,\" she tells the BBC. \"In fact, if political constraints did not matter, the best thing to do would be to scrap PDVSA, but that isn't going to happen.\n\"It's a big nationalist symbol, it's attached to sovereignty. Would the Venezuelans be willing to do whatever the US says and roll over? I don't think so.\"\n![AFP via Getty Images Venezuelan oil facilities out at sea](https://ichef.bbci.co.uk/news/480/cpsprodpb/66ec/live/af02af70-065d-11f1-ad0b-39fef3bf1fc8.jpg.webp)AFP via Getty Images\nVenezuela's oil facilities have been allowed to become rundown\nTrump has asked US oil firms to spend at least $100bn (\u00a375bn) on restoring Venezuela's battered infrastructure \u2013 an absolute necessity before his plan to ramp up sales can be realised.\nOfficially, Venezuela has 300 billion barrels of oil reserves \u2013 yet in 2023, it exported just 211.6 million barrels of oil, worth about $4bn. Compare that to second-placed Saudi Arabia, which has 267 billion barrels of reserves, but had exports worth $181bn in the same time period.\nSo on paper at least, there is room for improvement. However, Jackson says there are doubts over the true size of Venezuela's oil reserves.\nDuring the Ch\u00e1vez presidency, Venezuela reclassified its reserves. Previously, there were thought to be just 80 billion barrels of extractable oil, but by 2011, its reported figure had nearly quadrupled. That statistical change was made possible by high oil prices at the time, which allowed previously unviable projects to look feasible.\n\"There was a big step \u2013 jump \u2013 that people have questioned,\" Jackson says. \"But now the world is awash with oil and it's not clear that the same calculations still apply.\"\nWhen Ch\u00e1vez became Venezuela's president in 1999, oil prices were climbing. In the early 2010s, a barrel would often fetch about $100, providing the government with plenty of money to pour into social programmes. But with current prices around the $65 mark, the country looks less like a reasonable investment.\nVenezuela's oil is also of poorer quality than its Saudi equivalent. Its sour, heavy crude is difficult to extract and refine, while its high sulphur content makes it corrosive to pipelines. A resurgence of Venezuela's industry could pose problems for Canada, which produces similarly viscous oil and exports much of it to the US, but analysts reckon the risk is minor.\nAccording to research by Capital Economics, Canadian oil should remain competitively priced, even if Venezuelan production increases.\nIn the meantime, Venezuela's economic crisis has led to the exodus of nearly eight million people who have fled in search of a better life.\nThis includes expertise vital to keeping the oil pumps working: with the skilled engineers formerly on the PDVSA payroll now plying their trade elsewhere, the system limps on with a skeleton staff.\nThomas Watters, managing director and sector lead for oil and gas at research firm S&P Global Ratings, says US firms have the ability to repair Venezuela's infrastructure, but it has to make economic sense.\n\"At the end of the day, oil and gas companies have to deliver value to shareholders,\" he says. \"They have very good managers. You can build anything, as long as you can pay for it.\n\"But you need an oil price that makes that worthwhile. Unless you can generate sufficient money to justify that, it's very difficult to see the industry coming back.\"\n![Monica de Bolle Monica de Bolle, senior fellow at the Peterson Institute for International Economics, smiles at the camera](https://ichef.bbci.co.uk/news/480/cpsprodpb/eee9/live/20efe080-065e-11f1-ad0b-39fef3bf1fc8.jpg.webp)Monica de Bolle\nMonica de Bolle says that Venezuela's state oil firm needs to be rebuilt\nBesides, US oil firms have been bitten by Venezuela once before. In 2007, major US companies including ExxonMobil and ConocoPhillips had their assets seized when they refused to allow PDVSA to take majority control.\nThey went to the international courts and were awarded huge sums in damages \u2013 $8.3bn in the case of ConocoPhillips \u2013 which have never been paid.\nGiven that the current Venezuelan regime continues largely intact, with former Vice-President Delcy Rodr\u00edguez as interim leader, it will take a lot to dispel fears of renewed expropriation.\nMoreover, US Energy Secretary Chris Wright has said the Trump administration has no plans to offer security guarantees to oil companies in Venezuela \u2013 a worrying omission in a country where state-sanctioned paramilitary groups known as \"colectivos\" often operate as criminal gangs.\nWithout bigger government incentives, oil firms will be reluctant to take what could be an expensive plunge. Small wonder, then, that ExxonMobil boss Darren Woods has called Venezuela \"uninvestable\" in its current state.\nTellingly, Trump did not come back with an offer of sweeteners to promote investment. Instead, he threatened to block ExxonMobil investment in Venezuela.\nThe policy is \"all stick, no carrot\", says de Bolle of the Peterson Institute. \"And it doesn't seem like they understand that they do need carrots.\"\n![AFP via Getty Images Venezuelan policemen stand in front of a protest in Caracas over low wages](https://ichef.bbci.co.uk/news/480/cpsprodpb/f9a1/live/e9d101b0-065d-11f1-ad0b-39fef3bf1fc8.jpg.webp)AFP via Getty Images\nUS oil firms are also said to be concerned about the ongoing security situation in Venezuela\nIn de Bolle's view, the Trump administration has an \"imperialist vision\" of Latin America that leads it to see the region's resources as its property. For her, private oil firms' aversion to Venezuela is a welcome barrier to that kind of resource grab.\n\"This is a time when you think, 'Thank God the US doesn't have a state-owned oil company,'\" she says. \"They need the private sector, but for the moment, the private sector isn't budging. And what company in their right mind is going to put money into Venezuela?\"\nBut if Venezuela's oil output does eventually surge, could it bring global oil prices down? Analysts refuse to be drawn.\n\"It depends on the scale at which it happens,\" says Jackson of Capital Economics.\n\"The situation is very fluid, very opaque, and there's a big geopolitical angle. We're in the early stages where Venezuelan production is concerned.\"\nRead more global business stories\n[US economy](https://www.bbc.com/news/topics/c1038wnxypvt)\n[World of {"url": "https://www.bbc.com/news/articles/cg7zxgxdggjo", "depth": 1, "published_time": null, "markdown": "[](https://www.bbc.com/)\n[](https://www.bbc.com/sport)\n[](https://www.bbc.com/health)\n[](https://www.bbc.com/weather)\n[](https://www.bbc.com/newsletters)\n# Dating apps could be in trouble \u2013 here's what might take their place\n![BBC A treated image of two hands holding phones in front of a slightly pixilated love heart](https://ichef.bbci.co.uk/news/480/cpsprodpb/c752/live/e48825d0-eab6-11ef-a819-277e390a7a08.png.webp)BBC\n**A year into their relationship, Jess and Nate got engaged next to the sea. \"It was a golden, sandy beach \u2013 empty and secluded,\" says Jess, 26. \"It was just us two there, so it was really intimate.\"**\nExcept that the couple were actually hundreds of miles apart \u2013 and they were role-playing their engagement in the video game \nNate, 27, was living just outside London \u2013 and Jess was in Wales. After meeting briefly at an esports event in Germany in March 2023, the pair developed a long-distance relationship, playing the game together \"from the moment we woke up to the moment we went to bed\", says Nate.\nThe couple still play the game daily, even though they've been living together in Manchester since March 2024. And they know other couples who have found their partners through video games: \"It's a different way of meeting someone,\" says Jess. \"You both have such a strong mutual love for something already, it's easier to fall in love.\"\nNate agrees. \"I was able to build a lot more of a connection with people I meet in gaming than I ever was able to in a dating app.\"\n![A selfie of Nate and Jess on the left, and on the right, a screenshot from World of Warcraft showing Nate proposing with the words, \"Will you marry me?\" ](https://ichef.bbci.co.uk/news/480/cpsprodpb/3d5d/live/9b6ace30-eaab-11ef-a319-fb4e7360c4ec.png.webp)\nNate and Jess (pictured, alongside their virtual engagement), found love online - but not on a dating app\nNate and Jess are not alone. According to some experts, people of their generation are moving away from dating apps and finding love on platforms that were not specifically designed for romance.\nAnd hanging out somewhere online that's instead focused on a shared interest or hobby could allow people to find a partner in a lower-stakes, less pressurised setting than marketing themselves to a gallery of strangers. For some digital-native Gen Zs, it seems, simply doing the things they enjoy can be an alternative to the tyranny of the swipe.\n## Internet dating at 30 - a turning point?\nSince it first appeared with the launch of match.com 30 years ago, online dating has fundamentally altered our relationships. Around 10% of heterosexual people and 24% of LGBT people have met their long-term partner online, \nBut evidence suggests that \nAccording to a 2023 Axios study of US college students and other Gen Zers, 79% said they were forgoing regular dating app usage. And in its 2024 Online Nation report, Ofcom said: \"Some analysts speculate that for younger people, particularly Gen Z, the novelty of dating apps is wearing off.\" In a January 2024 letter to shareholders, Match Group Inc - which owns Tinder and Hinge - acknowledged younger people were seeking \"a lower pressure, more authentic way to find connections\".\n\"The idea of using a shared interest to meet someone isn't new, but it's been reinvented in this particular moment in time \u2013 it signals a desire of Gen Z,\" says Carolina Bandinelli, an associate professor at Warwick University whose research focuses on the digital technologies of romance.\n![Getty Images Joggers running in a park](https://ichef.bbci.co.uk/news/480/cpsprodpb/d3c1/live/82bcf330-ea02-11ef-a819-277e390a7a08.png.webp)Getty Images\nMany younger people are exploring alternatives to dating apps, from gaming to running clubs and other social activities\nAccording to Danait Tesfay, 26, a marketing assistant from London, younger people are \nAt the same time that membership of some dating apps appears to be in decline, platforms based around common interests are attracting more users. For instance, the fitness app Strava now has 135m users \u2013 and its monthly active users grew by 20% last year, according to the company. Other so-called \"affinity-based\" sites have seen similar growth: Letterboxd, where film fans can share reviews, says its community grew by 50% last year.\n## Rise of the hobby apps\nAnd just as in the pre-internet age, when couples might have met at a sports club or the cinema, now singletons are able to find each other in their online equivalents.\n\"People have always bonded over shared interests, but it's been given a digital spin with these online communities,\" says Luke Brunning, co-director of the Centre for Love, Sex, and Relationships (CLSR) at the University of Leeds.\n\"It's increasingly difficult to distinguish between behaviour that's on a dating app and dating behaviour on another platform.\"\n\"[Online] fitness communities are becoming big places to find partners,\" says Nichi Hodgson, the author of The Curious History of Dating. She says a friend of hers met his partner that way, and they're now living together.\nThe same appears to apply to Letterboxd, too. With users including Chappell Roan and Charli XCX, it's a popular platform for younger people - two-thirds of members in a survey of 5,000 were under 34.\nThe company says it's aware of several couples meeting through the app, including one who bonded over a shared love of David Fincher's opinion-dividing 2020 drama Mank. \"It could be that seeing other people's film tastes reveals an interesting aspect of themselves,\" says Letterboxd co-founder Matthew Buchanan.\n## Why the shift?\nSo what might be driving this? While dating apps initially appeared to offer \"the illusion of choice\", and a transparent, efficient way to meet partners, the reality for many has often proven to be different. The Pew Research Center \nThe recent decline in user numbers might also be a response to the way some apps are structured \u2013 in particular, the swipe feature for selecting potential partners, launched by Tinder in 2013 and widely copied.\nIts creator, Jonathan Badeen, was partly inspired by studying the 1940s experiments of psychologist BF Skinner, who conditioned hungry pigeons to believe that food delivered randomly into a tray was prompted by their movements.\n![Getty Images A psychological experiment with pigeons conducted by BF Skinner](https://ichef.bbci.co.uk/news/480/cpsprodpb/6729/live/9c63e460-ea20-11ef-a819-277e390a7a08.png.webp)Getty Images\nTinder's swipe mechanism was partly inspired by Harvard Professor BF Skinner's psychological experiments with pigeons in order to understand the brain's reward system\nEventually, the swipe mechanism faced a backlash. \"Ten years ago, people were enthusiastic and would talk quite openly about what apps they were on,\" says Ms Hodgson. \"Now the Tinder model is dead with many young people \u2013 they don't want to swipe any more.\"\nAccording to Mr Brunning, the gameifying interface of many dating apps is a turn-off. \"Intimacy is made simple for you, it's made fun in the short term, but the more you play, the more you feel kind of icky.\"\nThe pandemic may have had an impact, too, says Prof Brian Heaphy at the University of Manchester, who has studied dating-app use in and after the lockdowns: \"During Covid, dating apps themselves became more like social media \u2013 because people couldn't meet up, they were looking for different things.\"\nAlthough that didn't last after the pandemic, it \"gave people a sense that it could be different from just swiping and getting no responses \u2013 all the negatives of dating-app culture,\" says Prof Heaphy.\nAnd in that context, the fact that video games or online communities like Strava or Letterboxd aren't designed for dating can be appealing. By attracting users for a broader range of reasons, there's less pressure on each interaction.\n\"Those apps aren't offering a commercialised form of romance, so they can seem more authentic,\" says Prof Heaphy.\n![The World of Warcraft characters of PurplePixel and Wochi](https://ichef.bbci.co.uk/news/480/cpsprodpb/7eb8/live/b471b640-ea02-11ef-a319-fb4e7360c4ec.png.webp)\nThe humans behind Wochi and PurplePixel (pictured) met while playing World of Warcraft, though they say finding a partner wasn't their original intention\nIt's a type of connection free from the burden of expectation. A different couple who met on World of Warcraft \u2013 and go by the names Wochi and PurplePixel \u2013 weren't looking for love. \"I definitely didn't go into an online game trying to find a partner,\" says Wochi.\nBut although initially in opposing teams, or guilds, their characters started a conversation. \"We spent all night talking until the early hours of the morning, and by the end of the night, I'd actually left my guild and joined his guild,\" says PurplePixel. Within three years, Wochi had quit his job and moved to the UK from Italy to be with her.\nAccording to Ms Hodgson, \"While some dating apps can bring out the worst behaviours, these other online spaces can do the opposite, because people are sharing something they enjoy.\"\nBecause of these structural elements, she doesn't think the recent decline in numbers is temporary. \"It's going to keep happening until dating apps figure out how to put the human aspect back.\"\n## New kinds of dating app\nThe dating apps aren't giving up without a fight, however. Hinge is still \"setting up a date every two seconds\", according to a spokesperson; Tinder says a relationship starts every three seconds on its platform and that almost 60% of its users are aged 18-30. In fact, the apps appear to be embracing the shift to shared-interest platforms, launching niche alternatives including ones based around fitness, veganism, dog-ownership or even facial hair.\nThey're also evolving to encourage different kinds of interaction. On Breeze, users who agree to be set up on a date aren't allowed to message each other before they meet; and Jigsaw hides people's faces, only removing pieces to reveal the full photo after a certain amount of interaction.\nIt means that it's \nAnd there's a downside to people going to more general-interest apps looking for love \u2013 people might not want to be hit on when they just want to talk about books. Dating apps, at least, are clear about what their purpose is.\n## What might the future look like?\nIn an increasingly online world, the solution to improving relationships might not simply be to go offline. Instead, apps that can offer an experience which more closely mirrors the best of IRL interactions, while tapping into the possibilities of digital ones, might also show a way forward.\nWith the imminent integration of AI into dating apps, we are \"right on the cusp of something new\", says Mr Brunning. \"It's interesting to see if we'll end up with specific apps just for dating, or will we end up with something a bit more fluid?\"\nHe points to platforms in China that are more multi-purpose. \"People use them for chat, for community, and conduct business on them \u2013 they can also be dating platforms, but they're often not exclusively for that.\"\nIn the meantime, the interactions possible in less mediated communities like World of Warcraft could offer more of a chance to connect than conversations initiated by a swipe.\nJess and Nate's in-game engagement on the beach might not have been real, but the couple are hoping to change that soon. \"It's a matter of when, really. There are a few things we need to tick off the checklist, and then she'll be getting her ring,\" says Nate. And there'll still be a gaming element.\n\"You can role-play getting married,\" says Jess. \"So it could be funny to get all our friends together at some point in the World of Warcraft cathedral, and we could have a marriage ceremony.\"\n_Additional reporting by Florence Freeman_\n_Top image credit: Getty_\nMore from InDepth\n_is the home on the website and app for the best analysis, with fresh perspectives that challenge assumptions and deep reporting on the biggest issues of the day. {"url": "https://www.bbc.com/news/articles/ceqvjgrvpn3o", "depth": 1, "published_time": null, "markdown": "[](https://www.bbc.com/)\n[](https://www.bbc.com/sport)\n[](https://www.bbc.com/health)\n[](https://www.bbc.com/weather)\n[](https://www.bbc.com/newsletters)\n# Long queues and confusion mar first day of India's landmark AI summit\n![Getty Images International and domestic delegates stand in a long, winding queue at the India-AI Impact Summit 2026 on 16 February 2026 in New Delhi](https://ichef.bbci.co.uk/news/480/cpsprodpb/ea0b/live/e686fbd0-0bb8-11f1-9f3e-e50369986a84.jpg.webp)Getty Images\nThe event is being promoted as the first major international AI meeting hosted in the Global South\nIndia's AI Impact Summit, billed as a landmark gathering of global leaders and technology executives, was overshadowed by logistical chaos on its opening day in Delhi on Monday.\nParticipants complained about long queues, overcrowding and confusion at the venue, saying they had to wait for hours. Some also reported limited access to food and water and said their products were stolen from their stall. \nThe five-day summit, inaugurated by PM Narendra Modi, is being promoted as the first major international AI meeting hosted in the Global South. \nMore than 100 countries are taking part, with technology leaders including Sam Altman of OpenAI and Sundar Pichai of Alphabet Inc expected to attend.\n![EPA/Shutterstock Visitors seen thronging the AI Impact Summit 2026 at Bharat Mandapam in New Delhi as posters of the event line the venue on 16 February 2026](https://ichef.bbci.co.uk/news/480/cpsprodpb/a4f3/live/6bc52c70-0bbb-11f1-ab66-0b9a69be6bdd.jpg.webp)EPA/Shutterstock\nDelegates at the event complained of long queues and crowd mismanagement\nSpeaking at the inauguration, Modi said the summit showcased \"the extraordinary potential of AI, Indian talent and innovation\", adding that India aimed to shape solutions \"not just for India but for the world\".\nIndia's Information Technology Minister Ashwini Vaishnaw said the summit intended to look at both the benefits and harms of AI. \nIn the next few days, \"we are basically looking at and measuring what [AI's] impact on human society is going to be\", he said.\nBut the opening day was marred by complaints of poor crowd management at the summit venue Bharat Mandapam. By Monday afternoon, social media was flooded with complaints from founders, exhibitors and delegates who said security sweeps and last-minute closures left them stranded outside exhibition halls.\nMaitreya Wagh, co-founder of voice AI start-up Bolna, wrote on X that he was unable to access his company's booth after gates were closed. Punit Jain, founder of tech platform Reskill, described \"7 AM queues\" followed by hours of waiting and a \"full evacuation\" before the prime minister's arrival.\n  * [Why India's AI summit is key to its future in tech](https://www.bbc.com/news/videos/cgmlyrk28r0o)\n  * [The tech bros might show more humility in Delhi \u2013 but will they make AI any safer?](https://www.bbc.com/news/articles/cr5l6gnen72o)\n\n\nReuters reported that some speakers were still awaiting confirmation of their session timings, adding to concerns over mismanagement.\n![Getty Images Prime Minister Narendra Modi seen wearing a white kurta and beige vest flanked by three men in suits at the India AI Impact Summit 2026. He is standing before a map of India shown under a glass case.](https://ichef.bbci.co.uk/news/480/cpsprodpb/4b08/live/752b7c10-0bbb-11f1-ab66-0b9a69be6bdd.jpg.webp)Getty Images\nPrime Minister Narendra Modi inaugurated the summit on Monday\nDhananjay Yadav, founder of wearable AI start-up NeoSapiens, alleged that products from his company's stall were stolen at the venue.
