{"url": "https://www.bbc.com/news/articles/c9dne166pwlo", "depth": 1, "published_time": null, "markdown": "[](https://www.bbc.com/)\n[](https://www.bbc.com/sport)\n[](https://www.bbc.com/health)\n[](https://www.bbc.com/weather)\n[](https://www.bbc.com/newsletters)\n# 'A sad day' - Canada rocked by curling cheating row at Winter Games\nWatch: 'Integrity in sport is important' - Canadians react to Olympic curling cheating row\nA scandal at the Winter Olympics has left the Canadian curling teams on the defensive and Canadians reeling over the crack in their country's polite persona.\nOver the weekend, Canadian curler Marc Kennedy had an expletive-filled outburst after Sweden accused him of cheating during a match, and later said his team might be the target of a \"premeditated\" attack by their rivals.\nKennedy was accused of \"double-touching\" - touching the stone a second time after initially releasing it down the ice. The next day, Canadian women's captain Rachel Holman was accused of using the same move. \nBoth have denied the accusations, but Canada's curling teams, who historically have dominated the sport, now face questions over their tactics. \nWhile the curlers have shown their anger over the situation during matches, fans of the sport and Canadians have questioned whether the team acted in the spirit of curling.\n\"It's a sad day for Canadian sport,\" Tim Gray, from Alberta, told the BBC. \"Integrity in the sport is important, even if you have to call it on yourself.\"\nAn opinion piece in the Canadian news outlet, the Globe and Mail, pointed to some of the frustration: \"These Canadian curling teams are not fun bad guys. They come off like the sort of competitors who need so desperately to win that they will do anything \u2013 even things that are pointless \u2013 in order to get there.\"\nCathal Kelly, the writer of the opinion piece, continued: \"There's an easy way out of this \u2013 stop struggling. Stop acting like our curling reputation matters more than our national one. Be the bigger man and woman, even if you don't think you did anything wrong.\"\nThe controversy began on Friday when Swedish player Oskar Eriksson accused Kennedy of double-touching. \nAs the game continued, Kennedy and Eriksson got into a verbal back-and-forth that included expletives. \nTheir exchange quickly went viral as a video appearing to show Kennedy touching the stone on occasion.\nKennedy got a verbal warning from World Curling for using foul language, but he was not formally charged with cheating by the governing body. \nThe next day Kennedy said: \"I probably could have handled it better. But we're human out there and there's a lot of emotions. I'm not going to apologise for defending my teammates and standing up for myself.\" \n\"I've curled my whole life, never once with the intention of getting an advantage through cheating,\" he added. \n![Reuters Canada's mens curlers](https://ichef.bbci.co.uk/news/480/cpsprodpb/ce46/live/f0dc41b0-097b-11f1-9479-1f22ed0be724.jpg.webp)Reuters\nCanada's Marc Kennedy was accused of double-touching stones when he released them\nThen, Canadian curling had another instance of purported cheating. \nMatch officials accused the Canadian women's team on Saturday of the same double-touch violation. \nRachel Homan who said there was a \"zero percent chance\" of the violation, as she and her teammates looked on frustratingly. The Canadians lost to the Swiss, 8-7.\nOn Sunday, Great Britain's men's team was accused of the same violation. \nBoth Homan and British men's curler Bobby Lammie had stones removed from play due to alleged violations.\nHoman later slammed the officials' decision to remove her stone during a defeat to Switzerland, saying it was \"insane\".\nAll of the incidents led World Curling to clarify that double-tapping is not allowed.\n\"During forward motion, touching the granite of the stone is not allowed. This will result in the stone being removed from play,\" they said.\nWorld Curling does not use video to review play, but they did send two officials to monitor how players released their stones in subsequent games. \n\"Following a meeting with representatives of the competing National Olympic Committees, an update in the stone monitoring protocol has been confirmed, beginning with the evening session on Sunday 15 February,\" World Curling said in a statement.\n\"This change in protocol will see the two umpires who had previously been actively monitoring athlete deliveries remain available in the field of play, but will now only monitor athlete deliveries at the request of the competing teams.\"\n![AFP via Getty Images Canada's Rachel Homan \\(centre\\) delivers the stone during the curling women's round robin between Canada and Switzerland on 14 February 2026.](https://ichef.bbci.co.uk/news/480/cpsprodpb/670b/live/12d48f80-0b9c-11f1-8f96-0dc3eb3358b8.jpg)AFP via Getty Images\nCanada's Rachel Homan slammed the officials' decision to remove her stone\nReaction to the controversy in Canada has been mixed.\n\"Do I think the finger affects the rock, no I do not,\" Ankara Leonard from the Royal Montreal Curling Club told the BBC. \"Do I think we have to play within the rules? Yes.\"\nWhile curling columnist and Olympian Tomi Rantamaki, in an article for The Curling News, warned that Canada's dominance in the world of curling means its players should be mindful of the influence they have.\n\"Young players in Finland, Korea, Italy, Sweden \u2013 everywhere \u2013 often copy what Canadian teams do. They copy the athlete's delivery, the sweeping, the tactics, the communication,\" Rantamaki wrote. \"And they copy the behaviour.\"\n## [Canada accused of cheating again in curling row ](https://www.bbc.com/sport/articles/cjen94eg9nko)\n## [Swearing, illicit filming & rule changes - {"url": "https://www.bbc.com/news/articles/ce3g41z58vno", "depth": 1, "published_time": null, "markdown": "[](https://www.bbc.com/)\n[](https://www.bbc.com/sport)\n[](https://www.bbc.com/health)\n[](https://www.bbc.com/weather)\n[](https://www.bbc.com/newsletters)\n# Actor Robert Duvall, who starred in The Godfather, dies aged 95\nWatch: Robert Duvall honoured in Hollywood 'hand and footprint ceremony' in 2011\nActor Robert Duvall, who appeared in The Godfather and Apocalypse Now, has died at the age of 95.\nThe Oscar winner for Tender Mercies died \"peacefully\" at his home in Middleburg, Virginia, on Sunday, according to a statement sent by his PR agency on behalf of his wife, Luciana.\n\"To the world, he was an Academy Award-winning actor, a director, a storyteller. To me, he was simply everything,\" she said. \"His passion for his craft was matched only by his deep love for characters, a great meal, and holding court.\"\nGodfather star Al Pacino paid tribute, saying it was an \"honour\" to work with Duvall. \nHe added: \"He was a born actor as they say, his connection with it, his understanding and his phenomenal gift will always be remembered. \n\"I will miss him.\"\nDuvall was known for numerous tough-guy roles over an impressive six-decade career, such as the mafia consigliere in Francis Ford Coppola\u2018s The Godfather and The Godfather Part II. \nHe also played a forceful army officer in Coppola\u2019s Vietnam epic Apocalypse Now.\nHe only had a few minutes of screen time but his famous line in the 1979 classic, \"I love the smell of napalm in the morning\", became legendary.\nCoppola called his loss \"a blow\".\n\"Such a great actor and such an essential part of American Zoetrope from its beginning,\" Coppola said in a statement on Instagram, referring to his production company.\nHis Apocalypse Now character was originally meant to be even more over the top but Duvall toned it down and the name was changed from Captain Carnage to Lieutenant Colonel William Kilgore.\n\"I did my homework,\" Duvall told veteran talk show host Larry King in 2015. \"I did my research.\u201d\n![Getty Images Robert Duvall in 2018 at the premiere of Widows at the Toronto International Film Festival](https://ichef.bbci.co.uk/news/480/cpsprodpb/192d/live/ffc92110-0b70-11f1-8cee-7bb63a423dd9.jpg.webp)Getty Images\nTributes began to pour in for Duvall after news broke of his death. \nAmerican comedian and actor Adam Sandler posted photographs of the two from their time together shooting the 2022 film Hustle.\n\"Funny as hell. Strong as hell. One of the greatest actors we ever had. Such a great man to talk to and laugh with ... sending his wife Luciana and all his family and friends our condolences.\"\nOscar winner Jamie Lee-Curtis also posted a tribute on Instagram with a picture of Duvall as Tom Hagen in The Godfather. \n\"The greatest consigliere the screen has ever seen. Bravo, Robert Duvall.\" \nCo-star, Robert Patrick, who played the son of Duvall's character in the 2013 film Jayne Mansfield's Car said he was \"gutted\". \n\"Over the years I would call Bobby and we'd talk movies and barbecue. He loved barbecue and I'd always let him know when I was having it in Lockhart, Texas.\"\n\"I will miss Bobby. I will always be proud that I got to play his son. Rest in peace my friend.\" \nThe statement from Duvall's wife continued: \"For each of his many roles, Bob gave everything to his characters and to the truth of the human spirit they represented. In doing so, he leaves something lasting and unforgettable to us all.\n\"Thank you for the years of support you showed Bob and for giving us this time and privacy to celebrate the memories he leaves behind.\"\n![Getty Images Robert Duvall as Tom Hagen and Marlon Brando as Don Vito Corleone in The Now\nNominated for seven Oscars, Duvall won best actor in 1983 for playing a washed-up country singer in Tender Mercies.\nHis other roles included a bullying corporate executive in 1976's Network, and a Marine officer in 1979's The Great Santini, as well as parts in 1990's The Handmaid's Tale and 2014's The Judge alongside Robert Downey Jr.\nDuvall often said his favourite role was as the Texas Ranger-turned-cowboy Augustus McCrae in Lonesome Dove in the 1989 TV mini-series, based on the novel by Larry McMurtry.\nHe made his screen debut in the 1963 film adaptation of Harper Lee's To Kill A Mockingbird, playing the reclusive Boo Radley.\n\"When he did To Kill A Mockingbird he just destroyed you with his performance of Boo Radley, he used not a single word of dialogue, not a single word, and he just shatters you,\" US actor Alec Baldwin said in a short video tribute to Duvall.\nThe screenplay was written by Horton Foote, who also wrote several other films Duvall starred in, including Tender Mercies, Tomorrow and The Chase.\nIn 1997\u2019s The Apostle, which he wrote and directed, Duvall played an evangelical preacher who begins a new life in Louisiana after committing a crime.\nBritish actress Jane Seymour, who worked with Duvall on the 1995 film The Stars Fell on Henrietta, shared a heartfelt tribute on Instagram.\n\"We were able to share in his love of barbecue and even a little tango,\" Seymour wrote alongside a photo of herself with Duvall. \"Those moments off camera were just as memorable as the work itself.\"\n[Film](https://www.bbc.com/news/topics/cg41ylwvgjyt)\n* * *\nRelated\n## [Enigmatic aristocrat was ahead of his time, says It's a Sin Many have been lauded for their realism.\nBut the trend has also [sparked alarm from several Hollywood studios](https://www.bbc.com/news/articles/cjd9nllng22o) that have accused the AI platform's makers of copyright infringement.\nOn Friday, Disney sent a cease-and-desist letter to ByteDance accusing it of supplying Seedance with a \"pirated library\" of the studio's copyrighted characters, including those from Marvel and Star Wars.\nDisney's lawyers accused ByteDance of committing a \"virtual smash-and-grab\" of their intellectual property, including superheroes from Marvel, Star Wars and various cartoons.\nOn Monday ByteDance told the BBC that the company \"respects intellectual property rights and we have heard the concerns regarding Seedance 2.0.\"\n\"We are taking steps to strengthen current safeguards as we work to prevent the unauthorised use of intellectual property and likeness by users.\"\nByteDance did not respond to questions asking for details on the safeguards it plans to implement.\nLike other generative-AI tools, Seedance can create videos based on short text prompts.\nMany of Seedance's clips are based on real actors and shows and some have gone viral since the launch of its latest 2.0 version on 12 February. \nThe BBC has found clips online said to have been generated by Seedance showing Star Wars characters Anakin Skywalker and Rey battling with their lightsabres, and Spider-Man fighting Captain America on the streets of New York.\nThe company has not disclosed what data it uses to train Seedance.\nByteDance had previously said the product had already paused the ability for users to upload images of real people. \nThe company also said it respects intellectual property rights and copyright protections, and takes any potential infringement seriously.\nDisney last year \n## [Hollywood writers fear losing work to AI](https://www.bbc.com/news/business-66289583)\n## [DeepSeek: The Chinese AI app that has the world {"url": "https://www.bbc.com/news/articles/c1d64p3q2d0o", "depth": 1, "published_time": null, "markdown": "[](https://www.bbc.com/)\n[](https://www.bbc.com/sport)\n[](https://www.bbc.com/health)\n[](https://www.bbc.com/weather)\n[](https://www.bbc.com/newsletters)\n# US build-up of warships and fighter jets tracked near Iran\n![BBC USS Abraham Lincoln in the Arabian Sea and satellite image of the aircraft carrier ](https://ichef.bbci.co.uk/news/480/cpsprodpb/4e6f/live/ac6fcc90-0b60-11f1-b5e2-dd58fc65f0f6.png.webp)BBC\nBBC Verify has confirmed the location of US aircraft carrier the USS Abraham Lincoln near Iran using satellite imagery, as Washington continues to put pressure on the country over its military program and recent \nUS and Iranian officials are set to meet in Switzerland on Tuesday for a second round of talks. Iran says the meeting will focus on its \nThe Abraham Lincoln, which leads a strike group with three guided missile destroyers, carries 90 aircraft including F35 fighters, and 5,680 crew, was reportedly deployed to the Gulf region in late January but has not been seen in satellite imagery until now. It has been located off the coast of Oman, around 700km from Iran.\nThe US has also reportedly sent the USS Gerald R Ford, the world's largest warship, to the Middle East, which could arrive in the region within the next three weeks.\nThe Abraham Lincoln's arrival adds to what we know about the current US military build-up in the Middle East over the past few weeks, where BBC Verify has tracked an increase of US destroyers, combat ships and fighter jets in the region.\n![Reuters The U.S. Navy's Nimitz-class aircraft carrier USS Abraham Lincoln, Arleigh Burke-class guided-missile destroyer USS Frank E. Petersen Jr. and Lewis and Clark-class dry cargo ship USNS Carl Brashear sail during a photo exercise in the Arabian Sea, February 6, 2026. ](https://ichef.bbci.co.uk/news/480/cpsprodpb/101a/live/4659d600-0b5e-11f1-b5e2-dd58fc65f0f6.jpg.webp)Reuters\nIn February the US military released an image of the Lincoln carrier strike group in the Arabian Sea \n## What military assets has the US moved into the Middle East?\nPublicly available images from the European Sentinel-2 satellites show Abraham Lincoln in the Arabian Sea around 150 miles (240km) off the coast of Oman.\nIt had not been seen since it reportedly entered the region in January but it had been crossing the open sea where satellite coverage is limited. Military assets on land are more visible and frequently captured on satellite.\nIt means we have now tracked 12 US ships in the Middle East through satellite imagery: the Abraham Lincoln, a nuclear-powered Nimitz-class carrier, which together with three Arleigh Burke class destroyers forms a carrier strike group; plus two destroyers capable of carrying out long-range missile strikes and three specialist ships for combat near to the shore that are currently positioned at Bahrain naval station in the Gulf. Two other destroyers have been seen in the eastern Mediterranean near the Souda Bay US base, and one more in the Red Sea.\nWe have also been following the movements of US aircraft in the region, where we have seen an increase of F-15 and EA-18 fighter jets stationed in Muwaffaq Salti military base in Jordan, and an increase in US cargo planes and refuelling and communications aircraft moving towards the Middle East from the US and Europe.\n![Satellite image of USS Abraham Lincoln in the Arabian Sea next to map of Iran, Saudi Arabia, Yemen and Oman showing the USS Abraham Lincoln off the coast of Oman ](https://ichef.bbci.co.uk/news/480/cpsprodpb/6d89/live/b79c2ba0-0b5f-11f1-b5e2-dd58fc65f0f6.png.webp)\n## How has Iran responded?\nThe US Central Command released images of the Abraham Lincoln flanked by destroyers, fighter jets, surveillance aircraft and coastguard vessels in the Arabian Sea in an apparent show of military prowess on 6 February, which Iran has met with its own show of force.\nOn Monday the Islamic Revolutionary Guard Corps (IRGC) launched a maritime drill in the Strait of Hormuz, located in the Gulf between Oman and Iran. The drill saw IRGC Commander\u2011in\u2011Chief Maj Gen Mohammad Pakpour inspecting naval vessels at a harbour before missiles are seen launching from a ship, the IRCG-linked Tasnim News Agency reported.\nThe Strait is considered one of the world's most important shipping routes and a vital oil transit choke point. Around a fifth of the world's oil and gas flows through the Strait, including from Kharg Island, Iran's main oil export terminal. Pakpour was seen flying over the island in a helicopter in the report showing Iran's latest military manoeuvres.\n![USS Abraham Lincoln, Nimitz-class nuclear-powered aircraft carrier graphic showing speed 30+ knots 34.5pmh crew \\(ship & air\\) 5,680, propulsion 2 nuclear reactors 4 shafts aircraft 90 range unlimited displacement 88,000 tonnes and comparison in size with Eiffel Tower ](https://ichef.bbci.co.uk/news/480/cpsprodpb/add2/live/4ee49d40-0b5f-11f1-9972-d3f265c101c6.png.webp)\n## How does it compare with Venezuela and Operation Midnight Hammer?\nMilitary intelligence expert Justin Crump told BBC Verify that the current US military preparations in the Middle East show \"more depth and sustainability\" than its manoeuvres ahead of the seizure of former Venezuelan president Nicholas Maduro in January, or the operation carrying out air strikes on Iran's nuclear facilities last June.\nThey all feature a carrier strike group and several destroyers operating independently. However, the \nThe US deployed the Gerald R Ford to the Caribbean ahead of its strikes on Venezuela, one of eight warships we tracked in the region at the time, though it used fewer aircraft as it could easily send jets from surrounding US bases on America's mainland or from its base in Puerto Rico instead. The US also deployed amphibious assault ships within the Caribbean, which can be used as launch platforms for helicopter operations as was seen with the capture of Maduro. But Venezuela's military is generally seen as less capable of defending itself or retaliating against the US.\nWhen the US struck Iran last year in Operation Midnight Hammer, which \n![Map showing Jordan, Bahrain, Qatar and Iran. Fighter jets deployed to Muwaffaq Salti Air Base in Jordan. Destroyers and combat ships at Khalifa Bin Salman Port in Bahrain. New air defences at Al-Udeid Air Base in Qatar. ](https://ichef.bbci.co.uk/news/480/cpsprodpb/ba8b/live/135bd4a0-0b5f-11f1-9972-d3f265c101c6.png.webp)\nDuring Midnight Hammer the US had two aircraft carrier strike groups in the region, five destroyers placed in the Mediterranean and Red Seas, and three combat ships in the Gulf. It had also moved squadrons of fighter jets and refuelling aircraft from the US to Europe, but the B2 stealth bomber flights that were used to hit the Fordo, Isfahan and Natanz nuclear sites actually took off from US bases in Missouri.\nCrump, chief executive of risk and intelligence company Sibylline, said the build-up of US warships and aircraft, as well as eight existing airbases in the region would allow it to conduct a \"fairly intensive and sustained strike rate\" of about 800 sorties a day, with the aim of rendering any Iranian responses \"ineffective\".\n\"What we are seeing isn't just strike preparation, but rather a broader deterrent deployment capable of being scaled up or down,\" he said. \"This means it has more depth and sustainability than the force packages arranged for either Venezuela or Midnight Hammer last year. It's designed to sustain an engagement and counter all potential responses against US assets in the region and, of course, Israel.\"\n_Additional reporting by Barbara Metzler, Ghoncheh Habibiazad, Thomas Copeland, Yi Ma_\n[Iran](https://www.bbc.com/news/topics/cjnwl8q4ggwt)\n[United States](https://www.bbc.com/news/topics/cx1m7zg01xyt)\n* * *\nRelated\n## [Trump says he will be 'indirectly' involved in Iran nuclear talks](https://www.bbc.com/news/articles/cwyknjnd706o)\n## [Iran must abandon enriched uranium and not produce more, Netanyahu says](https://www.bbc.com/news/articles/cly191kz1lpo)\n## [Iran ready to discuss compromises to reach nuclear deal, minister tells BBC in Tehran](https://www.bbc.com/news/articles/clyz4y3zwz5o)\n* * *\nMore from the BBC\n[ Just now ![Hillary Clinton in BBC interview](https://ichef.bbci.co.uk/news/480/cpsprodpb/184b/live/2519e010-0bcd-11f1-b7e1-afb6d0884c18.jpg) Hillary Clinton accuses Trump administration of Epstein files 'cover-up' in BBC interview \"Get the files out. Satlat tries to fight back before one of the passengers appears to strangle him until he goes limp.\nThe trio, who were arrested over the weekend, were in court in Pretoria on Monday and have since abandoned their bail applications. They were not asked to comment on the charges.\nA fourth suspect later handed himself over to police on Monday.\nHe will appear in court on Tuesday, police said in a brief update after Dikeledi Mphela, 24, Goitsione Machidi, 25 and McClaren Mushwana, 30 made a brief appearance. All four also face robbery charges.\nSatlat, 22, was a Nigerian national but, in a country that has often been blighted by xenophobic violence, his family said the attack was not linked to his nationality.\nAccording to prosecutors, the accused ordered a ride on e-hailing platform Bolt last Wednesday using a number not registered in any of their names.\nWhen the car arrived, Mphela and the fourth suspect allegedly got into the car while Machidi and Mushwana followed them in a separate car, authorities added.\n## [Gunmen kill nine in South Africa tavern attack](https://www.bbc.com/news/articles/c79xyxdpzrxo)\nThey then \"forced the deceased to stop the vehicle, strangled him to death and robbed him of his cell phone and vehicle which was later recovered\", the prosecuting authority's spokesperson Lumka Mahanjana said.\nThere has been a growing number of reports of e-hailing drivers coming under attack in South Africa, with many drivers calling for greater protection, as the country grapples with high crime levels and one of the highest murder rates in the world.\nThe e-hailing partners' council condemned Satlat's killing, adding that it was not an \"isolated incident\". \nThe organisation praised the role that the dashcam footage and social media played in capturing his murder but reiterated calls for \"preventative security measures\" to better protect drivers.\nIt also called on e-hailing companies to \"vet and verify passengers to prevent criminals masquerading as customers\".\nNumerous political parties and e-hailing drivers gathered outside the court house on Monday to protest against Satlat's murder.\nOne driver called for the government to set up a task team to deal with the attacks on them, according to local broadcaster Newzroom Afrika.\nHe also called for the establishment of a system to compensate the families of drivers killed on the job. Spokesperson for the Satlat family Solomon Izang Ashoms said his relatives were left with unanswered questions.\n\"His dad is struggling, we're very afraid for [him] because his blood pressure's been shooting up [since the death],\" Ashoms said.\nThe case against the three was postponed to next Monday.\nMore BBC stories on South Africa:\n## [The king's speech - {"url": "https://www.bbc.com/news/articles/c5y4zl0w062o", "depth": 1, "published_time": null, "markdown": "[](https://www.bbc.com/)\n[](https://www.bbc.com/sport)\n[](https://www.bbc.com/health)\n[](https://www.bbc.com/weather)\n[](https://www.bbc.com/newsletters)\n# Reddit's human content wins amid the AI flood\n![Ines Tan Wearing a black leather jacket. Ines Tan smiles while standing at a waterfront.](https://ichef.bbci.co.uk/news/480/cpsprodpb/f3a2/live/cbb4bc20-fd17-11f0-9972-d3f265c101c6.jpg.webp)Ines Tan\nReddit is an \"empathetic\" place says Ines Tan\nFor Ines Tan there's one particular site she turns to again and again for advice \u2013 and that's Reddit.\nTan, who works in communications, regularly jumps on the site for skincare advice, to view reactions to shows she watches, such as The Traitors, and for help planning her upcoming wedding in May.\n\"It's a very empathetic place,\" she says of Reddit. \"For my wedding, I've found help emotionally, logistically and inspiration-wise.\"\nTan believes people are consulting the online discussion platform more as they're craving human interaction in the world of increasing AI slop.\nTan is far from the only person turning to Reddit, which launched in 2005 and was once viewed as a place for nerds.\nIn both the US and the UK women account for more than 50% of Reddit users. Amongst women in the UK, Reddit is the fastest-growing social platform. \nReddit is organised into user-created communities called subreddits, where content is ranked by user voting rather than chronological order.\nHere users can comment, post links, and add pictures \u2013 though many read the content without engaging.\nVolunteer moderators help run the site by managing specific communities and ensure users stick to the rules and keep to the subject.\nReddit also employs admins who can overrule moderators and delete subreddits.\nAccording to Reddit, that human element it key.\n\"I do think that in a world of AI \u2013 and there is a lot more AI driven content that's really crowded the internet - people recognise that what Reddit offers stands out more,\" says Jen Wong, chief operating officer at Reddit.\n\"The internet has become a place that maybe isn't all that human in authenticity which was maybe the original promise\u2026 but Reddit has preserved that.\"\nShe says popular topics gaining traction include parenting, reality TV and skincare.\nWong says the site's demographic has changed over time. \"We had this reputation of being gamers and tech guys\u2026 but now we're very gender balanced and very strong with Gen Z women and that's because we've grown in terms of our breadth of topics.\"\n![Reddit Jen Wong wearing a black jacket with a light pink shirt](https://ichef.bbci.co.uk/news/480/cpsprodpb/c717/live/a1c0c2e0-0034-11f1-b7e1-afb6d0884c18.jpg.webp)Reddit\nReddit has preserved \"human authenticity\" says Jen Wong\nHowever, the site faces issues concerning credibility of conversations on subreddits and inconsistent approaches to moderation.\n\"Reddit's biggest weakness is that credibility can look like consensus, like most other social media sites and their algorithms,\" says Dr Yusuf Oc, senior lecturer in marketing at Bayes Business School in London.\n\"Upvotes reward what a community likes, not what is true, so you can get information cascades, groupthink, and strong echo chambers in certain subreddits.\"\nCreating a decentralised site reliant on moderators has also proved problematic at times.\n\"The platform is also unevenly moderated, and quality varies massively by community,\" say Oc.\nThere are several ways a subreddit can be manipulated. \"Brigading\" involves a coordinated attack by social media users on another group. In Reddit that would be a group of users looking to manipulate the conversation or voting pattern on a subreddit.\n\"Astroturfing\", is another issue, where the true sponsor of a campaign is hidden behind a grassroots initiative.\nOc says that Reddit is vulnerable to such coordinated influence operations, which can push content up or bury it quickly.\n\"Narratives can be amplified or suppressed through coordinated activity and social pressure within communities. Moderators are volunteers so they can face pressure from highly-motivated groups, harassment, or sustained reporting campaigns, and because norms differ by subreddit, enforcement can be inconsistent.\"\nIn its defence a Reddit spokesperson said: \"Reddit's policies prohibit manipulated content and inauthentic behaviour and our safety teams enforce these rules with a combination of human review and sophisticated automated tooling that can detect this content at scale.\n\"On top of this, Reddit's community moderators set and enforce subreddit-specific rules that can be even stricter.\"\n![Josh Feldberg Josh Feldberg crouches down next to his dog](https://ichef.bbci.co.uk/news/480/cpsprodpb/89ee/live/6d4cd680-fd18-11f0-b7e1-afb6d0884c18.jpg.webp)Josh Feldberg\nJosh Feldberg says Reddit is useful for advice about pets\nJosh Feldberg, who lives in London, started using Reddit about 14 years ago. \"I started using it because I have ADHD - I didn't get a proper diagnosis for years - and then a friend said they sometimes share resources on there.\"\nThat led to Feldberg discovering other areas of interest such as fitness and more specifically, pet parrots. He said he favours it compares to other platforms. \"As it's anonymous, it essentially offers quality comments and the feedback is quite kind.\"\n\"There's a subreddit [community] for everything,\" adds the digital consultant for charities and non-profit who spends his time looking at subjects such as politics, tech and digital marketing.\nSome argue that Reddit's surge in popularity is down to deals signed with AI providers, including OpenAI. Under the deal between those two firm's OpenAI's ChatGPT gets access to Reddit content.\n\"These deals mostly increase Reddit's visibility and strategic importance, rather than being the original reason people use it,\" says Oc.\n\"AI answers and search features can now cite Reddit more often which reinforces discovery and traffic at the margin.\"\nBut Reddit's Wong feels people are visiting Reddit because of the \"authenticity, the content, and because they're discussing things that you really can't get elsewhere\".\nShe cites threads about people going bald or going through pregnancy.\n\"Those are deeply personal experiences where you want to hear from other people who have been there. I think you can't really get that in other places.\"\nIn the world of highly-polished content on social media sites such as Instagram and as content feels increasingly automated, \"people look for signals of lived experience, disagreement and nuance\", says Oc.\n\"Reddit's anonymity and community norms make answers feel more candid and less polished than influencer-style content.\"\nAs for Feldberg, during his 14 years on the platform he says he's noticed a number of changes including a more modern interface, more targeted ads, and the move away from seeing how many downvotes a post would attract.\nHe personally feels that there's fewer egos on Reddit compared to other social media platforms.\n\"If someone posts on Instagram and they have loads of followers, they will get more engagement and be pushed up the feed, but on Reddit there's no bias\u2026people don't post with that intention or for getting free stuff. It's anti that. There's no economic incentive to post stuff. Personally, it's as useful as ever.\"\nMore Technology of Business\n[Social media](https://www.bbc.com/news/topics/c207p54m4pdt)\n[International Business](https://www.bbc.com/news/topics/c4nrdv135zet)\n[Artificial intelligence](https://www.bbc.com/news/topics/ce1qrvleleqt)\n[Reddit](https://www.bbc.com/news/topics/cq265r29p6dt)\n[Technology of Business](https://www.bbc.com/news/topics/cw9l5jelpl1t)\n* * *\nRelated\n## [Bereaved mum in tears as data campaign to become law](https://www.bbc.com/news/articles/cze0p7551d3o)\n## [New adverts urge boys not to share sexist content online](https://www.bbc.com/news/articles/cp9mxgldmp0o)\n## [We will do battle with AI chatbots as we did with Grok, says Starmer](https://www.bbc.com/news/articles/cvg38x13x5yo)\n* * *\nMore from the BBC\n[ 46 mins ago ![A man shows visitors a robot at the AI Summit](https://ichef.bbci.co.uk/news/480/cpsprodpb/8f32/live/26201330-0bd2-11f1-b7e1-afb6d0884c18.jpg) Long queues and confusion mar first day of India's landmark AI summit The India-AI Impact Summit 2026 in Delhi is expected to be attended by top tech leaders from the world. ](https://www.bbc.com/news/articles/ceqvjgrvpn3o)\n[ 4 hrs ago ![Thomas Pritzker speaking to an audience during a panel while gesturing with his hands](https://ichef.bbci.co.uk/news/480/cpsprodpb/8301/live/b5803990-0b98-11f1-8310-539e7a6a061d.jpg) Hyatt Hotels chairman steps down over Jeffrey Epstein ties Billionaire Thomas Pritzker said he had exercised \"terrible judgement\" in keeping contact with Epstein. ](https://www.bbc.com/news/articles/c93wk4d7x19o)\n[ 8 hrs ago ![A man wearing a black pack takes a photo of a sign for the event, which says \"Hashtag India AI Impact Summit 2026\" in large white standalone letters. {"url": "https://www.bbc.com/news/articles/c1d6pkdk1xzo", "depth": 1, "published_time": null, "markdown": "[](https://www.bbc.com/)\n[](https://www.bbc.com/sport)\n[](https://www.bbc.com/health)\n[](https://www.bbc.com/weather)\n[](https://www.bbc.com/newsletters)\n# Oasis v Blur rivalry being revived in new play as cast take sides\n![Getty Images Damon Albarn wearing a black leather jacket with his arm around Noel Gallagher at the Brit Awards 2013](https://ichef.bbci.co.uk/news/480/cpsprodpb/849a/live/d67d1c00-0808-11f1-b56b-bf8bb0174edb.jpg.webp)Getty Images\nDamon Albarn and Noel Gallagher, pictured in 2013, buried the hatchet after the 1990s\n**The much-hyped 1995 Britpop battle between Oasis and Blur has been recreated for a new play in which the cast take sides.**\nAt 21, George Usher has spent most of his life unwittingly researching his first professional acting role - as Liam Gallagher.\n\"My dad was always massively into Oasis, so I grew up listening to it, and I was always such a big fan of him and how funny he is,\" he says. \"So to get to play him is just a dream.\"\nAsked to choose between the two Britpop bands, Usher sides with Oasis, of course. Alongside him, Paddy Stafford, who's portraying Liam's big brother Noel, slips into character as he adds: \"Greatest band in the world, do you know what I mean?\"\nThat view's not shared by the actors who are playing Oasis's rivals in the Britpop wars.\nOscar Lloyd insists Blur are best. \"Team colours, man, we've got to represent,\" the 27-year-old says, his floppy hair and fresh face making him the image of frontman Damon Albarn.\n\"It's the way they've reinvented themselves so many times. He just is clearly a musical genius.\"\n![Danny With A Camera George Usher and Paddy Stafford face-to-face in an argument as Liam and Noel Gallagher in a scene from The Battle](https://ichef.bbci.co.uk/news/480/cpsprodpb/db9f/live/e69782c0-0b59-11f1-b472-df9c1c12cffb.jpg.webp)Danny With A Camera\nGeorge Usher plays Liam Gallagher (left) with Paddy Stafford as brother Noel\nThe actors are starring together in The Battle, which has just opened at the Birmingham Rep theatre. It tells the story of the chart race that was the climax of an at-times acrimonious beef between Britpop's biggest beasts, which split fans and captivated the media.\nUnlike the real battle of the bands, the rivalry between the two sets of actors is entirely good-natured.\nHe may be playing Liam, but Usher admits to having a soft spot for Blur, too.\n\"I probably shouldn't be saying this. It feels a bit wrong,\" he says. \"But yeah, I do like them.\n\"They're not as good as Oasis - but you know, they've got some tunes.\"\n![Getty Images Liam Gallagher jostling Damon Albarn during a music industry celebrity football match](https://ichef.bbci.co.uk/news/480/cpsprodpb/924c/live/dcb66410-0807-11f1-b56b-bf8bb0174edb.jpg.webp)Getty Images\nLiam and Damon took their rivalry to the football pitch in 1996\nThree decades after they became Britain's biggest bands, interest in both is still huge - among their original fans and a new generation who were weaned on their songs.\nThe bands started 1995 on friendly terms, but as their stratospheric success grew, so did the comparisons and competition.\n\"By the time you get to July and August, they're literally wishing death on each other and the knives are out,\" writer John Niven says. \"So as a dramatist, you think, well, how did we get to that in five months?\"\nNiven's well-placed to tell that story. He was working for a record label in 1995 and is best-known for his 2008 novel Kill Your Friends, a wild tale of sex, drugs and violence in the 90s music industry.\n![George Usher \\(Liam Gallagher\\) standing next to and looking at Oscar Lloyd \\(Damon Albarn\\), both standing against a wooden wall with their arms folded](https://ichef.bbci.co.uk/news/480/cpsprodpb/627f/live/59c54a20-0b5a-11f1-b472-df9c1c12cffb.jpg.webp)\nThe best of frenemies: George Usher (Liam) and Oscar Lloyd (Damon) during a break in rehearsals at Birmingham Rep\nBlur and Oasis started rubbing each other up the wrong way when Liam made comments about Damon's girlfriend, Elastica singer Justine Frischmann, and when Blur turned up at an Oasis party.\nNoel caused controversy by saying he hoped Albarn and Blur bassist Alex James would \"catch Aids and die\". (He later apologised.)\nWhen Oasis scheduled their single Roll With It for release a week before Blur's Country House that August, Blur were worried about the possible ignominy of being kept off the number one spot. So they ended up moving Country House to the same date, setting up a head-to-head contest.\nThe chart battle was also billed as a class war. The press depicted it as loud-mouthed working-class northerners versus art-school middle-class southerners.\nThe NME's front page headline called it the \"British heavyweight championship\", and the wider media were also mad for it. A BBC TV News report about the chart battle is remembered as the moment when alternative music culture truly broke through to the mainstream.\nWatch: The Blur vs Oasis chart battle made headline news on BBC TV in 1995\nNiven has been teaching the young cast members about the history. \"It's hard to explain to them how something like this could dominate the entire culture - that it would be on every music paper, every broadsheet, every tabloid, the 10 O'Clock News at night,\" the writer says.\n\"Now, of course, everything's so fragmented. Somebody can have 30 million TikTok followers and play Wembley Arena, and I would never have heard of them.\n\"Whereas back then, the only way you got success was via the press or the TV or radio. So people from six to 60 would be across the Blur/Oasis thing.\"\n![Danny With A Camera Matthew Horne in a leather jacket, jeans and red canvas trainers, next to Oscar Lloyd in striped blue T-shirt and jeans, both sitting in front of a recording mixing desk and speaking in a scene from The Battle](https://ichef.bbci.co.uk/news/480/cpsprodpb/6c31/live/7a565870-0b59-11f1-b472-df9c1c12cffb.jpg.webp)Danny With A Camera\nMatthew Horne (left) stars as Andy Ross, head of Blur's label Food Records\nGavin and Stacey star Matthew Horne, who plays Blur's record label boss Andy Ross, remembers those days fondly.\n\"Starting this job was quite overwhelming for me,\" he says. \"I didn't realise how nostalgic it would be.\n\"I was 16 or 17, and being part of that movement, plus all the other bands, really gave me a sense of belonging as a teenager and solidified my passion for music, which I have now.\"\nThe Britpop era was \"a seminal movement\", he reflects. \"And it's a movement in British music, certainly, that won't ever be replicated, just because of the nature of the landscape of the music industry. Hopefully that's what this play brings out.\"\nThe Battle is directed by Olivier and Tony Award-nominated Matthew Dunster, and also stars Bridgerton's Harriet Cains as Justine and EastEnders' Louisa Lytton as Noel's then-girlfriend Meg Mathews.\n\"We're seeing people turning up to the theatre in Blur or Oasis T-shirts,\" says Lytton. \"It takes people back to, hopefully, a really great time in their lives. You can see it on their faces.\n\"And we're really pleased it's bringing in a whole new audience, because that's what theatre needs.\"\n![Four actors standing together in a row smiling with their arms around each other](https://ichef.bbci.co.uk/news/480/cpsprodpb/d033/live/23cc06f0-08e6-11f1-8adf-6f6ee42cf6d7.jpg.webp)\nLeft-right: Oscar Lloyd (Damon Albarn), Brandon Bendell (Alex James), Paddy Stafford (Noel Gallagher) and George Usher (Liam Gallagher)\nThe actors playing the Gallagher brothers were among the million or so fans who saw Oasis on their hugely successful reunion tour last year. It was the next best thing to experiencing their 90s heyday first-hand, Usher says.\n\"It was a time when the style was unbelievable, which is shown in this play, but just living such a free life without everything being on the phone.\n\"That was what was amazing going to see Oasis - it's like being transported back to that time, and I think that's what this show maybe does for people as well.\"\nThe differences between the two eras have also struck Lloyd while he's been playing Damon.\n\"It's nuts because it's 30 years ago, but [this play] kind of feels like a period piece,\" he says. \"To us, it does feel so different.\n\"We're doing a lot of party scenes and it's very easy for us to forget that there'd be a big difference in going to a party when no-one's got a phone and everyone's locked into this shared atmosphere.\n\"Maybe the hedonism of the time is a little bit different, where I feel like we're all quite censored a bit more nowadays in what we say and what we do.\"\nBrandon Bendell, who's playing Alex from Blur, agrees that must be felt by modern music stars, too.\n\"Yeah, [they're] more followed, monitored. I think pop stars, rock stars, they had a bit more freedom back in the day to act however they wanted without repercussions.\"\n![John Niven laughing and looking out of the window in the Birmingham Rep theatre bar](https://ichef.bbci.co.uk/news/480/cpsprodpb/2317/live/f900d3b0-08e5-11f1-8adf-6f6ee42cf6d7.jpg.webp)\nThe Battle is novelist John Niven's debut as a playwright\nAs for how the story ends - although it's based on real events, lots of people seeing the show don't know who won the chart battle.\nNiven recently appeared on a podcast whose host asked her listeners if they knew the outcome. \"So few of them knew who'd won, especially the younger generation,\" he says.\n\"I think they just know that it's a bit like the Bloods and the Crips in LA - there's this historic gangland beef between the two sides, but nobody really knows how it got started or who won it.\"\nThe show is, in fact, billed as being only \"mostly\" based on real events, and the writer and cast allude to a \"twist\".\nSpoiler: In reality, Blur beat Oasis to number one that week in 1995.\nBut Oasis went on to dominate the charts and airwaves later that year with (What's The Story) Morning Glory?, and that album has been back in the top 40 for more than a year since reunion fever took hold.\nThey may have lost the battle, but Oasis arguably won the war.\nBut then again... Blur were and still are brilliant. Just in a different way from their Britpop counterparts.\n\"I always loved both groups,\" Niven says when asked which side he was on.\n\"I didn't see why anybody felt compelled to pick a team. But a lot of people did, thankfully for the play.\"\n_The Battle is at Birmingham Rep until 7 March and at Manchester Opera House from 17-21 March._\n_Additional interviews by Colin Paterson._\n[Theatre](https://www.bbc.com/news/topics/c008ql15v4yt)\n[Blur](https://www.bbc.com/news/topics/cwezzj90qmet)\n[Oasis](https://www.bbc.com/news/topics/cyeljp82y8dt)\n[Music](https://www.bbc.com/news/topics/cz4pr2gd872t)\n* * *\nRelated\n## ['I'm nearly 90 and have just acted in my final play'](https://www.bbc.com/news/articles/c24d3l0evmdo)\n## [European boost for \u00a330m Shakespeare theatre project](https://www.bbc.com/news/articles/c4gqx08jd34o)\n## [Bacon off the menu for Peppa Pig family show](https://www.bbc.com/news/articles/c1kgnp7xwx2o)\n* * *\nMore from the BBC\n[ 3 hrs ago ![Billy Steinberg during the 2008 ASCAP \"I Create Music\" EXPO held at the Renaissance Hotel on April 11, 2008 in Los Angeles, California](https://ichef.bbci.co.uk/news/480/cpsprodpb/d7a4/live/b3178910-0bbb-11f1-ab66-0b9a69be6bdd.jpg) Billy Steinberg, Madonna's Like a Virgin songwriter, dies at 75 Hailed as among the \"most successful\" songwriters, Steinberg wrote for artists including Whitney Houston and Roy Orbison. ](https://www.bbc.com/news/articles/cvgveqzld59o)\n[ 15 hrs ago ![Harry Styles at the Grammys wearing a sparkly grey jacket and black collar](https://ichef.bbci.co.uk/news/480/cpsprodpb/16d2/live/0a9f84d0-0b58-11f1-b472-df9c1c12cffb.jpg) Harry Styles named curator of 2026 Meltdown Festival Harry Styles will join the list of artists that have overseen the annual London event. ](https://www.bbc.com/news/articles/cx28q0wxgw0o)\n[ 19 hrs ago ![A close-up of Lewis Capaldi on stage singing into a microphone and playing the guitar. Ken Goldin is next to them, and they're all smiling for the cameras](https://ichef.bbci.co.uk/news/480/cpsprodpb/5c7e/live/ceec02b0-0b57-11f1-b472-df9c1c12cffb.jpg.webp)Goldin\nLogan Paul was given a Guinness World Records certificate, and presented the card to its new owner AJ Scaramucci (centre) alongside auction boss Ken Goldin (right) \nLogan Paul has sold an ultra-rare Pok\u00e9mon trading card for a record-shattering $16.5m (\u00a312m), according to the auction company.\nThe YouTuber and wrestler bought the Pikachu Illustrator card - the best preserved example of one of the rarest Pok\u00e9mon cards ever - in a deal worth $5.3m (\u00a33.9m) in 2021, \nHe has now sold it for $16.492m, including buyer's premium, according to \nThe winning bidder was venture capitalist AJ Scaramucci, son of financier and former White House communications director Anthony Scaramucci. AJ Scaramucci said it was the first acquisition of a planned \"planetary treasure hunt\".\n![Goldin The card showing Japanese writing, a Pikachu illustration and PSA grading information surrounded by diamonds](https://ichef.bbci.co.uk/news/480/cpsprodpb/c30b/live/378533a0-0b58-11f1-b5e2-dd58fc65f0f6.jpg.webp)Goldin\nThe Pikachu Illustrator card is housed in a diamond-encrusted box\nThe auction result was \"absolutely insane\", Paul said on a YouTube live stream as confetti fell.\nGuinness World Records adjudicator Sarah Casson then appeared to declare it the most expensive trading card ever sold at auction.\nPaul was then surprised by Scaramucci, who told him about \n\"I'm on a quest to buy a T-Rex dinosaur fossil, I'm going to buy the Declaration of Independence, and I'm not stopping there,\" he said. \"This was only the beginning.\"\nPaul included the diamond necklace he used to wear it during his WWE debut at WrestleMania 38 in 2022, which he hung around Scaramucci's neck.\n## Virtually perfect\nPikachu Illustrator cards were issued as part of a contest in 1998 and only about 40 are known to exist.\nPaul's card was the only one of those to have been graded as a 10 by PSA (Professional Sports Authenticator), which rates the conditions of trading cards, meaning it is a \"virtually perfect card\".\nTrading cards related to the hugely popular Japanese animation franchise have soared in value in recent years.\nAuction boss Ken Goldin was seen persuading Paul to part with his prized possession in the latest season of Netflix's King of Collectibles: The Goldin Touch.\n## Expensive wedding\n\"The Pok\u00e9mon market is blazing hot. It's dominating the market and that's the number one card,\" Goldin told him.\nHe offered Paul a $2.5m (\u00a31.8m) advance and a cut of everything else sold in his Pok\u00e9mon and TCG (trading card game) auction, of which the Pikachu Illustrator was the star lot.\nPaul admitted the funds would be useful for his \"expensive wedding\".\n\"I'm not a reseller. I'm a collector,\" he told Goldin on the show. \"But, candidly, I could use the liquidity. I'm good at making money. I'm better at spending money.\"\nPaul married Danish model Nina Agdal in a lavish wedding in Lake Como, Italy, in August 2025.\n[Pokemon Go](https://www.bbc.com/news/topics/c207p54m4e5t)\n[Logan Paul](https://www.bbc.com/news/topics/cwm19wg3l15t)\n* * *\nRelated\n## [Pok\u00e9mon cards worth \u00a365,000 stolen in 'killer' blow to games shop](https://www.bbc.com/news/articles/cdjmejlwnywo)\n## [Owner says burgled shop 'not a safe space' for him](https://www.bbc.com/news/articles/cly5jze10y9o)\n## [Shop owner in shock after \u00a3100k Pok\u00e9mon cards theft](https://www.bbc.com/news/articles/cn0ynew9r9yo)\n* * *\nMore from the BBC\n[ 21 Jan 2026 ![Maxxine Dupri is pictured in a really close-up shot, mid-jump in a wrestling ring. {"url": "https://www.bbc.com/news/articles/cx2g4qglzz8o", "depth": 1, "published_time": null, "markdown": "[](https://www.bbc.com/)\n[](https://www.bbc.com/sport)\n[](https://www.bbc.com/health)\n[](https://www.bbc.com/weather)\n[](https://www.bbc.com/newsletters)\n# Obama clarifies views on aliens after saying 'they're real' on podcast\n![Reuters A headshot of Barack Obama against a black background](https://ichef.bbci.co.uk/news/480/cpsprodpb/3917/live/17923be0-0b1e-11f1-b2d9-a9bd89759413.jpg.webp)Reuters\nObama initially commented on the subject as part of a quickfire round of questions on a podcast\nFormer US President Barack Obama has clarified that the chances Earth has been visited by aliens is \"low\" after comments he made about extra-terrestrial species caught attention online.\n\"They're real but I haven't seen them,\" he told American podcast host Brian Tyler Cohen during an interview published on Saturday.\n\"They're not being kept in Area 51. There's no underground facility unless there's this enormous conspiracy and they hid it from the president of the United States,\" he added.\nObama has since said his comments were made in line with \"the spirit of the speed round\" of questions, adding that statistically the chances are that life is out there but he saw \"no evidence\" while president.\nObama's initial comments were made during a quickfire \"lightning\" round of questions.\n\"Are aliens real?\" Cohen had asked as his first question of the round for the former president.\nObama's answer was subsequently run in different media outlets and attracted attention online - prompting him to publish a clarifying statement on his official Instagram account on Sunday.\n\"I was trying to stick with the spirit of the speed round, but since it's gotten attention let me clarify,\" he wrote in a caption that accompanied the video clip from the podcast.\n\"Statistically, the universe is so vast that the odds are good there's life out there. But the distances between solar systems are so great that the chances we've been visited by aliens is low, and I saw no evidence during my presidency that extraterrestrials have made contact with us. Really!\"\nObama, who served as president of the United States between 2009 and 2017, has previously made comments about aliens and UFOs -\n\"They did a little bit of research and the answer was no,\" he said - but also pointed to records and footage of objects in the sky that had not been explained at the time.\n[Extraterrestrial life](https://www.bbc.com/news/topics/c95znz24zkkt)\n[Barack Obama](https://www.bbc.com/news/topics/cvenzmgywl4t)\n[United States](https://www.bbc.com/news/topics/cx1m7zg01xyt)\n* * *\nRelated\n## [Camera being designed to search for alien life](https://www.bbc.com/news/articles/c5y9lz2yj8lo)\n## [Where we might find aliens in the next decade](https://www.bbc.com/future/article/20240308-how-the-hunt-for-alien-life-is-hotting-up)\n## [Life on Mars? {"url": "https://www.bbc.com/news/articles/cy9gdvl4zv4o", "depth": 1, "published_time": null, "markdown": "[](https://www.bbc.com/)\n[](https://www.bbc.com/sport)\n[](https://www.bbc.com/health)\n[](https://www.bbc.com/weather)\n[](https://www.bbc.com/newsletters)\n# US Senator Mark Kelly tells BBC he will 'seriously consider' White House run\nWatch: Mark Kelly tells BBC Newsnight he will 'seriously consider' presidential run\nUS Democratic Senator Mark Kelly has said he will \"seriously consider\" running for president in 2028 as he battles the Trump administration over a video in which he urged military personnel to refuse illegal orders.\nThe Arizona senator, who was accused of \"seditious behaviour\" by Donald Trump over the November clip, said he and his wife, Gabrielle Giffords, received \"many\" death threats after the president's comments.\n\"We get them on a weekly basis now,\" he told BBC Newsnight. \"We had to get security to protect us 24 hours a day.\"\nAsked if he was considering a White House run, the retired Navy captain said he was considering it \"because we're in some seriously challenging times\".\nThe 61-year-old, who is a former astronaut, noted he was very different from most senators. \n\"I'm one of the very few engineers, I'm the only person with a graduate degree in engineering the United States Senate. Washington has previously indicated it wants to discuss other issues as well, such as Iran's missile stockpile.\nFraming the talks as \"very important\", Trump suggested Tehran was motivated this time to negotiate. \n\"I don't think they want the consequences of not making a deal,\" Trump said, adding that Iran learned the consequences of a tough posture in talks last summer when \n\"We could have had a deal instead of sending the B-2s in to knock out their nuclear potential. Petersen Jr. and Lewis and Clark-class dry cargo ship USNS Carl Brashear sail during a photo exercise in the Arabian Sea, February 6, 2026.\n](https://ichef.bbci.co.uk/news/480/cpsprodpb/115c/live/ee771ed0-0bbb-11f1-9972-d3f265c101c6.jpg.webp)Reuters\nThe US military released an image of the Lincoln carrier strike group in the Arabian Sea in February\nIran has responded to the US build-up with its own show of force. {"url": "https://www.bbc.com/news/articles/cvgjw0kpd89o", "depth": 1, "published_time": null, "markdown": "[](https://www.bbc.com/)\n[](https://www.bbc.com/sport)\n[](https://www.bbc.com/health)\n[](https://www.bbc.com/weather)\n[](https://www.bbc.com/newsletters)\n# Are wetter winters and frequent flooding here to stay?\n![Ben Birchall/PA Wire An aerial photo showing a sea of flooded fields in Somerset and in the foreground is a hill with a church on the top](https://ichef.bbci.co.uk/news/480/cpsprodpb/68fa/live/cceab760-0718-11f1-9972-d3f265c101c6.jpg.webp)Ben Birchall/PA Wire\nFlooded fields near Burrowbridge in Somerset this month\nAreas across the UK from Cornwall to County Down have seen their wettest January on record continue with heavy rain in February.\nThe deluge the country has been experiencing in the last week has been put down to a blocked weather pattern - a high pressure system over Scandinavia is preventing the wet weather from moving away.\nThe Met Office estimates that at current levels of global warming, wet winters like 2023/24 have gone from being once in 80-year events to once in 20 - and with further warming this could become even more frequent.\nThis could have significant impacts for housing, transport and food supply. \nOne farmer in Somerset told the BBC that he was living on a \"knife edge\" as his crops were days away from rotting in the floodwater.\n## Wetter winters more common\nOn Tuesday, more than 100 locations across the UK faced flood warnings and more than 300 homes had already succumbed to the flood waters, according to the Environment Agency (EA).\nThe heavy and continuous days of rainfall follow a similar pattern to the last few years of wetter winters. Six of the ten wettest since records began nearly 250 years ago have been this century, according to the Met Office.\n![A visual graphic of the UK showing rainfall across the UK in January 2026 as a percentage of the 1991 - 2020 average. Wetter areas are shown as dark blue and cover most of the country apart from East Scotland and North East of England](https://ichef.bbci.co.uk/news/480/cpsprodpb/d22e/live/72dbcc10-069f-11f1-b5e2-dd58fc65f0f6.png.webp)\nThe UK's rainfall is strongly influenced by natural variability, but the trend towards wetter winters is in line with predictions from the UK's meteorological organisation.\nIncreased burning of fossil fuels like coal and oil over the last two centuries has released greenhouse gases like CO2 into the atmosphere, which have warmed our planet.\nIt might not sound a lot, but for every 1C temperature rise our atmosphere can hold 7% more moisture \u2013 this can create heavier rainfall.\nAt the same time sea level rise around the UK is also accelerating, due to warmer, expanding oceans and melting glaciers.\nSince 1901, sea levels have increased by 20cm, which may not sound like a lot but coupled with extreme storm events and tidal surges can exacerbate coastal flooding.\n![Line graph showing the variation in total rainfall between December and February between 1836/37 and 2024/25. The lines are in blue and the trend line is black dashed, it shows a steady increase over the period, with slight fluctuations. The headline reads \"UK winters have been getting wetter\". The source is Met Office.](https://ichef.bbci.co.uk/news/480/cpsprodpb/eba1/live/8aa13fc0-0680-11f1-b5e2-dd58fc65f0f6.png.webp)\n## How much wetter could our winters get?\nCurrently the UK experiences about seven days a year where rainfall is more than 80mm a day \u2013 considered a heavy rainfall event. If 30mm of this falls in an hour it typically triggers a flash flooding warning.\nBut, according to the Met Office, if global temperatures rise by more than 2C (above pre-industrial levels) this would increase to nine days.\nEven with current policies to reduce our emissions, global temperatures are expected to have increased by at least 2.5C by the end of the century, according to the United Nations.\n![Graphic showing two maps of the UK side by side and how rainfall will change across the country in a 1.5C and 2C warming scenario. Title reads \"Predictions for UK winter rainfall in a warmer climate\"](https://ichef.bbci.co.uk/news/480/cpsprodpb/47ed/live/44665350-0690-11f1-b5e2-dd58fc65f0f6.png.webp)\nAs well as more intense rainfall we are also seeing more rainfall clustered together, explained Prof Lizzie Kendon, head of climate projections at the Met Office. \n\"That is really important, because that can lead to successive rainfall events, [which] can lead to very saturated soils, and as we're seeing currently in the UK, that can lead to exacerbated flooding as well,\" she said. \n## What is the damage from wetter winters?\nHeavy deluges and saturated soils have the potential to cause significant damage to homes, transport infrastructure and food supply.\nIn December, the Environment Agency estimated that by 2050 one in four properties would be at risk from flooding. This is the first time the EA has considered how a warmer climate could affect flooding in the UK.\nThe East Midlands, Yorkshire and The Humber, and south-east England are particularly at risk.\nThis number, the EA said, would rise if more homes were built on floodplains. The UK government plans to build 1.5 million homes in this Parliament, and in some parts of the country \nThe same picture is seen across the transport network - currently a third of railways are at risk of flooding, which leads to cancellations and damaged infrastructure.\nIn less than 25 years this will rise to more than half of the rail network being at risk, \n![A man wearing a green jacket and checked shirt stands to the right of a grassy field which is submerged below flood water, the edge of a fence can be seen behind him. ](https://ichef.bbci.co.uk/news/480/cpsprodpb/2530/live/03d5bb90-0682-11f1-b5e2-dd58fc65f0f6.jpg.webp)\nJames Winslade said nearly all of his farm has been flooded in the recent rains\nThe National Farmers Union (NFU) wrote to MPs in November last year to lay out the severe risks the farming sector was facing.\n\"Farmers and growers experience both [drought and floods] on an annual basis, and both severely impact their ability to produce food,\" it said.\nThe wet winter of 2024 \u2013 the second wettest on record \u2013 saw the sector experience \u00a31bn losses from damaged crops.\nJames Winslade, a beef and arable farmer from Somerset, has seen more than 90% of his farm submerged in the recent rains, and said he is days away from losing his crops to rot.\n\"We can't protect everywhere. There isn't insurance for crop damage. We don't get compensation.\"\nHe said he is a third-generation farmer, and that his father and grandfather never saw this level and recurrence of flooding.\nThe UK does have an extensive network of flood defences but there is a patchwork of organisations - including farmers, water companies and charities - responsible for them.\nHalf of flood defences \u2013 around 100,000 - are maintained by the Environment Agency and 9% of those are currently below their target condition. Each defence is given a score out of five they must achieve depending on their importance. \nBut previous analysis by the BBC Shared Data Unit estimated that the defences not maintained by the EA were 45% more likely to be below target.\nAnd increased development will only exacerbate flooding \u2013 concreting over surfaces for housing or car parks prevents rain being absorbed and instead it runs off into drains and rivers which can become overwhelmed and breach defences.\nAn Environment Agency spokesperson told the BBC: \"As a result of climate change, we are seeing more flooding and extreme weather. Whilst it is sadly not possible to stop all flooding, the Environment Agency is committed to helping communities to adapt.\n\"Through the government's flood programme a further \u00a310.5bn [will be] invested in protecting 900,000 more properties by 2036.\"\n_With additional reporting from Mark Poynting, Jonah Fisher, Miho Tanaka and Tom Ingham._\n![Thin, green banner promoting the Future Earth newsletter with text saying, \u201cThe world\u2019s biggest climate news in your inbox every week\u201d. {"url": "https://www.bbc.com/news/articles/cr5l6gnen72o", "depth": 1, "published_time": null, "markdown": "[](https://www.bbc.com/)\n[](https://www.bbc.com/sport)\n[](https://www.bbc.com/health)\n[](https://www.bbc.com/weather)\n[](https://www.bbc.com/newsletters)\n# The tech bros might show more humility in Delhi \u2013 but will they make AI any safer?\n![Getty Images A man wearing a black pack takes a photo of a sign for the event, which says \"Hashtag India AI Impact Summit 2026\" in large white standalone letters. The sign sits in front of a round copper coloured building.](https://ichef.bbci.co.uk/news/480/cpsprodpb/00dd/live/384bffe0-0b75-11f1-87ad-1da650b598ae.jpg.webp)Getty Images\nThose who shout the loudest about artificial intelligence tend to be in the West, notably the US and Europe. \nSo it's significant that a gathering of powerful leaders is being held in the Global South, a region of the world that runs the risk of being left behind in the AI race. \nTech bosses, politicians, scientists, academics and campaigners are meeting at the AI Impact Summit in India this week for top-level discussions about what the world should be doing to try to marshal the AI revolution in the right direction.\nAt last year's AI Action Summit, as it was then known, \nThe various Western powers jostled for pole position in Paris, and US vice president JD Vance delivered a blistering speech in which he said America's place at the top of the pack was non-negotiable.\nI suspect there may be a more humble vibe this week in Delhi: the capital of a country which has helped to build the foundations that support this mega-powerful new tech - but is not reaping as much reward as the more affluent west.\nThere are some significant AI hubs in India, including in Bengaluru, Hyderabad and Mumbai. It has a large tech workforce, and has attracted some big infrastructure investments from the likes of Google, Nvidia and Amazon.\nAt the same time, low-paid workers there have long been carrying out the unseen and painstaking task of \nIn her book Empire of AI, the journalist Karen Hao writes about an unnamed firm in India which was contracted to do content moderation of AI-generated images: she claimed it included workers looking at horrifying ones to decide which should be blocked from being reproduced.\nAccording to the recruitment website Glassdoor, the average salary for an AI data trainer in Chennai is 480,000 rupees - less than \u00a34,000 ($5,000) per year. \nIt's an essential role, but to put this into perspective OpenAI, the creator of ChatGPT, is valued at over $500bn.\n![Getty Images A man stands in front of an orange and white sign which reads \"AI impact summit\"](https://ichef.bbci.co.uk/news/480/cpsprodpb/ce94/live/ec58e3e0-0b25-11f1-a204-b3f033b84443.jpg.webp)Getty Images\n## 'More than technology' for India\nThe 2026 International AI Safety Report notes that while \"in some countries over 50% of the population uses AI, across much of Africa, Asia, and Latin America adoption rates likely remain below 10%.\"\nThe world's biggest US AI chatbots do not work in all of India's 22 official languages - let alone the hundreds of dialects that exist within them. ChatGPT and Claude currently support around half of them. Google's Gemini supports nine.\n\"Without tech that understands and speaks these languages, millions are excluded from the digital revolution - especially in education, governance, healthcare, and banking,\" Professor Pushpak Bhattacharyya, from IIT Mumbai, \nTo counter this, India is building its own sovereign AI platforms - the Indian government calls this the AI Mission - but progress is relatively slow. \nWhile the US products - as well as Chinese ones such as DeepSeek and ByteDance - race ahead with new releases, many of India's remain in development. \nThe Indian government budget of $1.2bn for this project pales into comparison of the deep pockets of the multi-billion dollar corporations.\nBefore Christmas, an Indian government official told me, perhaps unsurprisingly, that India has little interest in AI's geopolitical power struggles. The country's focus is on harnessing the tech to drive its own growth. \n\"For India, this is about more than technology, it is about economic transformation, digital sovereignty and building capability at scale,\" said Rajan Anandan, managing director at one of India's biggest tech investors Peak XV.\n\"Within the country there is a strong sense of momentum and confidence.\"\nWatch: Why India's AI summit is key to its future in tech\nThe US, meanwhile, may find itself rather unusually forced into more of a back seat. I imagine it's not going to like that very much.\n\"The Americans will have less to say with the Summit's proposed bottom-up, Global South approach to AI governance that focuses on people, planet and progress,\" says Professor Gina Neff, an AI ethics expert from Queen Mary University London.\n\"We need governments to act together to shape a more inclusive, democratic and people-centred vision of AI in the face of unprecedented corporate power,\" argues Jeni Tennison, executive director of the think tank Connected by Data.\n\"As the world's largest 'middle power', India could make that happen,\" she adds.\nAI expert Henry Ajder agrees. \"I hope we will see pragmatic efforts to move beyond a legislative patchwork towards meaningful consensus in addressing AI harms, maliciously caused or otherwise,\" he told me.\nAmanda Brock, chief executive of tech industry body OpenUK, thinks the answer is to force the AI companies to share how their products work so that others can build their own versions, make improvements and properly scrutinise the tech.\n\"For this summit to have any real impact for the Global South, there needs to be access for all to AI and that can only be achieved by opening it up,\" she argues.\nThere has been movement in that direction, but many of the AI giants are still keeping key elements, such as what training data they use, confidential.\nSome AI experts have told me privately that they are concerned about how far down the agenda safety and responsibility appears to have slipped.\nAfter the first AI Safety Summit, held in the UK in 2023, the word \"safety\" was quietly dropped from its title. One expert told me they have decided not go to Delhi at all this week as they have little confidence in any meaningful outcomes from it.\nBritish computer scientist Professor Dame Wendy Hall is attending the Summit but told me she shares these concerns. She fears there will be \"nothing significant\" from the event about how to minimise the dangers posed by AI.\n\"It's important that we go but my expectations of anything useful coming out of it are very low,\" she said.\n![A green promotional banner with black squares and rectangles forming pixels, moving in from the right. {"url": "https://www.bbc.com/news/live/cgqg0pwkq2kt", "depth": 1, "published_time": null, "markdown": "2,752 viewingUpdated 6 minutes ago\n# Year of the Fire Horse welcomed by millions from Beijing to Bangkok\n![Two young women holding up a golden horse ornament with Lunar New Year charms in the background](https://ichef.bbci.co.uk/ace/standard/1024/cpsprodpb/229d/live/8d83cf30-0bc3-11f1-b67a-27ea04288b53.jpg)\n## Summary\n  * The Year of the Fire Horse is here - and is [being celebrated by millions across Asia from Beijing to Bangkok](https://www.bbc.com/news/live/cgqg0pwkq2kt?post=asset%3Ae9e11732-0fda-4a21-81ef-b9280b0d8b7e#post)\n  * The Lunar New Year, which coincides with the [first new moon of the lunar calendar](https://www.bbc.com/news/live/cgqg0pwkq2kt?post=asset%3Ab1ea496c-6ef9-4756-adb5-7c7554d92bbb#post), is a 15-day festival held between between 21 January and 20 February every year\n  * Food is [key to the celebrations](https://www.bbc.com/news/live/cgqg0pwkq2kt?post=asset%3A80bc0086-ceb4-4590-95f8-4e9a808fa3a7#post). In Malaysia and Singapore, yusheng - a raw fish salad - is popular but it can only be eaten after being tossed by everyone with chopsticks. In South Korea, tins of luncheon meat are popular New Year gifts\n  * In China, authorities have [launched their annual crackdown](https://www.bbc.com/news/live/cgqg0pwkq2kt?post=asset%3A72c3e79d-776b-4945-8926-b31ac287ff74#post) on what they deem antisocial content. This year that includes posts celebrating the virtue of not having children, writes our China correspondent Stephen McDonell\n  * Meanwhile in South Korea, people are continuing a recent trend of [opting for simpler rituals](https://www.bbc.com/news/live/cgqg0pwkq2kt?post=asset%3Ae05c5b27-7e1f-44f4-845f-38257c57846f#post), Seoul correspondent David Oh reports. More than 60% have said they will not hold the traditional, and elaborate, charye tables honouring ancestors this year\n\n\n## Live Reporting\nEdited by Joel Guinto and Ayeshea Perera with inputs from BBC teams in Beijing, Sydney, Seoul, Thailand, Manila, Jakarta, New York, Yangon and Singapore\n  1. **Lana Lam**  \nBBC News, Sydney\nHong Kong-born Australian Christina Yuen, 42, and her husband Frank Bui, 45, have taken their two young sons out of school today but for good reason.\n\"It's a culture day,\" Christina tells the BBC on the steps of Sydney's Town Hall where the family have just watched a traditional Chinese lion dance.\n\"It's important for them to learn about their roots and understand Lunar New Year while growing up in Australia.\"\n\"Both our families are quite traditional and we want to keep that going but it's a bit lost on their generation now. For example, we try to maintain Cantonese, but it's hard.\"\nHarrison, 4, and his big brother Oliver, 6, are in the front row when the Chinese lions make a beeline for them, so they both get to stroke the nose of the lion, which is meant to bring good luck for the new year.\nAs the dance finishes, the family are heading to a toy shop - looks like the kids' luck has already started.\n  2. **Sylvia Chang**  \nBBC Chinese, Hong Kong\nGetty Images\nDr Christian Yao, a senior lecturer at Victoria University of Wellington's School of Management in New Zealand, tells the BBC that expressions using horse imagery are likely to appear widely in workplace speeches, presentations and email subject lines in 2026.\nHe said such language in the workplace becomes a tool of \"soft governance\" whereby employees are encouraged to reflect the proactive, speedy attributes of the Fire Horse.\nMany young professionals now use animal imagery as shorthand for their working lives. In corporate and official settings, they echo the slogans about speed, momentum, and \"success at a gallop\" in the Year of the Horse. \nAt the same time, some describe themselves as \"niuma\" - literally \"ox-horse\", a slang term for an overworked beast of burden.\n\"The coexistence of these two languages\", Dr Yao says, \"captures the most typical tension in today's Chinese workplace: the need to keep up an uplifting narrative in public, while privately admitting burnout and powerlessness.\u201d\n  3. **Duc Ha**  \nBBC World Service\nThe first day of the Lunar New Year in Vietnam is traditionally dedicated to family. While some visit temples to pray for peace, many others flock to cemeteries to offer incense in memory of their dead.\nIt is a day of remembrance, where the scent of burning incense symbolises the enduring bond between the living and their ancestors. Believing that the deceased share the same needs as the living, many families include contemporary snacks alongside their traditional offerings.\nIn certain regions, the tradition extends beyond family. Visitors light incense on graves of people unrelated to them. It's a gesture of respect and sincerity.\n  4. **BBC Burmese Service**\nWe bring you now to **Myanmar** , in Yangon's Chinatown also known as **Latha**.\nBefore the 2021 military coup, this area was a famous tourist spot. The Lunar Year is still celebrated, but in a less festive way.\nPhyo Ra Tha\nA Buddhist monk walks past a row of lion dancers\nPhyo Ra Tha\nA woman selling Lunar New Year ornaments waits for customers\nPhyo Ra Tha\nPeople light incense sticks and pray at a temple\n  5. **Mustofa**  \nBBC Indonesia\nIn Indonesia, Lunar New Year is seen as a moment to reflect on centuries of cultural harmony in the country. \nThe historic Masjid Jamik Sumenep stands as a symbol of Chinese\u2013local culture. \nBuilt between 1779 and 1787 and designed by Lauw Piango, an architect of Chinese descent, the mosque blends Chinese and local architectural elements, reflecting a long tradition of mutual respect. \nHistorical records show Chinese migrants arrived in Sumenep around 1740 seeking refuge during unrest in Java. \nThey were welcomed by local rulers and communities. Many married locals, forming a Chinese-Muslim community. \nHerman Susanto, 37, a Chinese Muslim, says he still celebrates Lunar New Year or Cap Go Meh, although not on a large scale. \u201cJust as a form of respect. Many of my cousins are non-Muslim and celebrating the day,\u201d he said.\n  6. To mark the Lunar New Year, families in Singapore and Malaysia toss a fish salad for luck and prosperity. It's a fun - and messy - tradition known as lohei.\nBut a group of students at Singapore's Nanyang Technological University (NTU) have gotten into trouble for getting a little too carried away with their lohei.\nWhile some saw it as typical campus fun, many online were outraged by what they saw as food wastage and rowdy behaviour.\nThe university has since said that the students in the video will \"receive counselling\". \nNot a very auspicious start to their year of the Horse... \n_And for those who want to know what lohei looks like, watch the video below._\nChinese New Year food: How to get rich tossing fish\n  7. **Stephen McDonell**  \nChina correspondent\nThe Lunar New Year is a time to be with family and a period of reflection about the future.\nBBC News visits Beijing's Lama Temple ahead of Lunar New Year\n  8. **Eunice Yang**  \nBBC Chinese\nTransport terminals in China are crowded for the Lunar New Year holidays\nMany young Chinese are choosing to make only a brief visit home for Lunar New Year, hoping to avoid their families\u2019 intimate questions regarding topics such as relationships, career and salary.\nOne woman, Zhang Yunxi, spent New Year's Eve at a hotel near the airport, having booked a last-minute flight back from her parents' home on Tuesday morning after having a career-related argument with her father.\n\"I no longer understand the concept of family reunion,\" she tells BBC Chinese. \"This isn't a gathering; it's an annual humiliation. My dignity is thrown to the ground and rubbed against me, left to be judged at will by my closest relatives.\"\nIt's a feeling shared by many, spurring viral hashtags such as \u201creturning to my rented home during the Spring Festival\u201d, as well as related search terms like \"I can't go back\" and \"I don't want to stay at home\".\n  9. Getty Images\nIf you're just joining us - welcome. Much of East and South East Asia is halfway into the first day of the Year of the Fire Horse and BBC teams across the region have been keeping us posted on the celebrations.\nRed envelopes have been given out, noodles tossed and mandarin oranges devoured, while many others have begun journeying home to mark the festivities with family and friends.\n     * In China a record 9.5 billion cross-regional journeys are expected to take place this year as part of the [annual travel rush](https://www.bbc.com/news/live/cgqg0pwkq2kt?post=asset%3A7ab199fb-579b-49bf-b50c-d24e37faa817#post) that is widely described as the largest human migration in the world. \n     * In South Korea, a record 720,000 outbound passengers were [expected at Incheon International
