Binondo](https://www.bbc.com/news/live/cgqg0pwkq2kt?post=asset%3Ab8bff4c1-282e-4da3-8ac2-9b9722dc5a01#post), the world's oldest Chinatown, to feast on sticky rice cakes, dumplings and stir-fried noodles. \n     * Lion dances were held in several locations across Sydney, including Darling Harbour and Town Hall where spectators were [treated to four different lions](https://www.bbc.com/news/live/cgqg0pwkq2kt?post=asset%3Ac18eae40-1638-4642-8917-68d7048d00fc#post) in green, yellow, blue and pink. \n     * Celebrations have been [muted in Bangkok](https://www.bbc.com/news/live/cgqg0pwkq2kt?post=asset%3Afb4778b2-e544-4261-8f17-1f8057e70690#post), as Thailand is still observing a one-year mourning period following the passing of Queen Sirikit, the Queen Mother since last October. \n  10. **Joel Guinto**  \nLive editor\nGetty Images\nA giant sticky rice cake topped with purple yam frosting from a Lunar New Year celebration in 2016\nSearch for Manila Chinatown on YouTube and you'll get a flood of videos from content creators on food walking tours, all racking up millions of views.\nThe centuries-old riverside district of Binondo is bustling with activity on a normal day, but on the first day of the Lunar New Year, its streets and alleyways are extra crowded with locals and tourists looking for pork and chive dumplings, toasted pork buns, and beef noodles.\nThe flavours in Binondo underscore the Philippines' strong Spanish and American influences. Fresh vegetable spring rolls are sometimes perfumed and given a golden hue using annatto seeds, commonly found in South American kitchens. Steamed buns in a convenience store are filled with pizza-flavoured pork mince.\nOn the eve of the Lunar New Year, President Bongbong Marcos, told Filipinos to visit Binondo.\n\"Walk its streets. Taste its flavours. Feel its history,\" he said.\n  11. **Sylvia Chang**  \nBBC News Chinese\nGetty Images\nAlthough rooted in ancient tradition, the Chinese Zodiac is now widely embraced in modern culture, celebrated in urban spaces, online and in branding campaigns.\nIn cities across China, Lunar New Year celebrations have increasingly become large visual spectacles. Shopping districts, transport hubs and landmark buildings incorporate zodiac imagery into installations and lighting schemes.\nZodiac imagery has become a common feature in commercial packaging. Many brands have launched Year of the Horse gift boxes, while motifs of horses and the colour red have been adopted in marketing campaigns for luxury goods, skincare, and food and beverages.\n  12. **Virma Simonette**  \nReporting from Manila\nMore from Manila's Chinatown. Street vendors are hoping visitors will make room from their tight budgets for charms that will bring them good fortune in the coming year.\nEmily Ramos says her family has been selling charms in Binondo since 1996.\n\u201cMy husband started this business 30 years ago. Though sales are low this year, we are grateful that people still buy from us,\u201d she tells us.\nAlvin Bongao, a charm shop vendor says sales were better last year.\n\"I have been working here for 10 years. But it\u2019s still early. I am hopeful that there will be more customers later in the day,\u201d he says\nA balloon seller for 15 years now, Ryan Cawili says he hopes he sells out all his wares by the end of the day and that everyone has a prosperous year ahead.\n  13. Watch: How to say Happy New Year in Mandarin, Korean and Vietnamese\nWe asked our BBC colleagues to show us how to say \"happy new year\" in Mandarin, Korean and Vietnamese.\nFor people in China and Chinese communities around the world, the common greeting is **\u65b0\u5e74\u5feb\u4e50 (Xin Nian Kuai Le)** , which means \"Happy New Year\".\nThough in Cantonese, the main language in Hong Kong and parts of southern China, people usually say **\u606d\u559c\u767c\u8ca1 (Gong Hei Fat Choy)** , which means \"wishing you prosperity\".\nIn Vietnamese:**Ch\u00fac m\u1eebng n\u0103m m\u1edbi (happy new year)** , Ch\u00fac n\u0103m m\u1edbi an khang th\u1ecbnh v\u01b0\u1ee3ng (wishing you a prosperous new year), V\u1ea1n s\u1ef1 nh\u01b0 \u00fd (may all your wishes come true), or Cung h\u1ec9 ph\u00e1t t\u00e0i (wishing you prosperity).\nIn Korean: **\uc0c8\ud574 \ubcf5 \ub9ce\uc774 \ubc1b\uc73c\uc138\uc694 (sae-hae bok ma-ni ba-deu-se-yo)** , which translates to \"please receive a lot of blessings in the new year\".\n  14. **Thanyarat Doksone**  \nProducer, BBC News Bangkok\nThe celebrations in Bangkok's Chinatown this year are subdued as Thailand is still observing a one-year mourning period following the passing of Queen Sirikit, the Queen Mother since last October. \nThe local district office has announced there will not be an official celebration, but the private sector was still able to hold their own festivities.\nEven though the street decorations are not in full swing as in the past years, revellers still came out to make a wish at temples and shrines and enjoyed the atmosphere.\n  15. Getty Images\nMandarin oranges are a hallmark of Lunar New Year, usually exchanged in pairs among Chinese communities - as even numbers are believed to usher in good luck.\nThis sweet citrus fruit is favoured, among others, as it symbolises good fortune. The Mandarin word for the fruit,_ju_ , sounds like _ji_ , the word for \"lucky\"; while its Cantonese pronunciation, _gam_ , is the same as that for \"gold\".\nThe orange hue and round shape of the fruit - the circle is a sign of unity and completeness - also makes it an auspicious symbol.\n  16. **Lana Lam**  \nBBC News, Sydney\nBack in Sydney now. Red's the traditional colour in Lunar New Year celebrations but on the steps of Town Hall, it's all green, yellow, blue and pink.\nThose are the colours of the Chinese lions performing this Tuesday lunchtime for onlookers such as Martina Taylor, 48, and her daughter Olivia, 4.\n\"She loves it,\" Martina says, dressed for the occasion with an outfit of red pants and shiny red shoes.\nIt's the third lion dance the pair have seen today, having watched one in Chinatown in the morning and [another one at the Chinese Gardens](https://www.bbc.com/news/live/cgqg0pwkq2kt?post=asset%3A810c2b9f-54c2-48fa-afc8-fb0b4a6db51d#post).\nAsked which lion is her favourite, Olivia says \"pink\" and that she loves the drumming.\nAlso in the crowd is Lychelle Delannoy, 31, who's originally from the Caribbean, lives in Singapore for work and is in Sydney for a week-long holiday.\nLunar New Year festivities are huge in Singapore, she says, so she searched online this morning for where she could see a lion dance in Sydney.\n\"It's not Lunar New Year without a lion dance,\" she says, but the Sydney version has caught her by surprise.\n\"It's the first time I've seen a lion dance with four, usually it's two.\"\n  17. Today, 17 February, is the first day of the Lunar New Year. It falls on an especially busy period for religious festivals.\nAsh Wednesday, a holy day of prayer for Christians which marks the first day of Lent, takes place on 18 February.\nThat date also marks the first day of fasting for the Muslim holy month of Ramadan this year.\n  18. Lunar New Year is a time of family reunions - and heaps of festive food.\nHere are some new year dishes that are found in different Asian communities:\nGetty Images\nA plate of fish is often found on the dining tables of Chinese people during the Lunar New Year. Fish signifies abundance, thanks to a pun on its Chinese pronunciation, yu\nGetty Images\nAnother fish dish, a staple of Chinese communities in Malaysia and Singapore, is the yusheng - a raw fish salad. It can only be eaten after being tossed by everyone with chopsticks - the rowdier, the better\nGetty Images\nIn South Korea, tins of luncheon meat - introduced by the Americans during the Korean War - are popular Lunar New Year gifts, prized for their long shelf life\nGetty\nIn Vietnam, Lunar New Year is celebrated by eating sticky rice cakes wrapped in leaves. Square rice cakes, banh chung, are eaten in northern Vietnam while the cylindrical versions, called banh tet, are found in central and southern regions\n  19. **Sylvia Chang**  \nBBC News Chinese\nGetty Images\nTravelers at Hongqiao Railway Station in Shanghai on 12 February\nIn China, millions of internal migrants return home only once a year, with Lunar New Year marking the most important time for family reunions. During this year's holiday period, a record 9.5 billion cross-regional journeys are expected to take place. \nThe annual travel rush is widely described as the largest human migration in the world.\nBut while the horse may represent efficiency and quick movement, for many travellers, the festive period has highlighted strain across the transport system, with tickets in short supply, rising travel costs and long working hours for service industry staff.\nSome users are questioning on social media who is able to benefit from the promise of opportunity and forward motion linked to the Fire Horse. Young people have joked about being \"lame horses trapped in ticket buying apps\", while others describe themselves not as charging ahead but being pushed forward by circumstance.\n  20. **Suranjana Tewari**  \nAsia Business Correspondent\nGetty Images\nThe Lunar New Year is often seen as a test of the health of China\u2019s economy.\nIt is the world\u2019s largest annual human migration, as hundreds of millions travel home, filling trains and planes, and spending on gifts, food and entertainment. For consumer-facing businesses, it can be a crucial boost.\nThis year, China continues to face slow growth and low business confidence, and so officials are hoping the festive rush will help revive consumption - long seen as one of the economy\u2019s weak spots.\nBeijing has signalled that it will support the services sector, from tourism and winter sports to healthcare and elder care, in an effort to unlock demand and shift reliance away from industry and property.\nBut some analysts warn that services alone cannot guarantee sustainable growth. \nWithout stronger income growth and a more secure jobs outlook, households may remain cautious - choosing to save rather than spend.\n\n\n", "metadata": {"title": "Lunar New Year 2026 live: Year of the Fire Horse - BBC News", "description": "The Year of the Fire Horse is here - and millions are celebrating with prayer, family and food.", "keywords": null, "author": null, "og:description": "The Year of the Fire Horse is here - and millions are celebrating with prayer, family and food.", "og:site_name": "BBC News", "og:title": "Lunar New Year 2026 live: Year of the Fire Horse", "og:type": "article", "og:url": "https://www.bbc.com/news/live/cgqg0pwkq2kt", "og:locale": "en_GB", "og:image": "https://ichef.bbci.co.uk/ace/branded_news/1200/cpsprodpb/fa9b/live/c364d1a0-0bd5-11f1-884e-c36fe0e186a7.jpg", "og:image_alt": "BBC News", "twitter:description": "The Year of the Fire Horse is here - and millions are celebrating with prayer, family and food.", "twitter:title": "Lunar New Year 2026 live: Year of the Fire Horse", "twitter:url": "https://www.bbc.com/news/live/cgqg0pwkq2kt", "twitter:creator": "@BBCWorld", "twitter:site": "@BBCWorld", "twitter:image": "https://ichef.bbci.co.uk/ace/branded_news/1200/cpsprodpb/fa9b/live/c364d1a0-0bd5-11f1-884e-c36fe0e186a7.jpg", "article:author": "https://www.facebook.com/bbcnews", "depth": 1, "parent_url": "https://www.bbc.com/"}, "scraped_at": "2026-02-17T10:02:06.875783"}
{"url": "https://www.bbc.com/news/articles/cwyg4vnrwedo", "depth": 1, "published_time": null, "markdown": "[](https://www.bbc.com/)\n[](https://www.bbc.com/sport)\n[](https://www.bbc.com/health)\n[](https://www.bbc.com/weather)\n[](https://www.bbc.com/newsletters)\n# America's Next Top Model shaped reality TV, but should it ever have been made? \n![Getty Images/CBS Photo Archive Runway expert J Alexander, creator and executive producer Tyra Banks and photographer Nigel Barker on America's Next Top Model judging panel.  ](https://ichef.bbci.co.uk/news/480/cpsprodpb/69e7/live/c1bd1fe0-08c6-11f1-aef6-c5b8e6ae55fa.jpg.webp)Getty Images/CBS Photo Archive\nA new documentary sees Tyra and her fellow judges address some of the shocking moments that happened on her hit reality show\n\"I was rooting for you! We were all rooting for you! How dare you?\" \nTyra Banks' rant - angry _and_ disappointed - at America's Next Top Model (ANTM) contestant Tiffany Richardson is the stuff of reality TV legend.\nWatching the former supermodel lay into the young hopeful in front of millions of viewers still ranks as one of the show's most shocking moments, 20 years later.\nBut it is far from the only one.\nBody-shaming, race-swapping, dangerous stunts and \"humiliating\" challenges were all ingredients of the show's huge success, keeping viewers hooked.\nEven at its peak, ANTM ignited \"how far is too far\" conversations around reality TV shows.\nBut during the Covid-19 pandemic, when a new audience discovered it for the first time, they had one question.\n\"How did this ever get made?\"\n![Getty Images/CBS Photo Archive Tiffany Richardson stands in front of the judging panel of America's Next Top Model. She is wearing a black dress and has black hair which she is wearing in a ponytail.](https://ichef.bbci.co.uk/news/480/cpsprodpb/55cc/live/49b71520-08c9-11f1-aef6-c5b8e6ae55fa.jpg.webp)Getty Images/CBS Photo Archive\nIn an episode called The Girl Who Pushes Tyra Over The Edge - Tiffany Richardson was grilled by the judges\nANTM - which pitted a group of aspiring cover girls against each other in weekly photoshoot challenges - was a phenomenon.\nIt ran for 24 seasons - known as cycles - from 2003 to 2018, always attracting a new batch of hopefuls looking to win a 12-month contract with a top agency.\nAccording to Tyra's own website, it has been shown in 170 countries and regions, with many of those launching their own local spin-off versions.\nSomeone who experienced both was Sophie Sumner, who first appeared on Britain's Next Top Model when she was 18-years-old.\n![Getty Images Sophie Sumner poses alongside former America's Next Top Model judge Jay Manuel. Sophie wears a yellow knit cardigan and has blonde hair and Jay wears a tweed jacket over a blue shirt. He has short cropped hair. ](https://ichef.bbci.co.uk/news/480/cpsprodpb/8853/live/97baa7f0-08d3-11f1-bed4-8368e91e11b3.jpg.webp)Getty Images\nCycle 18 winner Sophie Sumner says the reality show would deliberately try to \"wind you up mentally\"\nThree years later, aged 21, she appeared on - and won - cycle 18 of ANTM - which saw UK models go up against US rivals in a \"British Invasion\" gimmick.\nSophie tells BBC Newsbeat the American edition of the show made the British version look like a \"fluffy little competition\" in comparison.\nShe recalls the judges picking apart her appearance - something she says did \"mirror\" the cutting modelling culture of the time, when colleagues were told to get boob jobs and expected to be \"Kate Moss skinny\".\nBut other challenges, notably making contestants \"switch ethnicities\", were just \"trash on their end,\" she says.\n\"Everyone knew better.\"\n![Getty Images/CBS Photo Archive Tyra Banks is wearing a black suit jacket and has auburn hair. She is standing in a production studio surrounded by TVs and pointing at one of them.](https://ichef.bbci.co.uk/news/480/cpsprodpb/540a/live/498ce2c0-08c7-11f1-aef6-c5b8e6ae55fa.jpg.webp)Getty Images/CBS Photo Archive\nIn a new documentary, Tyra says she wanted to make a show which reflected the diversity she felt should be in modelling\nAs a contestant on the show, Sophie says the experience felt designed to push her buttons.\nShe says the production team persuaded her into doing a photoshoot on top of the 338 m (1,100ft) tall Macau Tower during a rainstorm - even after learning she had a fear of heights.\nWhen asked whether she feels the show considered its duty of care to the models, she simply laughs.\n\"We didn't have any help on the show,\" she says.\n\"It was to get the drama. It was to get the ratings.\"\nSophie says she was \"aware\" of what producers wanted - and while the show did not propel her to stardom, she says she's \"definitely grateful\" for the platform it gave her.\nIt also gave her access to the US, where she's now based.\nHowever, Sophie feels a lot of young girls plucked from small towns in America to appear on ANTM thought a \"huge career\" would follow.\nBut, more often than not, this was not the case. \n![Getty Images/CBS Photo Archive America's Next Top Model judge J Alexander stands before a group of contestants who are all wearing bikinis and heels. J Alexander is wearing a fushia pink sarong. ](https://ichef.bbci.co.uk/news/480/cpsprodpb/eaef/live/95254950-08ce-11f1-bed4-8368e91e11b3.jpg.webp)Getty Images/CBS Photo Archive\nThe contestants faced body-shaming and extreme challenges in a bid to be crowned America's Next Top Model\nANTM's producers didn't have to contend with social media in the same way today's reality show makers do - at least in its early days.\nBut modern content creators such as Oliver Twixt have built a career on hyper-analysing every moment. \nThe YouTuber has interviewed more than 100 contestants on his channel, and tells Newsbeat he feels that by today's standards there could be questions over how some contestants were treated and portrayed.\nHowever, he doesn't feel it is fair to judge ANTM by modern standards, and says that \"hindsight is always 20/20\".\n\"I do believe it is best to give it some grace and compassion because the show was created during the time where reality TV was very new,\" Oliver says. \n\"America's Next Top Model is one of the shows that pioneered the entire genre, on top of having a black woman be the face of the show.\n\"It was a new thing and they were going to make mistakes.\"\nOthers aren't convinced that it's so clear-cut.\n![Netflix Ebony Haith \\(bottom left\\) smiles at a camera alongside fellow cycle one contestants. She is wearing a black cardigan over a white top and has a shaved head. ](https://ichef.bbci.co.uk/news/480/cpsprodpb/8719/live/62f6d5a0-075e-11f1-b7e1-afb6d0884c18.jpg.webp)Netflix\nModel Ebony (bottom left) received comments about her skin texture and personality from the judges which she is \"still healing\" from\nEbony Haith, who starred in the first cycle of ANTM in 2003, is one of the stories explored in an upcoming Netflix documentary which delves into some of the show's most standout controversies.\nThe model from New York, who was 23 at the time, was told by the judges that her skin texture looked \"ashy\" and she was described as \"too harsh\". \nEbony tells Newsbeat that she is \"still healing\" from the experience 23 years later. \n\"It seemed as though my race, my skin complexion, my hairstyle became so loud that I couldn't be this innocent girl having this fun experience,\" she says.\nOver the years, viewers have argued the views and comments shared on the show were a product of their time.\nBut Ebony disagrees, and says plenty of people at the time were shocked by her treatment.\n\"The fact that an American show decided to take a black girl from New York named Ebony and to tell the world her skin texture was too rough,\" she says.\n\"It was amazing at how many people were embarrassed at the way I was treated on the show.\"\nEbony says those conversations have never stopped.\n\"I literally today will go on a bus. People will see me either put their heads down or they will start the conversation,\" she says.\nShe says that no-one from production has called her since to discuss or apologise for what happened and she has not seen Tyra since the series was broadcast. \n![Getty Images/CBS Photo Archive Judge Jay Manuel talks to contestant who are sat down at a table. Jay is wearing a navy short-sleeved \"I Love LA\" top. He has short, bleach bonde hair. ](https://ichef.bbci.co.uk/news/480/cpsprodpb/0f37/live/80c008e0-08f3-11f1-94dc-adc7436c6c75.jpg.webp)Getty Images/CBS Photo Archive\nThe reality show gained a new audience during the Covid-19 pandemic after it ended in 2018 \nThe new Netflix documentary, Reality Check: Inside America's Next Top Model, will finally see Tyra and her fellow judges - Jay Manuel, J Alexander and Nigel Barker - address some of these shocking moments. \nIn it, Tyra says she created ANTM after facing adversity in the industry as a black woman.\nShe says she set out to make a show which reflected the diversity she felt that could, and should, be seen in modelling.\nFormer contestant Sophie does believe that, \"deep down\", the host did want to champion that cause. \nBut she feels Tyra \"didn't know how to do it\" and \"getting ratings and making money probably became the overall goal\".\n\"I'm sure that Tyra says that she got swept up in a machine and she had to get the ratings and she had so much pressure,\" she says.\n\"Like, I can hear it.\"\nSophie, who now works as a presenter and producer, believes the fashion industry has changed for the better, hailing the #MeToo movement as the \"biggest defining thing\", rather than Tyra. \n\"Could she have been the person to make the change? Of course, but other people then did that instead,\" she says.\n![Getty Images Tyra Banks looks at the camera as she poses with the cast members from America's Next Top Model. She is wearing a black top and has light brown hair swept to the side. ](https://ichef.bbci.co.uk/news/480/cpsprodpb/4fdc/live/5e0d2140-08ce-11f1-bed4-8368e91e11b3.jpg.webp)Getty Images\nIn a new Netflix documentary, Tyra opens up about her rant against contestant Tiffany Richardson\nIn the three-part docuseries, Tyra admits that certain moments, such as her face-off with Tiffany in cycle four, \"went too far\".\nDocumentary directors Daniel Sivan and Mor Loushy tell Newsbeat the interview segments with the ANTM's creator were taken from a four-hour recording session.\nThey say neither Tyra nor any of the show's producers had any say over the final edit of the documentary or the editorial direction of the project.\n\"I think once she decided to participate, she came to speak up,\" Mor says. \n\"After Covid, so much about the show was on TikTok, media. \n\"She also wanted to speak up and give her side of the story.\"\nNot everyone agrees that ANTM needs to be reassessed through a 2026 lens, but Daniel believes viewers have a \"responsibility\" to critique the past from a new perspective. \nBut he's a bit less certain about which side of the argument he'd land on.\n\"I think this show did a lot of good to open up the fashion world to different ethnicities, different body types, but it also did a lot of wrong,\" he says. \n\"And I think looking at both sides of this coin through our lens is the only way to critique it.\"\nBBC has approached Tyra Banks, the show's developers and The CW Network for comment. \n  * _Reality Check: Inside America's Next Top Model launches on Netflix on 16 February._\n\n\n![A footer logo for BBC Newsbeat. It has the BBC logo and the word Newsbeat in white over a colorful background of violet, purple and orange shapes. At the bottom a black square reading \"Listen on Sounds\" is visible.](https://ichef.bbci.co.uk/news/480/cpsprodpb/8366/live/74a706f0-15d7-11ef-9b12-1ba8f95c4917.jpg)\nListen to Newsbeat Other foreign governments have also refused to repatriate their citizens.\nReuters news agency quoted a co-director of Roj camp as saying that the 34 Australians had been handed over to members of their families who had travelled to Syria for their release, and had boarded minibuses to travel to Damascus with a military escort.\nAustralian media reported that the group were intending to continue their journey to Beirut, where they hoped to be issued passports. \nThe Australian government has repeatedly said it would not repatriate people from Syria and said those who returned who had committed crimes would be \"met with the full force of the law\".\nBut it has previously taken the position that it is obliged to issue passports to citizens who present themselves at an Australian embassy.\nRoj is home to more than 2,000 others from 40 different nationalities - mainly women and children - who have been held there since IS was driven from its final foothold in Syria in 2019.\nAmong them is thought to be Shamima Begum, who was stripped of her British citizenship in 2019 on national security grounds.\nBBC's Orla Guerin reported from Roj camp last month\nSpeaking to \nShe added that children were growing up surrounded by \"dangerous ideas and ideologies\" in the camp.\n\"We brought families of ISIS-affiliated women and children here in 2017 and now it's 2026,\" she said. \"The more time passes, the more complicated the situation becomes.\"\nThe Australian government said in a statement: \"Our security agencies have been monitoring - and continue to monitor - the situation in Syria to ensure they are prepared for any Australians seeking to return to Australia\".\n\"People in this cohort need to know that if they have committed a crime and if they return to of Australians, consisting of four women and 13 children, \nLast month the UK home secretary said she would \nThe European Court of Human Rights (ECHR) has called for an investigation, but the {"url": "https://www.bbc.com/news/articles/cv2grggdzweo", "depth": 1, "published_time": null, "markdown": "[](https://www.bbc.com/)\n[](https://www.bbc.com/sport)\n[](https://www.bbc.com/health)\n[](https://www.bbc.com/weather)\n[](https://www.bbc.com/newsletters)\n# How Robert Duvall became a Hollywood great\n![CBS via Getty Images  Robert Duvall as Tom Hagen in The Godfather](https://ichef.bbci.co.uk/news/480/cpsprodpb/2f65/live/064354a0-0b6e-11f1-8cee-7bb63a423dd9.jpg.webp)CBS via Getty Images\nDuvall played mafia consigliere Tom Hagen in The Godfather and The Godfather Part II \nRobert Duvall was a distinguished and prolific screen actor who lent a brooding intensity and grizzled authority to seven decades of American film-making.\nNominated for Academy Awards on seven occasions, he won best actor for his role as a troubled country singer in 1983's Tender Mercies.\nHis many other roles included a mafia consigliere in The Godfather, a bombastic army officer in Apocalypse Now, and a Texas Ranger-turned-cattle driver in Lonesome Dove.\nMore character actor than leading man, he could be relied upon to inject a feisty, fiery machismo and a cantankerous contrariness into the most mainstream Hollywood offering.\nBorn Robert Selden Duvall in January 1931 in San Diego, California, Duvall was a self-proclaimed \"navy brat\" due to his father's life-long career in the United States Navy.\nHis father expected him to follow him into the Naval Academy at Annapolis, Maryland. Yet his son instead served two years in the army following his 1953 college graduation.\n![Getty Images Robert Duvall with Dustin Hoffman in 2014](https://ichef.bbci.co.uk/news/480/cpsprodpb/5d7d/live/098e1d60-0b6f-11f1-8cee-7bb63a423dd9.jpg.webp)Getty Images\nDuvall and Dustin Hoffman, pictured in 2014, studied acting together in the 1950s\nDuvall subsequently moved to New York to study acting, working as a postal clerk to make ends meet. His classmates included Dustin Hoffman and Gene Hackman, who both became lifelong friends.\n\"A friend is someone who many years ago offered you his last $300 when you broke your pelvis,\" he would later recall. \"A friend is Gene Hackman.\"\nDuvall began acting professionally at the Gateway Playhouse, a summer theatre in Long Island, where he starred in plays by Arthur Miller, Tennessee Williams and others.\nHis performance in Horton Foote's one-act play The Midnight Caller led the playwright to recommend him for the role of Boo Radley in the 1962 film version of To Kill a Mockingbird.\nBy that time, Duvall had already appeared on television in such shows as Naked City, Alfred Hitchcock Presents and Armstrong Circle Theatre.\nTo Kill a Mockingbird, which Foote adapted from Harper Lee's novel, was the actor's first film role and led to appearances in such films as The Chase, Bullitt and True Grit.\n![Getty Images Robert Duvall in To Kill a Mockingbird](https://ichef.bbci.co.uk/news/480/cpsprodpb/d4ee/live/115737c0-0b6f-11f1-8cee-7bb63a423dd9.jpg.webp)Getty Images\nDuvall played the reclusive Boo Radley in To Kill a Mockingbird\nThe Rain People, released in 1969, marked his first collaboration with director Francis Ford Coppola, with whom he would go on to work in the first two Godfather films.\nOther significant roles around this time included the highly strung Major Burns in Robert Altman's M*A*S*H and THX 1138, the eponymous protagonist of George Lucas's dystopian 1971 sci-fi debut.\nDuvall's performance as The Godfather's Tom Hagen, the shrewd chief advisor to Marlon Brando's ageing Don Vito Corleone, earned him his first Academy Award nomination in 1973.\nHe reprised the role in 1974's The Godfather Part II, by which time he had collaborated with Foote again in the 1972 film of his play Tomorrow. Network, from 1976, cast him as a venal television producer.\nMeanwhile, there were appearances with Michael Caine in The Eagle Has Landed, Laurence Olivier in The Betsy, and Muhammad Ali in the boxer's biopic The Greatest.\n![Getty Images Robert Duvall \\(centre\\) on the Apocalypse Now set](https://ichef.bbci.co.uk/news/480/cpsprodpb/ae83/live/ec71dff0-0b6e-11f1-8cee-7bb63a423dd9.jpg.webp)Getty Images\nRobert Duvall (centre) on the Apocalypse Now set\nIn 1979, Duvall gave perhaps his most memorable performance as Lieutenant Colonel Kilgore in Coppola's Vietnam war epic Apocalypse Now.\n\"I love the smell of napalm in the morning,\" his character famously declared after leading a deadly helicopter attack on a Vietnamese village.\nThe actor was shown to no less domineering effect in The Great Santini, playing a frustrated Marine pilot who hectors and browbeats his teenage children.\nThe scene in which he torments his son by bouncing a basketball against his head was spoofed by Mike Myers' Dr Evil in the second Austin Powers film.\nDuvall's work in Apocalypse Now and The Great Santini saw him nominated for Oscars in consecutive years. The former also earned him a Bafta and the first of four Golden Globe awards.\nYet it was his role as alcoholic singer Mac Sledge in Tender Mercies that finally saw him land an Oscar, beating an all-British line-up of fellow nominees that included Caine and Albert Finney.\nWatch: Robert Duvall honoured in Hollywood 'hand and footprint ceremony' in 2011\nDuvall's other Oscar nominations came for his performances in 1997's The Apostle, 1998 legal drama A Civil Action and 2014's The Judge.\nIn The Apostle, which he also wrote and directed, the actor played an evangelical preacher who begins a new life in Louisiana after committing a crime.\nIn real life, Duvall attended church regularly during his childhood but was reluctant to discuss his faith, merely disclosing he had \"always been a believer\".\nThe staunch Republican was less reticent about his political persuasions and was a guest at President George W Bush's inauguration in 2001.\nDuvall's other many screen roles included an LA police office in Colors, an astronaut in Deep Impact, and a Nascar crew chief in Days of Thunder alongside Tom Cruise.\nHe was reunited with Cruise in 2012's Jack Reacher, in which Duvall played a former soldier turned gun shop owner.\nYet Duvall often looked happiest when riding a horse, be it in the acclaimed TV mini-series Lonesome Dove or in the Kevin Costner Western Open Range.\n\"I think the Western kind of defines us,\" \n![Getty Images Robert Duvall at the 1984 Academy Awards](https://ichef.bbci.co.uk/news/480/cpsprodpb/552b/live/e0775360-355a-11ef-bbe0-29f79e992ddd.jpg)Getty Images\nDuvall was named best actor at the 1984 Academy Awards\nHis other passions included football, the tango and the Argentine capital Buenos Aires, a city he professed to love \"more than any place else\".\n\"A young actor once asked me 'What do you do between jobs?'\" he once recalled. \"I said, 'Hobbies, hobbies and more hobbies'. It keeps you off dope.\"\nDuvall was married and divorced three times and is survived by his fourth wife, the Argentine actress Luciana Pedraza. He did not father any children, \n[Film](https://www.bbc.com/news/topics/cg41ylwvgjyt)\n* * *\nRelated\n## [Actor Robert Duvall, who starred in The Godfather, dies aged 95](https://www.bbc.com/news/articles/ce3g41z58vno)\n## [Enigmatic aristocrat was ahead of his time, says It's a Sin star](https://www.bbc.com/news/articles/cj981k4ee7no)\n## [Lord of the Rings star cast as Lennon's wife in Beatles biopic](https://www.bbc.com/news/articles/c8egww2l2g9o)\n* * *\n", "metadata": {"title": "How Robert Duvall became a Hollywood great", "description": "A look at the life of the prolific actor whose many films included Apocalypse Now and The Godfather.", "keywords": null, "author": null, "og:title": "How Robert Duvall became a Hollywood great", "og:description": "A look at the life of the prolific actor whose many films included Apocalypse Now and The Godfather.", "og:image": "https://ichef.bbci.co.uk/news/1024/branded_news/2f65/live/064354a0-0b6e-11f1-8cee-7bb63a423dd9.jpg", "og:image:width": "1024", "og:image:height": "576", "og:image:alt": "Robert Duvall as Tom Hagen in The Godfather", "twitter:title": "How Robert Duvall became a Hollywood great", "twitter:description": "A look at the life of the prolific actor whose many films included Apocalypse Now and The Godfather.", "twitter:image:src": "https://ichef.bbci.co.uk/news/1024/branded_news/2f65/live/064354a0-0b6e-11f1-8cee-7bb63a423dd9.jpg", "twitter:card": "summary_large_image", "twitter:image:alt": "Robert Duvall as Tom Hagen in The Godfather", "depth": 1, "parent_url": "https://www.bbc.com/"}, "scraped_at": "2026-02-17T10:02:06.876066"}
{"url": "https://www.bbc.com/news/articles/crrnyg9e91jo", "depth": 1, "published_time": null, "markdown": "[](https://www.bbc.com/)\n[](https://www.bbc.com/sport)\n[](https://www.bbc.com/health)\n[](https://www.bbc.com/weather)\n[](https://www.bbc.com/newsletters)\n# The science of soulmates: Is there someone out there exactly right for you?\n![BBC A treated image of a glass beaker with two hearts inside](https://ichef.bbci.co.uk/news/480/cpsprodpb/dbbb/live/a942e2b0-01b4-11f1-b7e1-afb6d0884c18.jpg.webp)BBC\n**On Valentine's Day, there's the temptation to believe that somewhere out there is \"The One\": a soulmate, a perfect match, the person you were meant to be with.**\nAcross history, humans have always been drawn to the idea that love isn't random. In ancient Greece, Plato imagined that we were once whole beings with four arms, four legs and two faces, so radiant that Zeus split us in two; ever since, each half has roamed the earth searching for its missing other, a myth that gives the modern soulmate its poetic pedigree and the promise that somewhere, someone will finally make us feel complete.\nIn the Middle Ages, troubadours and Arthurian tales recast that longing as \"courtly love\", a fierce, often forbidden devotion like Lancelot's for Guinevere, in which a knight proved his worth through self-sacrifice for a beloved he might never openly declare.\n![Getty Images Two treated images of a drawing of Plato and a close up image of a statue of Zeus.](https://ichef.bbci.co.uk/news/480/cpsprodpb/8434/live/5e35de30-05c9-11f1-b5e2-dd58fc65f0f6.jpg.webp)Getty Images\nPlato (left) imagined humans were once whole, with four arms, four legs and two faces, before Zeus (right) split them, leaving each half searching for its other\nBy the Renaissance, writers such as Shakespeare were talking of \"star-crossed lovers\", couples bound together by an overwhelming connection yet pulled apart by family, fortune or fate, as if the universe itself both wrote their love story and barred them from a happy ending.\nIn more recent times, Hollywood and romance novels have sold us fairy tale love stories.\nBut what does the latest science say about soulmates? Is there a particular special someone out there for us?\n## How we fall for 'The One'\nViren Swami, Professor of Social Psychology at Anglia Ruskin University (ARU), in Cambridge, has traced our contemporary European understanding of romantic love back to medieval Europe and those stories of Camelot, Lancelot, Guinevere and the chivalry of the knights of the round table that swept across the continent.\n\"These stories first pushed the idea that you should choose one other individual as your companion and that companion is for life,\" he says.\n\"Before that, in much of Europe, you could love as many people as you like, and love was fluid, and it was often not about sex.\"\nOver time, as people are uprooted from their agricultural communities as industrialisation tears apart people's familiar attachments, individuals become \"alienated\", he says. \"They start looking for one other individual to save them, to save them from the wretchedness of their lives.\"\n![Getty Images A drawing of Lancelot and Guinevere](https://ichef.bbci.co.uk/news/480/cpsprodpb/667e/live/6922a6c0-05c9-11f1-b5e2-dd58fc65f0f6.jpg.webp)Getty Images\nViren Swami believes today's ideas about romantic love can be traced back to medieval European stories like Lancelot and Guinevere\nToday's dating apps turn that story into an algorithm, which Swami calls \"relation-shopping\". The search for a soulmate turns into the opposite of what they are looking for: \"For many people, that's a really soulless experience.\n\"You're shopping for a partner\u2026 going through possibly dozens of people on the dating app until you get to a point where you go\u2026 I need to stop,\" he says.\n## The One\nJason Carroll, Professor of Marriage and Family Studies at the US Brigham Young University, based in Provo, Utah, is sympathetic to the longing for \"The One\".\n\"We are attachment-based creatures,\" he says. \"We desire that bond.\" But in his lectures, he tells students they need to leave the idea of a soulmate, without giving up their desire for The One.\nIt sounds like a contradiction, but for Carroll, it's the difference between destiny and graft.\n\"A soulmate is just simply found. It's already pre\u2011made. But a one and only is something two people carve out together over years of adapting, apologising, and occasionally gritting their teeth,\" he says.\n## Soulmate trap\nCarroll's argument draws on decades of research, which he put together in his report, The Soulmate Trap, much of which distinguishes between what psychologists call \"destiny beliefs\" - the idea that the right relationship should feel effortless - and \"growth beliefs\", which focus on what partners can do to make things work.\nIn a widely cited series of studies in the late 1990s and early 2000s led by Professor C. Raymond Knee at the University of Houston, researchers found that people who believed relationships were \"meant to be\" were far more likely to doubt their commitment after conflict. Those with more growth-minded views tended to stay more committed, even on days when they argued.\nThose with growth-based views, Carroll argues, still want something special, but expect rough patches. \"They ask\u2026 what can they do to make their relationship better, have improvement and have growth?\"\n![Getty Images A silhouette of a romantic couple](https://ichef.bbci.co.uk/news/480/cpsprodpb/d880/live/6f422fd0-05c9-11f1-9972-d3f265c101c6.jpg.webp)Getty Images\nResearch suggests people with \"growth\" beliefs about relationships still want something special, but expect challenges along the way\nIn his view, the soulmate belief is a trap - not the romance itself, but the expectation that love should never be hard. The most \"soulful\" part of a long relationship, he says, is not a cinematic charge, but having \"front-row seats not only for each other's strengths, but... [their] challenges and weaknesses\".\n\"That's a pretty sacred space,\" he says. \"We only know those things because they've let us be there.\"\nFor Carroll, when love is treated as fate, people become less willing to do the unshowy work that actually keeps love alive. Carroll says the soulmate trap makes it much harder when a relationship hits its first serious snag.\n\"The first time there's any type of struggle, the immediate thought is, 'well, I thought you were my soulmate. But maybe you're not, because soulmates aren't supposed to deal with things',\" he says. \"But if relationships are going to go long term, it's never just going to be a downhill run.\"\n## Spark or trauma?\nVicki Pavitt, a London-based love coach, often helps people who thought they'd found their soulmate, only to discover that the fairy tale came with emotional manipulation, flakiness, and a constant sense of anxiety.\n\"When there is a lot of chemistry and the spark, I think that can sometimes be about opening old unhealthy patterns, like old wounds\", she says.\n\"A person who is inconsistent or plays a bit hot and cold can make you feel 'I can't wait to see them again', but what's really happening is they're giving you so much anxiety and that it has you wanting more\".\n![Vicki Pavitt A shot of Vicki Pavitt wearing a white suit with a black top.](https://ichef.bbci.co.uk/news/480/cpsprodpb/aa5c/live/75cd5fd0-0675-11f1-b7e1-afb6d0884c18.jpg.webp)Vicki Pavitt\nLove coach Vicki Pavitt often works with people who thought they'd found a soulmate, only to find the relationship brought anxiety\nPavitt says what we feel to be destiny may be a pull from our nervous system recognising something that hurt us before and trying to fix it, a pattern therapists call a trauma bond.\nThis bond can seem like love, she says, and leads to people magnetically drawn into unhealthy dynamics because they are familiar, not because they are the perfect match.\nOne study often cited is by Canadian psychologists Donald Dutton and Susan Painter. In research published in 1993 while they were at the University of British Columbia, they followed 75 women after they had left abusive partners.\nThe team measured how strongly the women still felt attached to their exes and compared this with what their relationships had been like.\nThey found the strongest bonds were not in women who had consistently been abused, but in those whose partners alternated between charm and cruelty.\n![Getty Images Two people tie a red ribbon to their little fingers](https://ichef.bbci.co.uk/news/480/cpsprodpb/8945/live/b4da1f10-0675-11f1-9972-d3f265c101c6.jpg.webp)Getty Images\nPavitt says what feels like destiny can sometimes be a trauma bond\nDutton and Painter argue that this trauma bond helps explain why people can feel magnetised back to relationships that are objectively bad for them \u2013 because the mix of danger and affection is familiar, not because it is healthy.\nIt is that distinction Pavitt tries to surface in coaching: \"It's about discerning whether the chemistry you feel is showing me this person's compatible with me or if it is a familiar sense of anxiety.\n\"In my language, I never talk about soulmates,\" she says. \"I don't personally believe that there is one person for everybody... but I do believe that we become \"The One\" for someone.\"\n## Real chemistry\nIf ruling out the existence of a soulmate sounds unromantic, the biology of attraction points in the same direction.\nHormonal contraceptives may subtly reshape how partners feel about each other. Research suggests that pills which flatten the natural ebb and flow of fertility can dampen shifts in attraction that typically occur across the menstrual cycle, potentially altering initial mate choice.\n![PA Wire A pharmacist stocks shelves at a chemist.](https://ichef.bbci.co.uk/news/480/cpsprodpb/63f1/live/3ba572e0-05c9-11f1-9972-d3f265c101c6.jpg.webp)PA Wire\nSome research suggests hormonal contraception may subtly influence attraction in partners\nOne large study of 365 hetrosexual couples found that women's sexual satisfaction was higher when their current contraceptive status matched what it was when they first chose their partner, hinting that changes in pill use can change how a partner is experienced. These effects are small but could help explain some couples' puzzling shifts in chemistry over time.\nIf hormones and pills can tilt who feels like \"The One\", then it becomes harder to argue there is a single, pre\u2011ordained match \u2013 which is where the mathematicians come in.\n## The one but not the only\nPsychology and biology offer one way of thinking about \"The One\", but mathematics puts forward another.\nDr Greg Leo, an economist at Vanderbilt University in Nashville, Tennessee, has come up with a compatibility algorithm. It finds that not only might you have a \"One\" you have lots of \"Ones\".\nIn his Matching Soulmates paper in the journal of Public Economic Theory, everyone is in a computer simulated dating pool, where thousands of digitally created daters rank each other. His algorithm picks \"first\u2011order soulmates\": pairs who choose each other in a stable matching. It removes them, and runs it again with those left, and you get second\u2011order soulmates, and so on.\nMore from InDepth\nIn his simulations, it was extremely rare for someone to have their mutual first picks; but many people had those that were second or third picks. In this scenario a couple counts as happy if each is near the top of the other's list and neither can find someone they and that other person would both prefer more.\nIt may only be number crunching, but the love algorithm tells us that there are many viable partners, not just The One.\n## Sweat the small stuff\nSo how can a couple co-create their Ones?\nJacqui Gabb, Professor of Sociology and Intimacy at The Open University, assessed this in her Enduring Love project, published in the journal Sociology in 2015.\nIt surveyed around 5,000 people and then followed 50 couples in forensic, sometimes intrusive detail, combining statistics with diaries, interviews and \"emotion maps\" of what happened in the home.\nWhen she asked people what made them feel appreciated, it wasn't sunset proposals or surprise trips to Paris.\nIt was \"surprise gifts, thoughtful gestures and the kindness of a cup of tea in bed\". Warming the car on a cold morning. Picking wildflowers and putting them in a vase. Sharing a private smile at a party.\nQuantitatively, what she describes as these \"everyday attentive acts\" turned out to be much more powerful than grand romantic gestures.\nIn her survey, 22% of mothers and 20% of childless women picked such small gestures as one of the top two things that made them feel valued \u2013 more than big nights out or expensive presents.\nRelationship satisfaction in the data wasn't primarily about money or romance; it was about \"intimate couple knowledge\" and its expression in daily life.\nIn one young couple's diary given to them for the project, Sumaira describes her partner coming home, the dinner she has cooked, the hug in the hallway, the two of them eating together at the table.\n\"It's perfect,\" she writes in her research diary. \"Just us and food. What more could I want?\"\nThen there is a spontaneous dance in the living room, a walk in long grass where she gets scared of the dark, and a photo her partner loves so much he makes it the background on his phone.\nIt reads like a lovely every day tale, not a fairy tale: no glass slippers, but wellies.\nYet Gabb points out that woven through the sweetness are money worries, family obligations and a history of depression that the couple are learning to manage together.\n\"The soulmate feeling here doesn't float above life; it is made, inch by inch, by life, in the way the pair meet those pressures,\" she says.\n## Valentine's Dinner\nThe science doesn't steal the romance \u2013 rather it's there to help it bloom, in good times and bad, according to Carroll.\n\"I'm pretty comfortable with the aspiration to be in a unique special relationship as long as we remember it needs to be created,\" he says.\nPavitt thinks \"it's fine, helpful, even to have faith that your person is out there, so long as you know there are many people that you could form a really great connection with and stop expecting anyone to be perfect\".\nAs for soulmates, the science points to a paradox. The people who end up with relationships that feel uniquely \"meant to be\" are often the ones who stopped waiting for fate, turned towards the flawed person in front of them and said, in effect: shall we make something of this?\n_Additional reporting by Florence Freeman_\n_Top image credit: Getty Images_\n![Thin, lobster red banner with white text saying \u2018InDepth newsletter\u2019. {"url": "https://www.bbc.com/news/articles/cvg38x13x5yo", "depth": 1, "published_time": null, "markdown": "[](https://www.bbc.com/)\n[](https://www.bbc.com/sport)\n[](https://www.bbc.com/health)\n[](https://www.bbc.com/weather)\n[](https://www.bbc.com/newsletters)\n# We will do battle with AI chatbots as we did with Grok, says Starmer\n![Getty Images A teenage girl with long-brown hair leans against her bed as she sits on the floor and looks at her phone](https://ichef.bbci.co.uk/news/480/cpsprodpb/094a/live/520e98d0-0ab6-11f1-afbc-01c60fb356b3.jpg.webp)Getty Images\nSir Keir Starmer has pledged to respond more quickly to close loopholes in laws designed to protect children online. \nThe prime minister said while the government had \"won\" the \"battle\" with X - after \nAs well as tightening legislation around chatbots, government proposals also included a measure requiring tech giants to preserve all of the data on a child's phone if they die.\nOpponents accused the government of \"inaction\", and have called for Parliament to be given a vote on the social media ban for children.\nSir Keir said no online platform will get a \"free pass\" over the issue of children's online safety as he promised to \"crack down on the addictive elements of social media\".\nThe main new proposals are:\n  * Coroners would have to notify Ofcom of the death of every child aged 5-18 so they can ensure tech companies do not delete their data, in case it is relevant to the way they died \n  * Artificial intelligence (AI) chatbots would be included in the Online Safety Act\n  * Getting new legislation around technology through much quicker\n\n\nThe government reiterated its plans to launch \nThe government has now said it wants to create new legal powers so it can take \"immediate action\" following the consultation. \nTechnology Secretary Liz Kendall told the BBC's Today programme while she felt a proper consultation was essential, the government should still be able to act swiftly once it had come to a decision.\n\"The first time the Online Safety Act was discussed I think was in a green paper in 2017 - that process is too long, because the technology is changing so quickly,\" she said.\n\"MPs have a Finance Bill every year with the budget - I think we need to think like that with technology because it is changing so fast.\"\nThe Online Safety Act - \nOther measures being considered include preventing children from using virtual private networks (VPNs) to get around age checks, and amending the law so chatbots have to protect users from illegal content.\nIn an \n\"And in that evolution, it has become something that is quietly harming our children.\"\nThe prime minister added he wanted to \"crack down on the addictive elements of social media, stop the auto-play, the never-ending scrolling, that keeps our children hooked on their screens for hours, and stop kids getting around age limits.\n\"And if that means a fight with the big social media companies, then bring it on,\" he said.\n'I never wanted another parent to ever be in my position' - Ellen Roome\nThere are also plans to change rules on how children's social media data is stored under the Crime and Policing Bill, after \nIn 2022, Ellen Roome's son Jools died aged 14. She believes he was trying an internet challenge which went wrong, but has never been able to access his data to prove it. \nUnder current rules, a child's data must be requested from tech companies within 12 months of their death by either a coroner or the police.\nBut bereaved parents say often by the time that happens, this record of what their child has been doing online has already been deleted.\nNew rules would mean it has to be preserved within five days if it might be relevant to the cause of death, which hopefully means more families will get some answers.\n\"This going forward will help other bereaved families,\" Roome said. \"What we now need to do is stop the harm happening in the first place. This is really relevant when a child dies, but we need to stop them dying in the first place.\"\nAndy Burrows, chief executive of the Molly Rose Foundation, a UK-based suicide prevention charity, said he strongly welcomed the government's ambition to move quickly but that added the prime minister must go further.\n\"Sir Keir Starmer should commit to a new Online Safety Act that strengthens regulation and makes clear that product safety and children's wellbeing is the cost of doing business in the UK,\" he said.\nChris Sherwood, the chief executive of NSPCC, said it was important to put children's voices at the centre of the debate.\n\"After their insights have been overlooked in discussions so far, their experiences must now help guide the decisions made in the months ahead,\" he said.\nLord Nash, a Conservative former minister who has been campaigning in the Lords for tighter social media controls for children, welcomed the adoption of Jools' Law by the government.\nBut, he said to avoid \"future tragedies\", the \"government must raise the age limit to 16 for the most harmful platforms now\". \nShadow education secretary Laura Trott described the consultation announcement as \"inaction\", and said Britain was \"lagging behind. \"I am clear that we should stop under-16s accessing these platforms,\" she said.\nLiberal Democrat spokeswoman Munira Wilson, too, accused the government of continuing to \"kick the can down the road\" and called for a \"much clearer, firm timeline\" on when action will be taken.\nTechnology Secretary Liz Kendall said the government was \"determined to give children the childhood they deserve and to prepare them for the future at time of rapid technological change\" and would \"not wait\" to take the action families needed. \n_Additional reporting by Laura Cress._\n![A green promotional banner with black squares and rectangles forming pixels, moving in from the right. {"url": "https://www.bbc.com/news/articles/cvgveqzld59o", "depth": 1, "published_time": null, "markdown": "[](https://www.bbc.com/)\n[](https://www.bbc.com/sport)\n[](https://www.bbc.com/health)\n[](https://www.bbc.com/weather)\n[](https://www.bbc.com/newsletters)\n# Billy Steinberg, Madonna's Like a Virgin songwriter, dies at 75\n![Getty Images Billy Steinberg during the 2008 ASCAP \"I Create Music\" EXPO held at the Renaissance Hotel on April 11, 2008 in Los Angeles, California](https://ichef.bbci.co.uk/news/480/cpsprodpb/d7a4/live/b3178910-0bbb-11f1-ab66-0b9a69be6bdd.jpg.webp)Getty Images\nGrammy-winning US songwriter Billy Steinberg, a key figure behind several chart-topping hits including Madonna's Like a Virgin, has died aged 75.\nSteinberg's family described him as a \"visionary lyricist, devoted husband, loving father, and one of the most influential songwriters of his era\".\nHis songs were performed by Whitney Houston, Roy Orbison, Celine Dion, Demi Lovato, and The Bangles.\nSteinberg's lawyer confirmed to the BBC he had died in Los Angeles following a cancer diagnosis.\nReflecting on his work, Steinberg's family said \"his lyrics often began as deeply personal reflections, transformed into anthems in which millions found themselves\".\n\"Billy Steinberg's life was a testament to the enduring power of a well-written song - and to the idea that honesty, when set to music, can outlive us all,\" the family's statement read.\nHis family noted that he received multiple industry accolades throughout his four-decade career, including a Grammy Award for his work on Celine Dion's 1996 album Falling Into You.\n\"Yet those closest to him knew that what mattered most was not recognition, but connection - the magic of hearing a crowd sing back something that once lived only in his notebook.\"\nBorn William Steinberg, the Californian songwriter began collaborating with vocalist and songwriter Tom Kelly in the early 1980s. \nThe duo that went on to contribute to several chart-topping singles and lead a short-lived rock band called i-Ten.\nSteinberg and Kelly's breakthrough occurred with Madonna's Like a Virgin in 1984, which reached number one in the US Billboard Hot 100 and became the titular track of her second album. \nThe pair also worked together on other successful hits including Cyndi Lauper's True Colors (1986), Whitney Houston's So Emotional (1987) and I Touch Myself by the Divinyls (1990).\nSteinberg went on to co-write songs in the early 2000s, such as JoJo's Too Little Too Late and Demi Lovato's Give Your Heart a Break.\nThe Songwriters Hall of Fame, into which Steinberg was inducted in 2011, describes him as one of the \"most successful songwriters\", whose songs have turned into \"enduring classics\".\nSteinberg is survived by his wife Trina, two sons and two stepchildren.\n[United States](https://www.bbc.com/news/topics/cx1m7zg01xyt)\n[Music](https://www.bbc.com/news/topics/cz4pr2gd872t)\n* * *\nRelated\n## [Hillary Clinton accuses Trump administration of Epstein files 'cover-up' in BBC interview](https://www.bbc.com/news/articles/czx47k34yqxo)\n## [Trump says he will be 'indirectly' involved in Iran nuclear talks](https://www.bbc.com/news/articles/cwyknjnd706o)\n## [FBI won't co-operate on Alex Pretti investigation, state officials say](https://www.bbc.com/news/articles/crk8nxg1p4xo)\n* * *\nMore from the BBC\n[ 6 hrs ago ![Damon Albarn wearing a black leather jacket with his arm around Noel Gallagher at the Brit Awards 2013](https://ichef.bbci.co.uk/news/480/cpsprodpb/849a/live/d67d1c00-0808-11f1-b56b-bf8bb0174edb.jpg) Oasis v Blur rivalry being revived in new play as cast take sides The 1995 chart battle is recreated for a new play - {"url": "https://www.bbc.com/news/articles/cx2gn239exlo", "depth": 1, "published_time": null, "markdown": "[](https://www.bbc.com/)\n[](https://www.bbc.com/sport)\n[](https://www.bbc.com/health)\n[](https://www.bbc.com/weather)\n[](https://www.bbc.com/newsletters)\n# How dark web agent spotted bedroom wall clue to rescue girl from years of harm\n![BBC Greg Squire in grey v-neck t-shirt and glasses, next to a treated image of a brick wall and a girl's silhouette](https://ichef.bbci.co.uk/news/480/cpsprodpb/1c3c/live/6ddc6370-0828-11f1-9972-d3f265c101c6.png.webp)BBC\n_**Warning: This article contains details about sexual abuse**_\nSpecialist online investigator Greg Squire had hit a dead end in his efforts to rescue an abused girl his team had named Lucy.\nDisturbing images of her were being shared on the dark web - an encrypted corner of the internet only accessible using special software designed to make owners digitally untraceable.\nBut even with that level of subterfuge, the abuser was conscious of \"covering their tracks\", cropping or altering any identifying features, says Squire. It was impossible to work out who, or where, Lucy was.\nWhat he was soon to discover was that the clue to the 12-year-old's location was hidden in plain sight.\nSquire works for US Department of Homeland Security Investigations in an elite unit which attempts to identify children appearing in sexual abuse material.\nA BBC World Service team has spent five years filming with Squire, and other investigative units in Portugal, Brazil, and Russia - showing them solving cases such as that of a kidnapped and presumed-dead seven-year-old in Russia, and the arrest of a Brazilian man responsible for five of the biggest child-abuse forums on the dark web.\nThe unprecedented access shows how these cases are often cracked, not through state-of-the-art technology, but by spotting tiny revealing details in images or chat forums.\n![Greg Squire and colleagues including Pete Manning in their office. Greg is wearing a green t-shirt and light trousers and is walking across a room crowded with computer screens, holding a laptop. ](https://ichef.bbci.co.uk/news/480/cpsprodpb/a04f/live/91d55910-0820-11f1-b5e2-dd58fc65f0f6.jpg.webp)\nSquire and his team monitor dark web chatrooms around the clock to watch for any clues that could identify and locate abused children \nSquire cites Lucy's case, which he tackled early in his career, as the inspiration for his long-term dedication.\nHe found it especially disturbing that Lucy was about the same age as his own daughter, and new photos of her being assaulted, seemingly in her bedroom, were constantly appearing.\nSquire and his team could see, from the type of light sockets and electrical outlets visible in the images, that Lucy was in North America. But that was about it.\nThey contacted Facebook, which at the time dominated the social media landscape, asking for help scouring uploaded family photos - to see if Lucy was in any of them. But Facebook, despite having facial recognition technology, said it \"did not have the tools\" to help.\nSo Squire and his colleagues analysed everything they could see in Lucy's room: the bedspread, her outfits, her stuffed toys. Looking for any element which might help.\nAnd then they had a minor breakthrough. The team discovered that a sofa seen in some of the images was only sold regionally, not nationally, and therefore had a more limited customer base.\nBut that still amounted to about 40,000 people.\n\"At that point in the investigation, we're [still] looking at 29 states here in the US. I mean, you're talking about tens of thousands of addresses, and that's a very, very daunting task,\" says Squire.\n  * _If you are not in the UK,__or_[ _listen to the World of Secrets podcast here_](https://www.bbc.com/audio/brand/w13xtvs0)\n\n\nThe team looked for more clues. And that is when they realised something as mundane as the exposed brick wall in Lucy's bedroom could give them a lead.\n\"So, I started just Googling bricks and it wasn't too many searches [before] I found the Brick Industry Association,\" says Squire.\n\"And the woman on the phone was awesome. She was like, 'how can the brick industry help?'\"\nShe offered to share the photo with brick experts all over the country. The response was almost immediate, he says.\nOne of the people who got in touch was John Harp, who had been working in brick sales since 1981.\n\"I noticed that the brick was a very pink-cast brick, and it had a little bit of a charcoal overlay on it. It was a modular eight-inch brick and it was square-edged,\" he says. \"When I saw that, I knew exactly what the brick was,\" he adds. \nIt was, he told Squire, a \"Flaming Alamo\".\n\"[Our company] made that brick from the late 60s through about the middle part of the 80s, and I had sold millions of bricks from that plant.\"\n![John Harp has short grey hair and a grey moustache. He is wearing a black short-sleeved shirt and green trousers, and is sitting in his brick factory, open boxes open behind him. ](https://ichef.bbci.co.uk/news/480/cpsprodpb/8504/live/a1fc4930-081f-11f1-b5e2-dd58fc65f0f6.jpg.webp)\nJohn Harp was able to identify the type of brick in the wall shown behind Lucy \nInitially Squire was ecstatic, expecting they could access a digitised customer list. But Harp broke the news that the sales records were just a \"pile of notes\" that went back decades.\nHe did however reveal a key detail about bricks, Squire says.\n\"He goes: 'Bricks are heavy.' And he said: 'So heavy bricks don't go very far.'\"\nThis changed everything. The team returned to the sofa customer list and narrowed that down to just those clients who lived within a 100-mile radius of Harp's brick factory in the US' south-west.\nFrom that list of 40 or 50 people, it was easy to find and trawl their social media. And that is when they found a photo of Lucy on Facebook with an adult who looked as though she was close to the girl - possibly a relative.\nThey worked out the woman's address, and then used that to find out every other address connected with that person, and all the people they had ever lived with.\nThat narrowed Lucy's possible address down further - but they didn't want to go door to door, making enquiries. Get the address wrong, and they could risk the suspect being tipped off that he was on the authorities' radar.\nSo Squire and his colleagues began sending photos of these houses to John Harp, the brick expert.\n![Greg Squire with his dog on a walk near his home - he is looking down at the black, white and tan dog looking up at him. ](https://ichef.bbci.co.uk/news/480/cpsprodpb/3242/live/453d8a40-0821-11f1-9972-d3f265c101c6.jpg.webp)\nSquire at his home in New Hampshire - he found it very disturbing that Lucy was a similar age to his own daughter \nFlaming Alamos were not visible on the outside of any of the homes, because the properties were clad in other materials. But the team asked Harp to assess - by looking at their style and exterior - if these properties were likely to have been built during a period when Flaming Alamos had been on sale.\n\"We would basically take a screenshot of that house or residence and shoot it over to John and say 'would this house have these bricks inside?'\" says Squire.\nFinally they had a breakthrough. They found an address that Harp believed was likely to feature a Flaming Alamo brick wall, and was on the sofa customer-base list.\n\"So we narrowed it down to [this] one address\u2026 and started the process of confirming who was living there through state records, driver's licence\u2026 information on schools,\" says Squire.\nThe team realised that in the household with Lucy was her mother's boyfriend - a convicted sex offender.\nWithin hours, local Homeland Security agents had arrested the offender, who had been raping Lucy for six years. He was subsequently sentenced to more than 70 years in jail.\nBrick expert Harp was delighted to hear Lucy was safe, especially given his own experiences as a long-term foster parent.\n\"We've had over 150 different children in our home. We've adopted three. So, doing that over those years, we have a lot of children in our home that were [previously] abused,\" he said.\n\"What [Squire's team] do day in and day out, and what they see, is a magnification of hundreds of times of what I've seen or had to deal with.\"\n![Greg Squire on a walk in the woods - he is wearing a dark coat and grey beanie hat.](https://ichef.bbci.co.uk/news/480/cpsprodpb/f1c5/live/91af96b0-0822-11f1-9972-d3f265c101c6.jpg.webp)\nSquire has struggled with his mental health as a result of his work \nA few years ago, that pressure on Squire started to take a real toll on his mental health, and he admits that, when he wasn't working, \"alcohol was a bigger part of my life than it should have been\".\n\"At that point my kids were a bit older\u2026 and, you know, that almost enables you to push harder. Like\u2026 'I bet if I get up at three this morning, I can surprise [a perpetrator] online.'\n\"But meanwhile, personally\u2026 'Who's Greg? I don't even know what he likes to do.' All of your friends\u2026 during the day, you know, they're criminals\u2026 All they do is talk about the most horrific things all day long.\"\nNot long afterwards, his marriage broke down, and he says he began to have suicidal thoughts.\nIt was his colleague Pete Manning who encouraged him to seek help after noticing his friend seemed to be struggling.\n![Greg Squire and Pete Manning walking along a beach holding takeaway drinks. They are both wearing blue trousers and brown jackets.](https://ichef.bbci.co.uk/news/480/cpsprodpb/1670/live/abc1d300-0823-11f1-b5e2-dd58fc65f0f6.jpg.webp)\n\"I feel honoured to be part of the team that can make a difference,\" says Squire, pictured with friend and colleague Pete Manning\n\"It's hard when the thing that brings you so much energy and drive is also the thing that's slowly destroying you,\" Manning says.\nSquire says exposing his vulnerabilities to the light was the first step to getting better and continuing to do a job he is proud of.\n\"I feel honoured to be part of the team that can make a difference instead of watching it on TV or hearing about it\u2026 I'd rather be right in there in the fight trying to stop it.\"\nLast summer Greg met Lucy, now in her 20s, for the first time.\n![Squire and Lucy on a park bench talking. Squire is wearing a dark t-shirt and Lucy is wearing a cap, and blue racer-back top. ](https://ichef.bbci.co.uk/news/480/cpsprodpb/94f1/live/11381910-0824-11f1-9972-d3f265c101c6.jpg.webp)\nLucy (left), now an adult, told Squire she had been praying help would come\nShe told him her ability to now discuss what she went through was testament to the support she has around her.\n\"I have more stability. I'm able to have the energy to talk to people [about the abuse], which I could not have done\u2026 even, like, a couple years ago.\"\nShe said at the point Homeland Security ended her abuse she had been \"praying actively for it to end\".\n\"Not to sound clich\u00e9, but it was a prayer answered.\"\nSquire told her he wished he had been able to communicate that help was on its way.\n\"You wish there was some telepathy and you could reach out and be like, 'listen, we're coming'.\"\nThe BBC asked Facebook why it couldn't use its facial recognition technology to assist the hunt for Lucy. It responded: \"To protect user privacy, it's important that we follow the appropriate legal process, but we work to support law enforcement as much as we can.\"\n_**If you've been a victim of child sexual abuse, a victim of crime or have feelings of despair, and are in the UK, you'll find details of help and support at**_ _._\n[Sexual violence](https://www.bbc.com/news/topics/c1038wnxe18t)\n[Child abuse](https://www.bbc.com/news/topics/c46zkd8y97et)\n[US Department of Homeland Security](https://www.bbc.com/news/topics/cv66w0r81w1t)\n[Dark web](https://www.bbc.com/news/topics/cyd7k069084t)\n* * *\nRelated\n## [Ex-church minister who admitted child sexual abuse to BBC still free years later](https://www.bbc.com/news/articles/cx2k23mlkzlo)\n## [Queen's letter of support left Pelicot 'overwhelmed' ](https://www.bbc.com/news/articles/ckgl9vljk13o)\n## [Watch: 'I am a survivor' Gis\u00e8le Pelicot tells BBC Newsnight ](https://www.bbc.com/news/videos/c80jvkem8lro)\n* * *\nMore from the BBC\n[ 3 days ago ![Elliot Jones is looking at the camera in a police custody photo.
